Evaluation Review Revision Record

This 11 October 2026 review copy derives from the 3 October coverage-recovery
manuscript and incorporates the 4 October draft’s five numerical and provenance cor-
rections. Appendix J distinguishes artifact comparisons, arithmetic from printed counts
and context-only occurrences. No new training or benchmark evaluation was performed,
and no frozen source, reference label, cohort selection or primary score was changed.
Historical releases remain separate and unchanged.
This public derivative omits operational and account identifiers, access correspondence,
financial details, signature images and student registration data. Private provider ex-
ports and recovery archives are not bundled. It is not a certified institutional submission
and does not carry the historical certificate. Six recovered original files resolved five
primary-file gaps; the scientific recovery conclusions and E14 denominator correction
remain, with inclusion and withholding boundaries in Appendix I and the public avail-
ability manifest.
The C1 extension (Sections 1.5, 4.6, 7.5, 9.3 and B.13; Appendix G) retains the frozen
11/64 endpoint (17.19%; Wilson 95% interval 9.88–28.21%) beside three conservatively
corroborated numerical witnesses on the same denominator. Reference errors, ambigui-
ties and unit mismatches limit interpretation; neither count is a training gain. The ledger
retains classifications, hashes and disagreements, and historical V2 remains separate.
Appendix H maps the evidence and distinguishes bundled content from retained exter-
nal sources. Appendix A corrects registry scope and P11 evidence-tier wording without
changing experimental values.
A 16 October 2026 correction follows a publication-readiness audit. The unsaturated
same-stack GRPO-versus-PPO gap (Section 6.5.5) is superseded by a rerun with a 512-
token cap and a warm-started critic that finds no detectable GRPO advantage; its figures
are provisional while three PPO seeds and an exposure-matched arm complete. The ZVF
U-shape and escalation asymmetry are restated as largely following from the binomial
identity and reward level, with contemporaneous prior work cited. The stack exhibits
are decomposed or bounded, the registry count and stackdiff limitations are stated,
five citations are corrected, and the AI-tool declaration is expanded. Appendix E lists

Declaration

I declare that this project report is my own work. Where the work of others has been
used, it is cited and acknowledged. The matter embodied in this report has not been
submitted, in part or in full, to this or any other university or institution for the award of
any other degree or diploma.
The multi-framework benchmark infrastructure, the baseline framework integrations,
and the initial GRPO/PPO/DPO experimentation were produced by the Semester-3
project group, of which I was a member, and are inherited here as the shared foundation.
The research direction, the experimental expansion, the statistical analyses, the P1–P8
internal reports, the reporting and registry artefacts, the adaptive group-size controller,
the applied fraud study, and the E1–E14 evaluation campaign reported in this document
are my individual contribution, carried out under the guidance of my project guide.
Quantitative results are tied to named evidence. Appendix B distinguishes recovered
primary campaign files, inspectable artefacts and the remaining limits on underlying
payload verification. Incomplete, blocked and negative results are reported rather than
omitted. The boundary between inherited and individual work is stated precisely in
platform_hybrid/sem 4 work/PROVENANCE.md.

                                             iii
Declaration on the Use of AI Tools

AI tools were used extensively in this work, including autonomous coding agents that
edited the repository, ran analyses and launched cloud jobs. The uses recorded in the
project repository are listed below.

    • Agentic coding and writing. Anthropic Claude models through Claude Code (in-
      cluding multi-agent teams and background subagents) and OpenAI Codex wrote
      and revised a large share of the code, analysis scripts and documents, including
      drafts and revisions of this report and the defence materials. These agents often
      ran autonomously with permission prompts disabled. Commits they authored or
      co-authored carry a Claude, Codex or other agent attribution in the git history; a
      small number of commits are attributed to Factory Droid, claude-flow and Mini-
      Max agents.

    • Parallel agent sessions. Several agent sessions worked on the repository at the
      same time under a recorded ownership log: xAI Grok CLI (defence preparation
      and statistical audit), Muse CLI (code consolidation and test suites), Claude Code
      (audits and experiment replication) and Google Antigravity with Gemini mod-
      els (coordination and test maintenance), coordinated with the squad multi-agent
      tool. Gemini 3.1 Pro also reviewed build and verification code through the agy
      command-line tool.

    • Experiment execution. Agents prepared and launched training and evaluation
      jobs on Modal, Tinker and Colab, including the replication run reported in Sec-
      tion 6.5.5, and pulled their results. The resulting measurements come from those
      executed runs.

    • Literature work. Agent pipelines (hyperresearch and similar) searched, fetched
      and summarised literature, and checked references against arXiv and Crossref.
      Citations in this report were checked against the cited sources, but not every ref-
      erence was read in full by the author.

    • Frontier-model synthesis. ChatGPT Pro Extended and Gemini Deep Think pro-
      duced proposed derivations and reasoning recorded in platform_hybrid/paper

                                           iv
      /sections/frontier_synthesis_*.tex, which those documents mark as not
      being experiments. Where this report uses such a result, it states its own derivation
      (Chapter 6 §6.3.3).

    • Independent verification and review. An LLM verification pass (Kimi, 6 July
      2026) flagged two single-seed results as SUSPECT; both were re-tested and reported
      as nulls (Chapter 6 §6.4.3). The pre-defence self-review of 27 September 2026,
      the October evaluation review and the 16 October 2026 publication-readiness au-
      dit were carried out by AI agents.

    • Evaluation triage. The Jev (TypeSafe) judgment harness was used in the E1–E14
      ledger only for classification, faithfulness and value ordering, never for arithmetic
      (Chapter 4 §4.5).

No AI tool fabricated experimental data: every reported measurement comes from an
executed run or evaluation recorded in the repository, and simulated values are labelled
as simulations. AI assistance does not transfer responsibility. The research direction,
the choice of claims, their final wording and any remaining errors are the author’s re-
sponsibility.

                                          vi
Abstract

This report studies group-relative reinforcement-learning post-training of large language
models through an explicit record of the training and evaluation stack. Controlled com-
parisons seek to isolate the factor under test; remaining differences and missing evidence
limit attribution. It makes four contributions.
(1) A recomputable signal-starvation diagnostic and a loss-form audit. The Zero-
Variance Fraction (ZVF) is logged per step and recomputed from stored reward ten-
sors, with a closed-form Bernoulli null. Reading the runners’ code distinguishes cus-
tom REINFORCE group-mean loops from clipped surrogates, nonzero-KL configura-
tion records and unknown managed settings.
(2) Stack-conditioned reporting. A minimum reporting standard and a machine-
readable stack registry with a stackdiff tool that flags label-flip risk between two runs
from their records alone.
(3) Calibrated negative results. No reliable scaling law is recovered from reward
curves across twelve fitted anchors. A same-stack PPO/GRPO contrast (0.992 against
0.990, 𝑝 = 0.374, five seeds) and a group-size sweep find no detectable difference on sat-
urated, in-distribution arithmetic draws; neither establishes equivalence or an optimum.
On an unsaturated GSM8K rerun, an initial 5.0-point GRPO advantage over an untuned-
critic PPO under a binding 200-token cap did not survive a corrected rerun (512-token
cap, warm-started critic, 1,319 test items): GRPO minus PPO is +0.8 points (95% CI
[−0.3, +1.9]), equivalent within ±2 points (provisional; seven PPO seeds), and 𝐺 = 8
versus 𝐺 = 2 is not significant after multiplicity adjustment. ZVF, studied concurrently
as advantage collapse, is recomputable, but its predictive validity is unestablished: the
historical pooled association mixes simulation and measured records with inconsistent
outcome definitions. An adaptive group-size controller derived from ZVF reports zero
rescue under a plug-in counterfactual model; 92% of its escalations target observed all-
correct groups, a share the reward level alone predicts, without proving latent certainty
or impossibility of recovery with fresh rollouts.
(4) An evaluation-governance protocol. One lightly trained actor (Qwen3.6-35B-A3B
with a LoRA adapter, 40 training steps) is evaluated on fourteen suites absent from its


                                           vii
recorded training-suite list under native graders at pinned revisions. Content-level de-
contamination remains unverified. Receipts, a no-pooling rule and a per-lane serving au-
dit expose a serving defect in a zero-scoring run; the defect’s contribution to that score is
unquantified. Complete scopes include VerilogEval (129/312 = 41.35%), LAB-Bench
(0.2288) and AgentDojo benign utility (88/97); six replacement scopes completed on
27 September 2026 (Chapter 8, Table 8.C); seven original contracts remain externally
blocked. A paired trained-versus-base rerun on 5–151 identical items per lane finds no
significant difference in any lane (inconclusive at those sizes). No claim of campaign-
level improvement over the base model is made.
C1 diagnostic extension. Benchmark reference errors, ambiguous wording and equiv-
alent units limit the interpretation of a new frozen-policy study on public GSM8K train.
The first 64 of 87 eligible questions from 4,096 screened questions received 24 fresh re-
sponses after eight valid, uncapped parser-zero responses. The frozen endpoint is 11/64
(17.19%; Wilson 95% interval 9.88–28.21%). Assistant-assisted reconciliation corrobo-
rates three numerical witnesses on the same denominator, while preserving convention-
dependent and ambiguous cases and one wrong-reference match. This is conditional
diagnostic evidence, not a population estimate, reasoning validation or training gain.
A separate, undeployed parser candidate preserves 2,025 previously valid parse objects
and admits four unambiguous extractions in a predefined future subset of 2,048 outputs.
It does not alter C1’s frozen parser-v2 score.


Keywords: GRPO, RLVR, zero-variance fraction, signal starvation, stack-conditioned
results, benchmark evaluation, reproducibility, evaluation governance, attribution.

                                       xxi
  Part I

Foundations




     1
Chapter 1

Introduction

1.1     Background: critic-free RL post-training and the
        group-relative estimator

Post-training is now the stage at which a base language model is turned into something
that reliably follows instructions and solves problems. For several years the dominant
recipe was reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), in which a learned
reward model is trained on human preference comparisons and then used as the reward
signal for a policy-optimisation loop. RLHF works, but it introduces a second learned
model whose errors the policy can exploit, and it makes reward construction expensive
and hard to audit. The alternative that has taken hold more recently is reinforcement
learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR): instead of a learned reward model, the re-
ward comes from a programmatic checker — exact-match against a reference answer
for grade-school mathematics, unit tests for code, a formal or semi-formal validator for
structured output. The checker is deterministic, transparent, and free to run. What it
gives up in generality it gains in measurability: every reward is a fact about a generated
string rather than a prediction from a model (Figure 1.1).
The workhorse optimiser for both settings has been proximal policy optimisation (PPO),
which optimises a clipped surrogate objective against a value function critic. The critic
is a second network, usually of comparable size to the policy, trained to predict the
expected return of the current state; its predictions supply the baseline that the advantage
estimator subtracts. This works, but it is expensive and fragile. The critic consumes
memory that could hold policy parameters, it has its own learning rate and its own failure
modes, and its errors propagate directly into the policy gradient as biased advantages.
Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) removes the critic entirely. Rather than
learn a value function, GRPO samples a group of 𝐺 completions for the same prompt,


                                             2

Figure 1.1: The GRPO training loop. A prompt is sampled 𝐺 times, each completion is
scored by a reward function, advantages are formed relative to the group, and the policy
is updated. When all 𝐺 rewards coincide the group-relative advantage collapses to zero
and no reward-relative gradient flows, although the rollouts are still paid for. Number
line: the Phase-1 P2 batch ZVF of 0.72–0.77 (four methods, 40 steps, 16 prompts ×
8 samples, GSM8K, seed 0, Qwen3.5-4B) and the group-size sweep on Qwen2.5-0.5B
two-digit addition over three seeds, where mean ZVF falls from 0.838 at 𝐺 = 2 to 0.631
at 𝐺 = 16 (a fall of 0.207).




                                                                       3
scores all of them with the verifier, and derives the advantage of each completion from
the group’s own reward statistics. Concretely, for a group with rewards 𝑟 1 , . . . , 𝑟 𝐺 , the
advantage assigned to completion 𝑖 is a within-group standardisation,

                    𝑟𝑖 − 𝜇𝑔              1 Õ                    1 Õ
             𝐴𝑖 =           ,     𝜇𝑔 =       𝑟𝑗,        𝜎𝑔2 =       (𝑟 𝑗 − 𝜇𝑔 ) 2 ,
                    𝜎𝑔 + 𝜀               𝐺 𝑗                    𝐺 𝑗

which in the original GRPO formulation is then used in a clipped, KL-regularised policy-
gradient update. Several inspected custom runners omit KL regularisation; the custom-
loss Tinker reference loop is REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline and has no ratio
or clipping. This does not describe every run: TRL configuration records include a
reference policy and nonzero KL, while managed or unrecoverable settings remain un-
known. Table 4.2 gives row-wise loss evidence; the campaign actor’s original execution
source is not recoverable (Appendix D). The empirical baseline is the group mean; there
is no value network to train, no value loss to weight, and no critic to keep in sync with the
policy. The trade is a much simpler optimiser for a much smaller effective batch, since
𝐺 samples of the same prompt now carry one learning signal rather than 𝐺 independent
ones.
That trade has a sharp consequence, and it is the observation this project is organised
around. Because the advantage is a within-group standardisation, its numerator is a de-
viation from the group mean and its denominator is the group’s own spread. When every
completion in a group receives the same verifier outcome — all correct, or all incorrect
— the group contributes nothing: 𝜎𝑔 = 0, every numerator is zero, and every advan-
tage 𝐴𝑖 is exactly zero. The group has been fully sampled and fully scored, at full token
cost, and the reward-relative part of the update is identically zero. In an objective with
a KL penalty, that penalty would still pull the policy toward the reference; for verified
zero-KL group-relative policy surrogates there is no such contribution, although auxil-
iary/value losses and optimiser state can still update parameters. Exact ties imply zero
reward-relative advantage; the tolerance-based diagnostic in §4.3 also counts near-ties
and does not assert exactly zero gradient for them. We call the fraction of a training
batch that falls into this state the Zero-Variance Fraction (ZVF), and it is the single
measurement that most of this report’s diagnostics are built around.
ZVF is not a rare edge case, but its size depends on the regime. The Phase-1 main config-
uration (Qwen3.5-4B, GSM8K, 40 steps, 𝐺 = 8) records a ZVF of approximately 0.72–
0.77, meaning that roughly three quarters of that rollout batch contributes no reward-
relative gradient. Selected measured cohort means span 0.16 (Qwen3-8B on held-out
GSM8K at 𝐺 = 8) to 0.84 (Qwen2.5-0.5B arithmetic at 𝐺 = 2), so no single value is
typical; the measured values are collected in the canonical-numbers table (Table 1.1).
The fraction responds to the one knob that most directly controls it: on the Qwen2.5-

                                              4
0.5B arithmetic sweep, mean ZVF falls from 0.838 at 𝐺 = 2 to 0.631 at 𝐺 = 16, a
decline of 0.207 across that range. Larger groups buy more contrast, with diminishing
returns that saturate well before the group is small enough to be cheap.
There is a second and less comfortable observation in the background, and it is about
attribution rather than mechanism. “GRPO” is not a single algorithm. It is a label that
covers implementations in Tinker, TRL, SkyRL, verl, OpenRLHF and a growing num-
ber of research forks, each of which chooses its own answer parser, sampling temper-
ature, top-𝑝, KL coefficient, clip range, LoRA target set, precision and evaluation har-
ness. Two runs that both say “GRPO” in their titles may differ in a dozen ways that
plausibly move the outcome, and the reported headline number does not distinguish be-
tween them. The project’s own stack-sensitivity probe makes this concrete: a nominally
matched configuration (Qwen3-8B, seed 42, 𝐺 = 8, learning rate 1 × 10−5 , GSM8K-
500, 30 steps, visible hyperparameters held equal, although the managed Tinker run
used the Qwen3-8B-Base checkpoint and TRL the instruction-tuned Qwen3-8B) was
run across four framework paths, two of which completed and two of which are dry-
run placeholders, with results recorded in experiments/results/framework_com
parison.json. Four separate marginal one-way ANOVAs over 42 experiments give
𝜂2 = 0.546 for library or framework, 𝜂2 = 0.558 for training algorithm, 𝜂2 = 0.471
for model family and 𝜂2 = 0.322 for model scale — with the important caveat, stated
by the source itself, that these factors are confounded with one another and with back-
end, reward implementation, hardware and task. Because each value is a marginal per-
factor 𝜂2 on a collinear corpus, the four sum to 1.897 and are not a variance decompo-
sition; none estimates a factor’s independent contribution (Chapter 6 §6.1; source: plat-
form_hybrid/paper/sections/_shared_methods.tex). The framework factor is nonethe-
less not a nuisance parameter to be wished away: marginally, outcomes vary with it
about as strongly as with the algorithm label.



1.2     A Motivating Example

Consider a single training step with a group size of 𝐺 = 8 on an easy GSM8K prompt
that the current policy already solves. All eight sampled completions produce the correct
final answer, so each receives reward 𝑟𝑖 = 1. The group mean is 𝜇𝑔 = 1 and the group
standard deviation is 𝜎𝑔 = 0, so every advantage 𝐴𝑖 = 0. The compute spent on that
group is real — eight full forward-decode passes at whatever temperature the sampler
is configured with — and the group supplies no task-learning signal. It contributes zero
reward-relative surrogate gradient in a verified group-centred implementation; KL and
auxiliary losses, and optimiser state, remain separate. Now imagine a batch of 16 such
prompts in which 12 are already solved and 4 are not: 12/16 = 0.75 of the batch is

                                           5
“dark” compute. What the Semester 4 work adds to this picture is that 0.75 is not a
worst case: it is close to the 0.72–0.77 measured on the Phase-1 main configuration. It
is not a universal value either, since selected measured cohort means range from 0.16 to
0.84 depending on model, task and 𝐺 (Table 1.1), so any claim that GRPO is learning
efficiently has to be read against the ZVF of its own regime (Figure 1.2).

Figure 1.2: The confounding problem this work addresses. A single reported reward
figure sits on top of an unstated stack; without fixing and reporting that stack the num-
ber is not attributable to the method under test. Top right: of the four frameworks in the
nominal configuration only Tinker and TRL were run; veRL and OpenRLHF were not
installed in the sandbox and have no measurement. The 17× gap between the two mea-
sured stacks is an under-specification exhibit, not a clean back-end-only effect, because
the managed stack also pinned a different base checkpoint (Qwen3-8B-Base vs. Qwen3-
8B). Bottom left: one-way 𝜂2 shares over 42 reported experiments; they are confounded
with one another and with back-end, reward implementation, hardware and task, so they
do not sum to one. Bottom right: the PPO-versus-GRPO rows of the Phase-1 table, one
run per arm, shown without inferential statistics; the Qwen arms did not share a back-end
(GRPO on the managed API, PPO on a Modal H100 cluster), so no directional Qwen
algorithm claim is made.

Now suppose, having noticed this, that one wants to answer the obvious follow-up ques-
tion: is the group-relative estimator actually a better choice than a learned critic? The
natural experiment is a matched comparison — same model, same training prompts,

                                                                          6
same verifier, same step budget, same evaluation, with PPO and GRPO as the only dif-
ference. The project’s first attempt at that contrast is recorded in a historical internal
report under the label “Qwen3-8B”, with a Welch 𝑡-test of 𝑝 = 0.7605 computed over
the final ten steps of a single run per arm. Those ten steps are autocorrelated observations
of one run, not ten independent runs, and the project’s later methods note re-examined
the row and marks it “provenance conflict; not estimable”. No effect size, confidence
interval or power figure is therefore quoted for it here, and the Welch value is carried
only as a provenance record (Chapter 6 §6.2.3). The project’s own rule is that single-
seed Tinker experiments have “zero statistical power” and are “treated as descriptive
only and are not subject to significance testing”.
Two further observations from the same historical source make the example sharper
rather than cleaner. First, the same table reports the PPO-versus-GRPO contrast on
Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct in the opposite direction; it is also one run per arm, so again no
inferential statistic is quoted, but the direction alone shows that the apparent answer is not
stable across model families. Second, the Qwen arms were not executed on a common
back-end — the GRPO arm ran through Tinker-managed infrastructure and the PPO
arm on a Modal H100 cluster — and the source states this explicitly and declines to
draw the algorithmic conclusion it invites: “We therefore treat the PPO/GRPO rows as
stack-conditioned evidence, not as an algorithm diagnostic baseline”, and “We therefore
withhold a directional Qwen algorithm claim”. The same section records an aggregation
gap that illustrates why such care is warranted: the Qwen PPO last-10 GSM8K figure
is 22.5% in the primary results ledger but 35.0% in the statistical summary, and the
source names the ledger value as primary and treats the summary value as evidence of
an aggregation defect rather than as a second measurement.
The genuinely same-stack evidence in the repository is also a null, and its scope has to
be stated with it. The same-stack PPO/GRPO run (Qwen2.5-0.5B, 40 steps, five seeds,
paired by seed but not by evaluation item) is a two-digit addition task on which both
arms are at ceiling: evaluation accuracy is 0.990 for GRPO and 0.992 for PPO, the
paired difference is −0.002 with p = 0.374, and the 200 evaluation prompts are fresh
random draws from the same generator used in training rather than a disjoint split;
the two arms of a seed share only 3–7 of their 200 evaluation items, and PPO had
drawn about 40% of its evaluation items during training against about 7% for GRPO
(§6.2.3; sources: platform_hybrid/experiments/results/samestack_ppo_grpo.json; plat-
form_hybrid/experiments/modal/modal_samestack_ppo_grpo.py). At that ceiling a null
cannot separate “no effect” from “no headroom”, so the result is that no difference was
detectable on a saturated task, not that the estimators are equivalent; a paired TOST
at a ±0.005 margin gives p = 0.10 (Table 4.2 gives the exact loss form of both arms).
Rerun on the unsaturated Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT configuration with a disjoint test


                                              7
split, a first version (v1) showed a +5.0-point gap for GRPO at G = 8 over PPO, but
under a binding 200-token cap and with a cold, untuned critic whose PPO arm fin-
ished below its starting accuracy (0.195 against 0.200). A corrected v2 rerun with
a 512-token cap, a warm-started critic and the full 1,319-item test split finds no de-
tectable GRPO advantage: the difference is +0.8 points, equivalent within ±2 points
(§6.5.5, Table 6.2; provisional while three PPO seeds and an exposure-matched PPO
arm complete). The variant audit (not to be confused with campaign lane E1) takes
four GRPO-family variants (DAPO, GSPO, Dr. GRPO, AERO) plus the GRPO refer-
ence, runs five arms across eight paired seeds for 40 units at a frozen stack fingerprint,
and evaluates on 500 held-out GSM8K examples per unit. Every one of the four con-
trasts is inconclusive. The figures below are paired-𝑡 95% intervals and paired-𝑡 𝑝-
values over the eight seeds, recomputed from zvf-program/audit/results/full
/*.json: DAPO +0.00100, CI [−0.00632, +0.00832], 𝑝 = 0.756; GSPO +0.00500,
CI [−0.00357, +0.01357], 𝑝 = 0.210; Dr. GRPO −0.00200, CI [−0.01272, +0.00872],
𝑝 = 0.673; AERO −0.00075, CI [−0.01029, +0.00879], 𝑝 = 0.858. These paired-𝑡
intervals replace the audit’s own intervals (corrected; see Table E.1). After Benjamini–
Hochberg correction every adjusted 𝑝 is at least 0.84. The pre-audit aggregator’s verdict
that the differences “disappear” is itself declared invalid, because the locked exact non-
central paired-𝑡 minimum detectable effect of 0.010115893 exceeds the 0.010 margin
the study was designed around. The audit’s permitted wording is deliberately narrow:
across eight paired seeds no arm produced a BH-significant held-out difference from
GRPO, and none met the locked exact-power equivalence rule — which is not the same
as saying the methods are the same, nor that they fail to improve. That audit runs on
Qwen3-8B GSM8K with 500 evaluation examples per unit (Appendix A, P11).
Taken together, these two halves of the example motivate what follows. The mechanism
half shows that sampling can produce groups with no reward-relative advantage signal
even while reward rises. The attribution half shows that the comparative question —
is this estimator better than the alternative? — cannot be answered from the labels
“GRPO” and “PPO” alone. The historical comparisons are confounded or at ceiling; the
later unsaturated comparison’s apparent gap against an untuned critic disappeared once
the token cap and critic were fixed, so it supplies no ranking either. These observations
motivate better measurement and fuller reporting, not a claim that the GRPO estimator
is defective.



1.3     Problem Statement

The project is organised around four problems, in decreasing order of how often the
literature acknowledges them.

                                            8
Attribution requires more than an algorithm label. RL post-training frameworks can
differ in parser, sampler, KL coefficient, clip range, LoRA targets, precision and evalua-
tor. When those components vary together, a difference between runs labelled “GRPO”
and “PPO” cannot be assigned to the estimator alone. The project’s own marginal per-
factor 𝜂2 values put the framework factor at a magnitude comparable to the algorithm
factor, although they are not a partition of the variance. Its same-stack comparison on
saturated 0.5B addition returns a paired difference of −0.002 with 𝑝 = 0.374. These
local examples motivate an explicit stack record; they do not establish how often the
wider literature makes invalid comparisons.
Reward alone does not expose signal starvation. Mean reward, loss, gradient norm,
entropy and completion length do not directly report the fraction of groups whose rela-
tive advantage is zero. Rising reward can coexist with a high or low ZVF. The project
therefore persists group rewards and reports this fraction explicitly. Prior methods al-
ready recognise and intervene on zero-variance groups, including DAPO and GRESO
(§2.3); the contribution here is the recomputable measurement and its bounded inter-
pretation, not discovery of the mechanism.
GRPO-specific provenance is incompletely specified. General reporting and repro-
ducibility frameworks already exist (§2.4). The project’s targeted literature search
through July 2026 did not identify an equivalent GRPO-specific machine-readable run
datasheet combining executed loss form, per-component implementation status and an
explicit unauditability marker. This is a bounded search finding, not proof of absence.
The practical problem addressed is how to record these distinctions consistently and
validate the resulting records before using them in a comparison.
Evaluation results are rarely reported with their terminal states. Benchmark suites
in the agentic and reasoning evaluation space are frequently multi-scope (a public split
versus a private original contract, a benign-utility scope versus an adversarial scope),
quota-limited, or gated behind private payloads. Reports tend either to omit such suites
or to collapse them into a single number, which conceals how much of the suite was ac-
tually graded. The project treats this as a first-class concern rather than a presentational
one: a result that is genuinely partial should be recorded as partial, with its coverage
denominator and its reopen condition, and a lane that cannot be run at all should be
recorded as externally blocked rather than quietly dropped.



1.4     Objectives

The project pursued eight objectives. The aims are distinguished from their current
attainment in the table below.


                                             9
O1 — Build a single harness that can dispatch the same experiment to multiple
back-ends. Provide one control surface over Tinker, TRL, SkyRL, verl and OpenRLHF
paths so that a configuration can be held fixed while the framework varies. The harness
is the deliverable; it is not a completed multi-framework benchmark. Only Tinker and
TRL produced completed runs, and those two used different base checkpoints (Qwen3-
8B-Base and Qwen3-8B Instruct), so no framework comparison is claimed (Chapter 5
§5.2).
O2 — Instrument the training loop so that signal starvation is directly observable.
Emit per-step telemetry carrying ZVF, gradient utilisation, policy entropy, completion
length and KL divergence, and persist the raw per-group reward tensors so that any
derived statistic can be deterministically recomputed after the fact rather than trusted
from a log line.
O3 — Measure the zero-variance regime on real reward tensors and test the levers
that might move it. Characterise ZVF empirically on genuine GRPO reward tensors,
and investigate re-baselining and curriculum interventions as ways of converting dark
compute into contrast.
O4 — Establish and hold a scientific protocol. Multi-seed runs, matched baselines,
held-out evaluation with explicit denominators, deterministic recomputation, declared
(not pre-registered) effect-size and power accounting, and reporting of negatives and
nulls — including the cases where the answer is that the study was underpowered to
answer.
O5 — Identify a bounded contribution relative to prior work. Use a literature scan
to position a GRPO-specific reporting proposal against existing documentation and re-
producibility frameworks. This produced the minimum reporting standard (P5) and
machine-readable stack registry (P6); the originality claim is limited to the documented
search and distinctions in §2.4 and Table 2.1.
O6 — Turn the diagnostic into an intervention. Derive an adaptive group-size con-
troller from the ZVF measurement and evaluate it as a training-time policy rather than
a post-hoc observation (P7).
O7 — Build and exercise an evaluation-governance protocol on one trained ac-
tor. Evaluate a single trained checkpoint across fourteen lanes with named native or
replacement scopes, source-specific evaluator revision records, receipts, budgets and
explicit terminal states. The suites are absent from the actor’s recorded training-suite
list; content-level decontamination remains unverified, and revision bindings have the
limits reported in §8.2. The contribution is the protocol and the receipt-bound record
it produces. The campaign is not evidence about GRPO: the actor’s training run was
short (40 steps, Chapter 8 §8.1.1), and the only trained-versus-base comparison is the


                                          10
small-sample Table 8.B.
O8 — Report under a deliberate asymmetry. Where the evidence supports a struc-
tural or descriptive claim, state it; where it does not, withhold it. No cross-suite aggre-
gate, no pooling of original-contract and replacement-scope numbers, and no claim of
improvement over the base model in the E1–E14 campaign. Its paired trained-versus-
base comparison (Chapter 8, Table 8.B) is inconclusive in every lane. The controlled
studies in Chapter 6 separately report pre-to-post gains and the M1b GRPO–PPO con-
trast, limited to the tested stack and an untuned critic; five seeds give an exact sign-flip
p-value of 0.0625 despite the paired t-test p-value of 0.016.
The status below separates delivered software and analyses from experimental aims that
remain open. A working component does not by itself establish its scientific benefit.

The objectives are what the project set out to build. The results chapters answer four
research questions that the objectives make testable. Each is mapped to the table that
answers it, and §9.3 gives a one-line answer to each.

    • RQ1. Is the Zero-Variance Fraction recomputable from stored reward tensors, and
      does it carry information about training outcome? Answered by the ZVF rows of
      Table 1.1 and §6.3.
    • RQ2. With the training stack held fixed, do algorithm labels (PPO vs GRPO,
      GRPO vs Dr. GRPO) or group size separate on evaluation accuracy? Addressed
      by the same-stack rows of Table 1.1, the loss-form table in §4.3, and §6.2.3, §6.4
      and §6.5. The experiments reported here can bound this question but not answer
      it: the PPO/GRPO, group-size and arithmetic GRPO/Dr. GRPO contrasts were all
      run on a saturated task at evaluation accuracy near 0.99, and the two unsaturated
      GRPO/Dr. GRPO comparisons (§1.2, §6.5.4) are inconclusive and establish no

                                          12
      equivalence (§9.3). A same-stack rerun on an unsaturated task (§6.5.5) first sug-
      gested a GRPO advantage over a minimal untuned-critic PPO; the corrected v2
      rerun (512-token cap, warm-started critic) finds no detectable advantage, equiv-
      alent within ±0.02, while the G = 2 versus G = 8 contrast is not significant after
      multiplicity adjustment.
    • RQ3. Can a machine-readable stack manifest flag label-flip risk between two runs
      from their records alone? Addressed by §7.2 (registry health and stackdiff),
      including the revised 16 October version of the tool.
    • RQ4. What does a governance-first evaluation of one trained actor yield, and what
      can it not establish? Answered by Table C.3 and Tables 8.A and 8.B.

Contributions. Measured against these objectives, the thesis makes four contributions.
Everything else in it is supporting evidence for one of them.

   1. A recomputable signal-starvation diagnostic and a loss-form audit. ZVF is
      logged per step and recomputed from stored reward tensors against a closed-form
      Bernoulli null (§4.3, §6.3). Reading the runner code distinguishes custom RE-
      INFORCE group-mean loops from clipped surrogates, nonzero-KL configuration
      records and unknown managed settings (Table 4.2).
   2. Stack-conditioned reporting. A minimum reporting standard (P5) and a
      machine-readable stack registry with stackdiff, which flags label-flip risk be-
      tween two runs from their records alone (Chapter 7).
   3. Calibrated negative results. No reliable scaling law is recovered from reward
      curves; the same-stack PPO/GRPO and group-size contrasts are nulls on a sat-
      urated task, and on an unsaturated rerun the apparent PPO/GRPO gap from an
      untuned critic under a binding token cap did not survive a corrected rerun; ZVF
      predictive validity remains unestablished because the historical correlation mixes
      synthetic cells and heterogeneous outcomes; and the ZVF-derived controller’s
      zero-rescue counterfactual is imposed by a plug-in model fitted to observed all-
      equal groups, not a test of fresh-rollout recoverability. Each is reported with the
      power limitation that bounds it (Chapters 6–7).
   4. An evaluation-governance protocol. Native graders at pinned revisions, per-
      lane receipts, a no-pooling rule, a per-lane serving audit and a paired same-engine
      trained-versus-base design, exercised on one lightly trained actor across fourteen
      suites absent from its recorded training-suite list (Chapter 8). Content-level de-
      contamination remains unverified. The contribution is the protocol and the record
      it produces, not the actor’s scores.




                                           13
1.5     Scope and Non-Goals

The C1 extension dated 3 October 2026 adds a frozen-policy diagnostic whose chief
limitation is the reference itself. Exact-match zeros can reflect an incorrect key, am-
biguous wording or equivalent units as well as a wrong response. Among the first 64
eligible questions from a fixed 4,096-question screen, the frozen score records 11 fresh-
response events; assistant-assisted review corroborates three numerical witnesses on the
same denominator. This extension separates scoring from semantic support and does
not imply reasoning improvement, useful RL gradients or training gain. Section 7.5 and
Appendix G retain the full accounting, including disagreements. Historical V2 remains
separate.
The scope of this report is measurement and diagnostics, not leaderboard performance.
The controlled studies and the campaign actor use small-to-mid open models — the
Qwen2.5, Qwen3, Qwen3.5 and Qwen3.6 families, from roughly 0.5B parameters up to
the 35B sparse mixture-of-experts configuration used for the campaign actor. The P1
cross-scale roster is wider, 0.6B to 1T total parameters, but its frontier-scale anchors
are single-seed runs through a managed API and are read descriptively (Chapter 6 §6.1;
source: platform_hybrid/paper/sections/p1_abstract.tex). Verifiable rewards come from
mathematics and code checkers, principally GSM8K exact-match and HumanEval-style
unit tests. Held-out evaluation uses suites’ own native evaluators at pinned revisions.
Several things are explicitly out of scope. This is not an attempt to set a state-of-the-art
result on any benchmark, and no such claim is made anywhere in the report. It is not a
study of frontier reasoning models. The evaluation sets used in the controlled compar-
isons are small, and their sizes differ by study: 200 fresh prompts for the 0.5B arithmetic
runs, 200 per seed for the 1.5B GSM8K chain-of-thought runs, 500 for the variant au-
dit, and only 8 to 20 in several Phase-1 openings (the layer-freeze result rests on n = 8).
Table 1.1 gives the n behind each headline number. Claims about novel algorithms are
not made: the length-weighting intervention (P4) and the adaptive controller (P7) were
designed during the work and are reported as designed and simulated systems with their
evaluation limits attached, not as demonstrated improvements.
The report states negative, null and withdrawn results alongside positive ones; this is
its one general statement about reporting practice, and later chapters report the results
without repeating it. Three evidentiary rules apply throughout and are worth stating
here because they constrain what the later chapters are allowed to say. First, every num-
ber in this report is traceable to a repository file that was actually read; where a figure
could not be sourced, the finding is described qualitatively and the figure is attributed to
its source rather than reconstructed. Second, original-contract and replacement-scope
benchmark results are never pooled and never averaged across suites — the two are dif-


                                            14
ferent measurements of different things under different licences, and treating them as
one column would manufacture a number that no suite reported. Third, no improve-
ment over a baseline or over a base model is claimed unless the source states it with
a test; where the source reports a difference without a test supporting it, or reports an
inconclusive test, that is what is written here.



1.6     Contribution of the Candidate

This report covers work that spans two semesters and two very different modes of author-
ship, and the distinction matters for how the contribution should be assessed. Semester
3 was a group project (Group 6). The multi-framework repository, the baseline integra-
tions, the initial Tinker, SkyRL and GRPO experiments, the literature foundation, and
the early benchmark, evaluation and reproducibility scaffolding were produced by that
group, of which the candidate was a member. Semester 4 is the candidate’s individual
continuation, and the new research direction, the experiment expansion, the analysis, the
report writing and the P1–P8 internal reports are the candidate’s solo work (Figure 1.3).
The boundary is recorded precisely rather than loosely. The inherited state is frozen at
the Git tag capstone-final-2026-04-25, corresponding to boundary commit 21a99
ef766543a1ca86bfbad188445d3596ea73c of 23 April 2026. At the time the separa-
tion was documented, 584 commits had been made after that boundary, touching 5,050
changed files — a figure the provenance record explicitly declines to present as a measure
of academic contribution, since it counts mechanical and generated changes alongside
substantive ones. The transition to explicit solo authorship is marked by commit 282e6
b4f0f158de11b3f5bb4656e2d871f68f5c0 of 7 July 2026.
What was added after that boundary is the substance of this report. It includes the
eight unpublished internal research reports: P1, a cross-library and cross-scale study
of GRPO behaviour; P2, the Zero-Variance Fraction as a descriptive diagnostic; P3,
group size, contrast density and the relationship to DPO; P4, length bias and held-
out generalisation; P5, the minimum reporting standard for stack-conditioned results;
P6, a machine-readable GRPO stack registry; P7, an adaptive group-size controller
derived from the signal-starvation diagnostic; and P8, a side study comparing a lan-
guage model against gradient-boosted trees in credit-card fraud detection. It includes
the post-capstone experimental, audit, synthesis and report iterations — 206 recorded
analysis iterations — the Berkeley-course-derived research audits and prototypes, the
expanded statistical analyses, and the new GRPO diagnostics and controllers. And it
includes the E1–E14 benchmark campaign, in which a single Tinker-trained actor (base
Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B at commit 995ad96e…, with the campaign-adapter adapter

at commit withheld-adapter-revision…, in bfloat16) was evaluated against fourteen
suites under receipt, budget and decontamination governance. Its results are reported per
suite in Chapter 8 and never pooled; Table C.3 is the single source for each lane’s scope
and terminal state.
Two further contributions deserve naming because they are contributions of restraint
rather than of result. The first is that the campaign’s negative findings were kept rather
than discarded. A later BankerToolBench report records 100 base-model trials with
mean reward 0.0. The underlying trajectories remain unavailable, and the earlier recov-
ered finish ledger predates that rerun. The bridge’s missing stop sequences provide a
plausible failure mechanism, but the available comparison does not identify its contri-
bution to the full aggregate zero. The finding is a source-limited, confounded diagnostic
rather than a controlled model-capability estimate (Chapter 8 §8.5). The second is the
discipline applied to the project’s own claims: the same-stack audit that invalidated the
pre-audit aggregator’s “disappears” verdict did so on the strength of the study’s own
locked minimum detectable effect, and the permitted wording it left in place is narrower
than the finding it refuted.
The report does not reallocate the shared runtime code. The inherited repository remains
where it was; the Semester 4 internal reports cite their canonical LaTeX sources and
evidence directories rather than duplicating them, and the distinction recorded here is a
distinction about academic contribution, not about file ownership.



1.7     Organisation of the Report

The report is organised in four parts.
Part I — Foundations contains the present chapter, followed by Chapter 2, the litera-
ture survey, which situates the work against the RLHF and RLVR literature, the critic-
free line of estimators, the group-size and advantage-estimation literature, and the re-
producibility and reporting literature on which the provenance argument depends; and
Chapter 3, the system requirements specification, which states the functional and non-
functional requirements the harness, the telemetry layer and the campaign infrastructure
were built to satisfy.
Part II — Design and Implementation contains Chapter 4, the proposed methodol-
ogy, which sets out the measurement design, the statistical protocol, the evidence rules
and the study-to-evidence mapping; and Chapter 5, the implementation details, cover-
ing the multi-framework harness, the per-step telemetry and raw tensor persistence, the
campaign launcher and its receipt and budget governance, and the registry and validator
components.


                                           17
Part III — Results contains three chapters. Chapter 6 reports the core GRPO studies
— the cross-library and cross-scale scaling work, the ZVF diagnostic, the group-size
and contrast-density sweep, and the length-bias and held-out generalisation study, cor-
responding to P1 through P4. Chapter 7 reports the reporting, registry and controller
work, corresponding to P5 through P7, including the minimum reporting standard, the
machine-readable stack registry, and the simulated behaviour of the adaptive group-size
controller. Chapter 8 reports the E1–E14 benchmark campaign: the actor, the gover-
nance model, the strictly completed scopes, the original-contract scores, the incomplete
and blocked lanes, the verification results and the campaign’s terminal states.
Part IV — Synthesis contains Chapter 9, which draws the cross-study synthesis, states
the threats to validity, gives the conclusions the evidence will bear, and sets out future
work and the reopen conditions under which the blocked lanes could be completed.
Ten appendices follow. Appendix A gives the historical run registry and its identity
reconciliation. Appendix B documents reproducibility and provenance, including the
inherited-versus-individual boundary and the environment records needed to reproduce
the reported results. Appendix C gives the notation, symbols and abbreviations used
throughout, and its Table C.3 is the single record of each E1–E14 lane’s scope, status
and receipt. Appendix D holds the lane-by-lane campaign detail moved out of Chapter 8.
Appendix E lists corrections applied to internal source documents. Appendix F reports
the applied fraud-detection side study (P8), which uses synthetic data and supervised
fine-tuning and so sits outside the RL contribution. Appendix G records the C1 case
ledger, reference classifications and source bindings. Appendix H is the evidence map
for the preceding scientific chapters and appendices, arranged section by section. Ap-
pendix I records the scientific coverage audit and public-source verification boundary.
Appendix J records the number audit, distinguishing artifact comparisons, printed-count
calculations and context-only occurrences.

                                                            20
Chapter 2

Literature Survey

This chapter surveys the prior work on which the project is built and the gap it occupies.
It revises the survey of the Phase-1 report, retaining that survey’s seven-part structure
and updating each part with the material the Semester 4 work added: the P1–P8 internal
reports and the E1–E14 evaluation campaign. Material on RLHF, PPO, DPO, GRPO,
DeepSeek-R1, parameter-efficient fine-tuning and evaluation benchmarks also draws on
the Semester-3 Group 6 capstone literature survey [1], a group work of six authors of
whom the present author was one, which is an internal, unpublished course report rather
than a publicly retrievable source; where this chapter reuses that survey’s findings they
are attributed to it here. Two rules of construction carry over from Phase 1. The first
concerns proportion. The contribution of this project is overwhelmingly a contribution
of measurement and reporting practice rather than of a new training algorithm, so the
literature that establishes measurement practice receives more room here than the liter-
ature that establishes methods. The second concerns evidence. A figure is quoted only
where a repository source actually states it; where a source states a finding without giv-
ing a number, the finding is described qualitatively and the chapter says so. Where the
project’s own documents state the same quantity differently, the divergence is reported
rather than silently reconciled, and Section 2.5 records one such divergence in full.



2.1     From RLHF and PPO to Group-Relative Methods

Policy-gradient post-training of language models begins with the RLHF pipeline. Chris-
tiano et al. [2] established the pattern of reward-model training on human pairwise pref-
erences followed by reinforcement learning against that reward model, on Atari and Mu-
JoCo tasks starting from a randomly initialised policy; the supervised fine-tuning stage
that precedes both in the language-model pipeline was added in later work [3, 4]. Ziegler
et al. [5] adapted the pattern to language, collecting approximately 60,000 comparisons


                                           21
and, following Jaques et al. [6, 7], adopting the per-token KL penalty against a frozen
reference policy that has been standard ever since. Stiennon et al. [3] scaled the recipe
to a 6.7B summarisation model with 64,832 comparisons, demonstrated transfer to an
out-of-distribution summarisation set (CNN/DailyMail), and produced early empirical
evidence of reward over-optimisation: continued optimisation against a frozen reward
model eventually degrades the true objective. Ouyang et al. [4] then built InstructGPT,
whose three-stage pipeline used roughly 40 contractors to produce about 13,000 super-
vised fine-tuning prompts, about 33,000 reward-model prompts, and about 31,000 PPO
prompts. Two details of that work matter for this project. First, the reward model was 6B
rather than 175B because the larger reward model proved unstable during training — a
reminder that the reward model is itself a trained component with its own failure modes.
Second, the preference data were collected as rankings of K ∈ {4, …, 9} responses,
which yields C(K, 2) pairwise comparisons from a single annotation batch; this is pre-
cisely the within-prompt comparative structure that group-relative methods would later
exploit directly. InstructGPT’s headline results were that a 1.3B InstructGPT was pre-
ferred to the 175B GPT-3 base model, that the 175B InstructGPT was preferred to GPT-3
in 85% of evaluations, that hallucination on a closed-domain task fell from 41% to 21%,
that toxicity was reduced by roughly 25%, and that TruthfulQA performance roughly
doubled; inter-annotator agreement was approximately 73%. The same source records
four limitations — annotation cost, reward-model over-optimisation, the difficulty of
aligning to user intent rather than to annotator preference, and the residual capability
costs of alignment (Figure 2.1).
The optimiser for that pipeline was PPO [8]. PPO’s clipping mechanism, with 𝜀 ≈ 0.2,
restricts each update’s policy ratio, and generalised advantage estimation [9] with its
standard defaults (𝜆 = 0.95, 𝛾 = 0.99) provides the advantage estimate. Recasting lan-
guage generation as an RL problem makes the state the prompt plus generated prefix, the
action the next token, the policy the language model, and the reward sparse and termi-
nal. The practical consequence is a four-model architecture — policy, reference, reward,
and value — whose memory cost depends on which components are trained, their sizes,
optimiser states, precision and placement; counting four components does not establish
a fourfold memory ratio to supervised fine-tuning; PPO-ptx mitigates capability regres-
sion by mixing pretraining gradients into the update. Zheng et al.’s “Secrets of RLHF”
study [10] found that policy constraints, not algorithmic choice, are the single most crit-
ical factor in whether RLHF succeeds: without them, training exhibits reward hacking,
length explosion, and catastrophic pattern collapse. That finding is the intellectual an-
cestor of the zero-variance analysis in this project, because it locates the failure mode in
the constraint structure rather than in the objective.
Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), introduced with DeepSeekMath [11], re-

                                    retains reward model                                drops critic

                    RLHF                                          PPO                                           GRPO
