Figure 2.1: Algorithmic lineage from RLHF through PPO to GRPO, with DPO as an
orthogonal branch. Each transition removes a component or substitutes a cheaper es-
timator; GRPO removes the critic and replaces it with a group-relative baseline. DPO
(dashed frame) removes the on-policy loop and the reward model rather than simplify-
ing the critic, trading online exploration for stability. Dates are first publication or first
use at scale. GRPO variants (DAPO, Dr. GRPO and others) are discussed in the text and
not drawn.




                                                                   23
moves the learned value network altogether. For each prompt it samples a group of G
completions, scores them with a reward function, and standardises each completion’s
reward against the group mean and standard deviation; the resulting z-score serves as
the advantage for every token of that completion. In the DeepSeekMath configuration
G = 64 and 𝛽 = 0.04. Eliminating the value model reduces the number of trained and
served models from four to three; the paper motivates this as reducing PPO’s memory
usage but does not quantify the saving. DeepSeekMath-RL 7B reached 51.7% top-1
accuracy on MATH against a 46.8% supervised fine-tuning baseline, 60.9% with self-
consistency over 64 samples, GSM8K improved from 82.9% to 88.2%, and CMATH
improved from 84.6% to 88.8% despite no Chinese data in the RL training set. The
paper’s iterative variant retains 10% of historical data in a replay buffer to prevent catas-
trophic forgetting in the co-evolving reward model. RLOO [12] is the closely related
leave-one-out estimator that baselines each sample against the mean of the other sam-
ples in the group; it already performs a within-group baseline, and is therefore directly
relevant to the signal-starvation analysis this project conducts.
DeepSeek-R1 [13] then demonstrated that pure outcome-reward RL can elicit chain-of-
thought behaviour from a base model without supervised cold-start data. R1-Zero was
trained on DeepSeek-V3-Base, a 671B mixture-of-experts model, using rule-based re-
wards only and no neural reward model. AIME 2024 pass@1 rose from 15.6% to 71.0%
over training in the original preprint — the Nature version cited here reports 77.9% af-
ter extended training — with consensus decoding over 64 samples (cons@64) reaching
86.7% and MATH-500 reaching 95.9%. The source states the training configuration as
16 sampled outputs per question (the group size), a training batch of 512 outputs (32
questions × 16 samples), a learning rate of 3 × 10−6 , a KL coefficient of 0.001, and
approximately 10,400 policy update steps. The paper documents spontaneous chain-
of-thought, self-verification, and the widely reported “aha moment” as emergent rather
than taught behaviours. The full R1 model, trained with a four-stage pipeline that in-
cludes a language-consistency reward, reports 79.8 on AIME, 97.3 on MATH-500, and
71.5 on GPQA, against 79.2 / 96.4 / 75.7 for o1-1217. Distillation into six students
from 1.5B to 70B produced Distill-Qwen-1.5B at 28.9% AIME and 83.9% MATH-500,
and Distill-Qwen-32B at 72.6% and 94.3%, against 47.0% and 91.6% for Qwen2.5-
32B-Zero trained from scratch; the 7B and 16B base models failed to show meaningful
reasoning gains from direct RL.
For this project, the significance of that lineage is narrower than the headline numbers
suggest. GRPO’s defining operation is a within-group standardisation, and a standardi-
sation is undefined when the group’s rewards are all identical. That degenerate case is
not an edge case: it is the modal case early in training on a hard task and late in training
on an easy one. The rest of this chapter and the empirical chapters that follow are con-


                                             24
cerned with what that degeneracy does to the training signal, and with how honestly the
field currently reports it.



2.2     Direct Preference Optimisation as a Contrast

An orthogonal branch of post-training removes on-policy sampling entirely. Direct Pref-
erence Optimisation (DPO) [14] exploits the fact that the RLHF objective with a KL
constraint admits a closed-form optimal policy, which permits the reward to be repa-
rameterised in terms of the policy itself. The result is a simple classification loss over a
fixed dataset of preference pairs, with no reward model, no value model, and no sampling
loop. This removes components of the PPO training stack; no fixed memory or wall-
clock saving is asserted here, because it depends on the implementation and workload.
Four limitations are documented in the same source: the training data are static and of-
fline, so the implicit reward goes stale as the policy drifts; there is no online exploration
beyond the dataset’s support; the loss can lower the absolute likelihood of preferred
completions while widening their margin over dispreferred ones (likelihood displace-
ment); and the method has weaker credit assignment for long-horizon reasoning, where
correctness of later steps depends on earlier ones.
The contrast is structurally instructive for this project. The canonical offline DPO ob-
jective does not standardise within a sampled group, so it does not have this particular
zero-variance denominator: its contrast is supplied by the preference dataset. It also
does not itself specify an online verifier-driven sampling loop. A verifier can still sup-
ply preference labels for that dataset; the distinction here concerns the training objective
and collection protocol, not an impossibility of combining DPO with verifiable feed-
back. Group-relative RLVR constructs its contrast during rollout, making the frequency
of homogeneous reward groups a relevant measurement. The DPO family has since di-
versified into inverse-preference and non-paired variants — IPO [15], KTO [16], ORPO
[17], SimPO [18], and SLiC [19] among them — which the project’s cross-library pro-
tocol treats as no-rollout baselines.
The boundary between the two families is also less sharp than it first appears. Wu et
al. [20] argue that GRPO is “secretly DPO” — that its efficacy stems from an implicit
contrastive objective, which makes it structurally related to DPO — and report that 2-
GRPO retains 97.6% of the performance of 16-GRPO at 12.5% of the rollouts (arXiv
v3; the earlier v2 reported 98.1%). That contrastive interpretation motivates the PCD
                                                            = |𝐵| −1 Í𝑔 𝑝b𝑔 (1 − 𝑝b𝑔 ),
statistic used here. The empirical binary-reward statistic PCD
where 𝑝b𝑔 is the observed correct fraction of group g, has expectation PCD = ((G −
1)/G) · E[p(1 − p)] under conditionally independent Bernoulli rewards with per-prompt


                                             25
probability p. It supplies a magnitude-aware contrast summary rather than a measured
gradient norm, and the group-size question becomes a question about how much contrast
a given sampling budget buys. The present project is careful about what it inherits from
that argument: the reduction is cited as prior theory, while the empirical claims resting
on it are scoped to this project’s own measurements, reported in Chapter 6.



2.3     Fixing GRPO’s Biases and Collapse

A dense and fast-growing literature targets specific GRPO pathologies. Dr. GRPO [21]
removes two normalisation terms from the standard objective — the per-response 1/L
factor and the question-difficulty amplification — restoring the sequence-level gradi-
ent up to a global scale, and is motivated as a fix for an optimisation bias in GRPO
that artificially inflates response length, especially of incorrect responses. DAPO [22],
from ByteDance, changes four things in one release: asymmetric Clip-Higher clipping,
dynamic sampling that resamples until the batch carries signal, token-level loss normali-
sation, and over-long filtering; the reported result is 50 points on AIME 2024 with a 32B
model. GSPO [23] (Qwen team, 2025) moves the importance ratio from the token level
to the sequence level and was credited with stabilising the Qwen3 series, particularly its
mixture-of-experts variants. Production-side variants include VAPO [24], a value-based
PPO framework for long chain-of-thought reasoning.
A third thread attacks the zero-variance failure mode directly, and this is the thread the
project’s own diagnostic sits in. GRESO [25] pre-screens prompts before sampling so
that uninformative prompts are never paid for. CPPO [26] prunes completions after roll-
out, reporting up to 7.98× speedup on GSM8K. NGRPO [27] adds advantage calibration
and asymmetric clipping so that all-incorrect groups still carry gradient. Scaf-GRPO
[28] injects tiered scaffolding hints when learning stagnates, reporting a 44.3% relative
improvement on AIME 2024. MO-GRPO [29] addresses vanilla GRPO’s collapse to a
single reward component under multiple objectives through variance-based reward re-
weighting. SSPO [30] operates at subsentence granularity to balance GSPO’s stability
against token-level GRPO’s expressiveness, raising GRPO’s five-benchmark mathemat-
ics average from 43.01 to 46.72 on Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B. The quantity itself has been
named and analysed in contemporaneous work: the Advantage Collapse Rate [31] mea-
sures the share of batches with ineffective group-relative gradients, and a study of gradi-
ent starvation in binary-reward GRPO [32] proves that heterogeneous prompt difficulty
pushes the degeneracy rate above the i.i.d. Bernoulli prediction. RL-ZVP [33] takes
the opposite approach to filtering: it extracts signal from zero-variance prompts through
entropy-guided advantage shaping. AERO [34] instead adapts the rollout allocation and
prunes rollouts to avoid zero-advantage dead zones. GVPO [35] re-weights by within-

                                            26
group variance. The tail continues with 𝜆-GRPO [36], MC-GRPO [37] (median-centred
advantage), G2 RPO-A [38], ConSPO [39], and Goldilocks RL [40].
One act of bookkeeping in this literature deserves emphasis, because it is a claim about
what may not be inferred. AVSPO [41] and other interventions are described in the
project’s own related-work source as prior or concurrent interventions, not as evidence
for this project’s runner, and the internal report explicitly claims neither priority nor
an intervention effect over them. The present thesis inherits that disclaimer without
qualification.
Against this background, the project’s own contribution to the area is deliberately mea-
gre in algorithmic ambition and deliberately demanding in evidential standard. The
Zero-Variance Fraction (ZVF) — the fraction of groups whose completions are all cor-
rect or all incorrect — was reported across libraries, model scales, and training phases
in the companion ZVF report (P2). The later source audit shows that its pooled collapse
and outcome associations mix synthetic and measured records with heterogeneous tar-
gets (§6.3.4), so they do not validate measured collapse tracking or held-out prediction.
ZVF is mechanically entangled with accuracy and group size; its defensible role here
is descriptive instrumentation. A minimal binomial model of group degeneracy was
then proposed, in which the expected ZVF indicator is ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝) = 𝑝 𝐺 + (1 − 𝑝) 𝐺 and
the usable signal is 𝑆 = 𝑝(1 − 𝑝)(1 − ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝)); three regularities follow in closed form,
each paired with an empirical check drawn from a 368-run Weights & Biases audit that
counts all logged runs and is therefore a superset of the benchmark’s 70+ core training
runs. Because ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝) is symmetric under 𝑝 ↔ 1 − 𝑝, ZVF assigns the same value to
a mastered prompt and to a hopeless one. The U-shape reported across reward levels
in a 368-run audit (nine model labels; earlier drafts said six) is largely this identity plus
prompt-difficulty heterogeneity: on three stored Qwen3-8B tensors a beta-binomial with
intraclass correlation 0.16 predicts ZVF 0.170 against 0.158 observed, where the homo-
geneous binomial predicts 0.054 (§6.3.3), so the U-shape is not independent support
for the model; and exact-tie ZVF is sensitive to reward rounding: jitter of order 10−4
eliminates exact ties while shifting measured within-group contrast by less than 10−6 .
This does not hold for the declared sample-variance threshold of 10−6 , which retains
95/600 groups before and after jitter (§6.3.1). Pairwise-contrast density is proposed as a
magnitude-aware candidate control signal, without this test establishing its superiority
over tolerance-based ZVF, and the toy-scale validation reports a gradient-norm corre-
lation of corr = +0.71, marked directional only; only the scalar survives, so it cannot
be recomputed, and it is in large part mechanical. The word controller in that work
names the implemented policy, not an established improvement: the decisive static-G =
16 matched-token bakeoff remains unrun.
The one genuinely measured GRPO-variant comparison in the repository is a Dr. GRPO


                                             27
versus GRPO control. On Qwen2.5-0.5B two-digit arithmetic at group size 8, 40 steps,
and 5 seeds, both arms were at ceiling and indistinguishable: GRPO 0.987 ± 0.003
against Dr. GRPO 0.992 ± 0.003 (mean ± SE over 5 seeds; SDs 0.006 and 0.008), paired
difference +0.5 percentage points, t = 1.05, df = 4, p = 0.35; mean completion length
was 4.74 against 4.71 tokens. At that ceiling, and with completions under five tokens,
the task cannot show either an accuracy or a length difference. In a second regime —
Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct on GSM8K with a 200-token chain-of-thought cap and 3 seeds
— GRPO moved from 20.2% to 26.3% and Dr. GRPO from 20.5% to 25.5%; per-seed
McNemar tests were significant in 2 of 3 seeds for GRPO (p = 0.071, 0.036, 0.016) and 1
of 3 for Dr. GRPO (p = 0.122, 0.017, 0.122), and the pooled p < 10−3 is expressly rejected
as overstating significance; with the seed as the unit, one-sample t-tests on the three per-
seed gains give p = 0.005 (GRPO) and p = 0.010 (Dr. GRPO) (recomputed from drgrpo_
gsm8k_cot.json). Neither arm inflated length over 30 steps (GRPO 195 → 180 tokens;
Dr. GRPO 194 → 187). The same source contains head-to-head and rank-correlation
tables whose values are projections, not measurements: the GRPO baseline row is a
synthetic baseline produced by a dry run of platform_hybrid/experiments/var
iance_mitigation_integration.py, the file states that it makes no measurement
claim for any row of that table, the correlation coefficients (0.78 / 0.66 / 0.63 / 0.31)
carry the same caveat, and the configuration proxies under platform_local/unified/
are explicitly not faithful reimplementations. Those numbers are reported here only
to mark them as unusable as evidence. The nearest thing to a headline cross-library
effect in the project is also the one that must be read most carefully: holding the visible
group size, learning rate, data, seed, and label fixed while changing stacks moved final
GSM8K reward from 85.6% to 5.0%, a 17× swing; the audit that followed found the
managed stack had also pinned a different base checkpoint (Qwen3-8B-Base on Tinker
against the instruction-tuned Qwen3-8B on TRL), so the source describes this as an
under-specification exhibit rather than a backend-only causal effect. The checkpoint
change alone accounts for about 64% of the gap on the Tinker harness, and several other
stack components also differ (§7.1). The complementary telemetry fact is that running
“DAPO” on an open Colab trainer whose dynamic sampling demonstrably filters zero-
variance groups produced mean ZVF 0.00, while running “DAPO” on a closed managed
stack where only an asymmetric-clip surrogate is user-settable produced mean ZVF 0.58
— the same label, opposite telemetry.




                                            28
2.4     Verifiable Rewards, Frameworks and Reporting
        Practice

RLVR — reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards — was formalised for open
post-training in TÜLU 3 [42], which is the source this project follows in treating a pro-
grammatic checker rather than a learned preference model as the reward. The implemen-
tation landscape the project unifies is crowded and heterogeneous. On the training side
it includes TRL, OpenRLHF [43], veRL (HybridFlow) [44], NeMo-RL, DeepSpeed-
Chat [45], trlX, Axolotl, and Tinker as a managed back-end; each makes different de-
fault choices about advantage normalisation, KL estimators, tokenisation loss masking,
rollout batching, and importance-sampling granularity, and the project records these
as candidate explanations for the observed cross-library gap. The comparison retains
checkpoint and managed-setting confounders, so their individual contributions are not
identified (§5.2). On the inference side, vLLM [46] and SGLang [47] supply the rollout
backends on which modern frameworks depend, with FlashAttention [48] and LoRA
[49] as load-bearing kernels. Tinker is adopted here through its message_with_tool
interface as the neutral substrate for tool-use extensions, and the same source explicitly
cites tool-use and agentic RL settings as concurrent environments in which this project’s
ZVF measurements will need to be re-evaluated rather than assumed to transfer.
The evaluation benchmarks are equally well established and equally awkward. GSM8K
[50] contains 8,792 problems in the cited split, 7,473 training and 1,319 test, on which,
at release, a fine-tuned 175B GPT-3 baseline solved roughly a third of the test problems
and test-time verification over 100 sampled solutions raised this to about 55% (Cobbe et
al., Figure 5); GSM8K-Platinum [51] has since documented label noise in the original
set, which is why the source describes the benchmark as necessary but not sufficient.
MATH [52] comprises 12,500 problems across seven subjects and five difficulty levels,
on which the original results reached 6.9%; MATH-500 is the 500-problem subset intro-
duced with the Lightman process-supervision work [53]. Each AIME paper comprises
15 problems with integer answers in 0–999; reported AIME 2024 accuracies include
GPT-5 at 95.7% and Grok 4 at 94.3%. Because these benchmarks are close to satura-
tion, the source frames chain-of-thought as the action space that makes them interesting
at all, and identifies process reward models as the bridge from terminal outcome signals
to step-level credit.
Reporting practice is where the project’s own contribution is anchored, and its lineage
is explicit. Model Cards [54] introduced documentation of intended use, out-of-scope
use, and per-slice evaluation results; Datasheets for Datasets [55] extended the idea to
datasets, covering composition, preprocessing, annotation process, and discriminatory-
impact mitigation; Data Statements [56] added the speaker-demographic axis; and Data

                                           29
Cards [57] bridged research and industry practice with schema, provenance, sampling-
distribution, and audit-transparency fields. A cross-coupling audit at iteration 109 maps
these four works onto the project’s legacy 18-field experimental manifest, showing that
they cover 12 distinct fields, that the remaining six are either RL-specific (per-step ZVF,
group-size schedule) or stack-coverage items with no direct analogue in the prior litera-
ture, and that items 9–12 — annotation process and annotator demographics — have the
lowest evidence density in the live manifests. The same iteration verified all four entries
against CrossRef metadata (4/4 pass on year, five-gram title, and author-family overlap
≥ 0.6) and retrofitted two entries that had been present in the bibliography but never
cited. The resulting standard, MIN-REPORT-RL, has eight items: seven run-manifest
fields and one evaluation item (held-out pass@k), which belongs to the evaluation report
rather than to run-start stack identity. The count was aligned at eight on 2026-07-11, and
this thesis uses “the eight-item standard” and “the seven manifest fields” in that sense
throughout.
The reproducibility literature to which this standard is a deliberate echo is older and bet-
ter established. Henderson et al. [58] showed that deep-RL results depend on codebase,
hyperparameters, and even the choice of random seeds, to the point that the same algo-
rithm from two repositories behaves as two algorithms. Colas et al. [59] and Agarwal et
al. [60] (rliable) supplied the statistical repair kit — rigorous comparison protocols
and small-sample-aware aggregate metrics with interquartile-mean reporting and per-
formance profiles. Jordan et al. [61] argued that rigorous benchmarking in RL is com-
putationally prohibitive at the scale it would require, and called for an additional exper-
imentation paradigm to complement it. Dodge et al. [62] proposed reporting expected
validation performance as a function of compute budget; the project’s run manifest is
the RL-for-LLM analogue, replacing budget with stack. Pineau et al. [63] formalised
the NeurIPS reproducibility criteria, and the project draws the explicit lesson that self-
attested checklists have limits, which motivates an artifact-computed badge rather than
a declared one. Statistical treatments of evaluation noise (Miller [64]) quantify the un-
certainty floor against which the project’s own within-noise findings should be read, and
the evaluation-stack sensitivity documented by the LM Evaluation Harness effort [65],
by audits of reasoning evaluations [66], and by contamination studies of the data side of
the same coin, together establish that RL training inherits all of this — the verifier is an
evaluator in the loop — plus the additional vectors unique to on-policy optimisation.
Three existing resources come closest to the project’s registry and are the reason it ex-
ists in its present form. HELM [67] aggregates evaluation across a large model and
scenario matrix, but its unit is the (model, scenario) cell, not the training stack. The LM
Evaluation Harness [65] standardises evaluation by executing implementations, whereas
the registry here standardises by describing stacks it cannot execute — many of them


                                            30
closed — which is precisely why the project required a null-versus-absent convention
and an R5 rung that records un-auditability rather than converting it into a false value.
Papers with Code catalogued paper–code–leaderboard linkage at scale, stopped at the
repository boundary, and was discontinued in July 2025; the source treats its fate as a
governance cautionary tale. The project’s own claim to novelty is narrow and stated
negatively: no existing resource provides the executed loss form at GRPO-variant gran-
ularity, per-component implementation status, or an unauditability marker.



2.5     Scaling and Layer-Adaptation Context

Compute-optimal pretraining is governed by well-characterised scaling laws (Kaplan et
al. [68]; Hoffmann et al., Chinchilla [69]), but RL post-training is much less well charac-
terised, and the sources the project builds on separate cleanly into those that predict and
those that describe. Hilton et al. [70] derived compute-efficiency frontiers for single-
agent RL across Procgen games, Dota 2 and a toy MNIST-based task. For RLHF, Gao et
al. [71] characterised the over-optimisation curve of reward models — the point at which
additional optimisation against a frozen reward model buys a decreasing true objective
— and Rafailov et al. [72] extended over-optimisation scaling laws to direct alignment
algorithms (DPO, IPO and SLiC), which have no separate proxy reward model. Nim-
maturi et al. [73] derived an empirical scaling law specifically for GRPO, identifying
three training phases (slow start, rapid improvement, plateau) and reporting that training
beyond roughly 80% of a single epoch yields negligible further reward gain (abstract,
p. 1). Tan et al. [74] treat group size as a budget knob in an empirical scaling study
of GRPO post-training, in an appendix ablation over G ∈ {4, 8, 16, 32} on 7B models
showing that larger G is more sample-efficient while the compute-optimal G shifts with
the training budget (Appendix B.2). Snell et al. [75] treat inference-time compute as a
scaling axis in its own right, and the project’s own related-work source cites these as the
works against which its measurements should be read.
The project’s scaling study covers 70+ runs across seven RL libraries and five model
families. This thesis uses one model range throughout: 0.6B–1T, with the anchors above
235B single-seed and descriptive. The rule is that the range spans every model with at
least one completed run in the P1 roster. The source documents state narrower ranges
because each covers a subset: 0.6B–~671B in the P2, P3 and P4 introductions, and
0.6B–235B for the multi-family data in the related-work source. The principal empir-
ical result of the scaling work is a null: across that range, no reliable scaling law is
recovered from reward curves. The companion group-size study adds a second bounded
negative: a three-seed sweep does not establish an optimum, since the measured arms
are statistically indistinguishable, and the measured signal-to-noise ratio grows at only

                                            31
                   √
about 52% of the G ideal, with G ∈ {2, 4, 8, 16} measured and G ∈ {4, 8, 16, 32, 64}
explicitly reconstructed rather than run.
Layer adaptation supplies the second half of this section’s context, and connects the
project’s white-box investigation to the parameter-efficient fine-tuning literature. LoRA
[49] freezes the pretrained weights and injects trainable low-rank matrices into the at-
tention projections, so that a d × d projection at d = 4096 with rank r = 16 trains 2dr
rather than d2 parameters, a 128× reduction (this chapter’s arithmetic; the paper itself
reports up to 10,000× fewer trainable parameters for GPT-3 175B). QLoRA [76] extends
this by quantising the frozen base to NF4 and adding double quantisation, which saves
about 0.37 bits per parameter — approximately 3 GB for a 65B model — and permits
65B fine-tuning on a single 48 GB GPU. The motivating arithmetic is that Adam at
mixed precision costs roughly 16N bytes, exceeding 100 GB for a 7B model. Prefix tun-
ing, prompt tuning, and adapter layers are the earlier members of the same family, and
LoRA’s advantages for RL specifically are that the frozen base doubles as the reference
policy and that the trainable parameter count is small enough for modest hardware. The
project’s layer-adaptation pillar asks a narrower and orthogonal question: where inside
the network GRPO-style adaptation concentrates, and whether the layers that will matter
can be identified early enough to freeze the rest. The reported finding is moderate con-
centration of per-layer adaptation, with the important negative that step-1 predictability
does not survive scaling. This connects directly to Mukherjee et al. [77], who found
that RL fine-tuning updates only 5–30% of model parameters across seven algorithms
and ten model families, suggesting that RL activates a sparse subnetwork. The length-
bias pillar completes the scaling picture with its own bounded null: under a 200-token
GSM8K generation cap, GRPO and Dr. GRPO produce held-out gains that are indis-
tinguishable at the project’s seed budget, and neither arm shows length inflation; the
source is careful to state that the 200-token cap is part of the estimand rather than a nui-
sance detail, so the null bounds the capped, short-horizon regime and cannot adjudicate
uncapped reasoning or long agent trajectories.



2.6     Process-, Tree- and Stability-Aware Variants

Beyond the group-baseline fixes, a parallel line of work changes what signal drives the
update. Process reward models supply denser, step-level feedback instead of a single
terminal reward, which reduces the chance that a whole group collapses to identical ter-
minal rewards. Let’s Verify Step by Step [53] showed that step-level PRMs substantially
outperform outcome reward models on MATH; Math-Shepherd [78] derives process re-
wards from automatically generated process annotations, avoiding costly human labels;
PRM800K [53] and PRMBench [79] supply step-level evaluation data; OmegaPRM [80]

                                            32
uses Monte-Carlo tree search to supervise process rewards without human-in-the-loop;
ReasonEval [81] scores reasoning steps for validity and redundancy with trained LLM
evaluators; and Implicit PRM [82] learns process rewards implicitly from outcome la-
bels, with VersaPRM [83] extending process rewards to multi-domain tasks. Tree-of-
thought search [84], self-consistency [85], and critic-style post-hoc verifiers all either
benefit from or substitute for process supervision. The counter-current that matters
most here is evaluation sensitivity: “A Sober Look” [66] shows that reported gains on
mathematical-reasoning benchmarks are highly sensitive to decoding parameters, ran-
dom seeds, prompt format and hardware, so that many reported improvements fall within
evaluation variance. Reward hacking is a separate risk, documented for learned reward
models as over-optimisation [71]; together they are the project’s stated motivation for in-
sisting on binary verifiable rewards and fixed evaluation settings in its own mathematics
work.
Stability-aware variants constrain the update itself. GSPO’s sequence-level impor-
tance ratio, discussed above, is the clearest instance, credited with stabilising the
Qwen3 mixture-of-experts series. SSPO’s subsentence-granularity objective is explic-
itly framed as balancing GSPO’s stability against token-level GRPO’s expressiveness.
Entropy-guided advantage shaping in RL-ZVP regulates the update when the group car-
ries no reward contrast, and GRESO’s pre-screening is a sampling-side stability con-
trol. On the reasoning-RL side, GRPO-Lead [86] and LCPO [87] tune reasoning length
through length-aware rewards, LIMR [88] selects a small high-impact subset of RL
training data, Search-R1 [89] trains reasoning agents to interleave search with chain-of-
thought, and TTRL [90] performs test-time RL on unlabelled data using majority-vote
rewards.
The Phase-1 survey’s judgement on this body of work is retained without amendment,
because the Semester 4 evidence has not changed it. These methods propose levers;
this project first builds the instrumentation needed to tell whether any such lever con-
verts wasted rollouts into held-out accuracy — ZVF, group utilisation, pairwise-contrast
density, and the collapse decomposition. The nulls reported in the results chapters are
exactly the kind of evidence that instrumentation is designed to produce, and they con-
stitute a standing caution against adopting a variant on the strength of a single-seed im-
provement. The project applies that caution to its own proposals: the adaptive group-size
controller is described as an implemented policy rather than an established improvement,
and its decisive matched-token comparison remains unrun.

and, more importantly, what is not. Table 2.1 reproduces the Phase-1 positioning and
updates it with the Semester 4 evidence (Figure 2.2).

The Semester 4 evaluation campaign extends this positioning from method comparison
to benchmark practice. Rather than adding one more leaderboard number, it evaluates a
single trained actor against fourteen agentic and reasoning suites absent from its recorded
training-suite list, each through its own native grader at a pinned revision, under receipt
and budget controls. Content-level decontamination remains unverified. The campaign
retains these limits, and it reports every lane’s terminal state rather than a cross-suite
aggregate. Its results are in Chapter 8; Table C.3 gives each lane’s scope and status. One
finding bears directly on the benchmark-practice literature: E4’s zero-scoring run used
a serving bridge that passed no stop sequences (Section 8.5). The defect is a plausible
mechanism, but its contribution to the mean reward of 0.0 across 100 base-model trials
remains unquantified, which is exactly the kind of unreported stack detail this positioning
argues must be recorded.
Two gaps in this survey should be stated plainly, since the chapter’s purpose is to locate
the work rather than to flatter it. First, the primary papers for the fourteen evaluation
suites are not surveyed here at the depth the algorithmic literature receives; the suites are
discussed as instruments, sourced to the campaign’s own results document, and their na-
tive protocols are documented in Chapter 8 and its receipt trail rather than in this chapter.
Second, the survey’s treatment of tool-use and agentic RL is inherited from the project’s
own related-work source and is explicitly flagged there as a concurrent setting in which
the ZVF measurements will need to be re-evaluated rather than assumed to transfer.
What the chapter does establish is that the project’s contribution is a measurement and
reporting contribution, that the literature it sits in is dense enough that claim-narrowing
rather than claim-extension is the appropriate move, and that the specific gap the port-
folio occupies — machine-readable, per-component, executed-loss-form disclosure for
group-relative RL runs — is not filled by any of the resources surveyed above.




                                             37
Chapter 3

System Requirements Specification

This chapter states what the Tinker RL Lab system must do, what qualities it must
hold while doing it, and how each requirement is verified. It inherits the specifica-
tion of the Phase-1 report and extends it across the Semester-4 additions: the multi-
framework benchmark rosters, the Zero-Variance Fraction (ZVF) diagnostic and the
adaptive group-size controller derived from it, the minimum reporting standard and the
machine-readable GRPO stack registry, and the E1–E14 evaluation campaign with its
fail-closed completion gate. It is written as a specification rather than as a retrospective
description, but every requirement below corresponds to a component that exists in the
repository, and every functional requirement is paired with the concrete test that checks
it. These are design requirements, not a claim that every historical run satisfies them.
Incomplete framework parity is recorded in §3.2 and §3.8; per-run provenance and loss-
form gaps are recorded in Table 4.2. The former cloud-capacity blockers are historical
limitations (§3.6), while the white-box gradient requirement is explicitly restricted to
models whose internals are exposed.



3.1     Overview and Actors

The central object of the Phase-1 specification was the run: one configuration, one
seed, one framework, one training budget, producing per-step telemetry and a persisted
set of raw group reward tensors. Semester 4 introduces two further first-class objects.
The first is the lane: a single benchmark suite evaluated under a named scope — an
original contract with the suite’s owner, or a declared replacement scope — carrying its
own provider, payload, licence, quota and terminal state. The second is the receipt: an
immutable, hash-bound record that a declared step actually happened, against declared
inputs, at a declared revision. Requirements that concern runs, lanes and receipts are
distinguished below where the distinction matters.


                                            38
Four actors interact with the system, and the fourth is new in Semester 4.
The experimenter declares work. Under the original design this meant declaring a
run through a single configuration object and nothing else: the model, the dataset, the
reward function, the decoding parameters (temperature, top-𝑝, maximum tokens), the
group size 𝐺, the learning rate, the KL coefficient 𝛽 and the seed all live in one file,
and the framework is a switch rather than a rewrite. Under the campaign design the
same actor declares a lane through a sealed resource request: the lane specification fixes
an immutable task bundle, a pinned native runtime, a native grader, a budget and an
applicable licence in advance, and those declared inputs are then checked for internal
consistency before any launch is permitted.
The back-end executes the training loop and, under the managed path, is the Tinker
service; under the white-box path it is a local or rented GPU. For evaluation, the corre-
sponding actor is the provider grader: the benchmark owner’s own native evaluator at
a pinned revision. The system is permitted to record a suite score only when that eval-
uator produced it, or when a declared replacement harness reproduces it under a scope
that is labelled as a replacement.
The auditor is a downstream script or an independent reader who recomputes published
diagnostics from stored raw artefacts. In Semester 4 this role acquires force: the audit
suite of §3.4 is executed as a gate with a non-zero exit status rather than as advisory
tooling, and the completion gate for the campaign lanes is fail-closed by construction,
in that a missing or malformed declaration stops the procedure instead of degrading it.
The campaign operator holds launch authority for paid work. Spend on any lane re-
quires a bound authorization receipt issued by the operator; the system will not mint
one for itself, and lanes are recorded as blocked when the receipt or the technical chain
behind it is absent.
A fifth party, the external access holder, is an actor only in the negative sense: several
lanes depend on a private payload, a hosted evaluation service or a quota grant that only
the provider can issue. The specification requires that such a lane be recorded with an
explicit terminal state and a named reopen condition rather than silently dropped, which
is why the campaign’s terminal-state vocabulary exists as a first-class output.

3.4     Test-Case Specification

The test cases below specify checks attached to the requirements. A test specification or
source-code inspection is not an execution receipt, and a structural audit pass does not
independently establish a numerical or scientific claim. The traceability matrix names
the intended verification route; dated result evidence and remaining source limitations
appear in the results chapters and Appendix B. TC-1 through TC-5 are inherited from
the Phase-1 specification and are restated here because later chapters depend on them;
TC-6 onward are the Semester-4 additions, and TC-6 is the suite that carries the largest
share of the functional requirements.
TC-1 — Recompute ZVF and the collapse decomposition from stored tensors. Re-
compute the published ZVF and collapse figures from the persisted per-group reward
tensors of the main configuration, using the documented analysis script. The published
values are a ZVF of approximately 0.72–0.77 and an all-correct fraction of 0.65–0.71;
recomputation must match. Verifies FR-3 and FR-5.
TC-2 — Recompute the detector metric from raw tensors. Recompute the Phase-3
detector’s area under the ROC curve from raw tensors under five-fold stratified cross-


                                           46
validation. The published figure is an AUROC of 0.84±0.01 against 0.43 for the reward-
only comparator. Verifies FR-5 and NFR-3.
TC-3 — Re-run a matched-budget arm comparison. Re-run the baseline and the
curriculum arm at a matched token budget over three seeds; the Phase-1 result is that
both arms average +0.028 on the held-out set with no curriculum advantage. Verifies
FR-1, FR-2 and FR-6.
TC-4 — Recompute headline diagnostics. Recompute each headline diagnostic
quoted in a results chapter directly from stored artefacts, with the recomputation
recorded next to the published value. Verifies NFR-3.
TC-5 — Provenance verification. Verify a run’s provenance record end to end, from
configuration to rollout hashes to the grader version. Verifies FR-8.
TC-6 — Execute the submission audit suite as a gate.                        Run python
platform_local/run_all_audits.py.                     The suite executes nine au-
dits — claim_issues, sync_issues, anon_issues, strength_issues,
package_issues, workflow_issues, export_guard_issues, caveat_issues and
scientific_issues — emits one METRIC <name>=<count> line per audit, and re-
turns exit status 0 only when every audit reports zero issues. Individual checks that this
test enforces include the presence of scope and caveat language in the review documents
(readme_missing_key_results_scope_header, readme_missing_heldout_lan
guage, checklist_missing_preliminary_key_results_label), the absence of
overstated checkpoint claims (misleading_checkpoint_availability_claim, sub
mission_overstates_checkpoint_release), the presence of every reviewer caveat
(heldout_scope, tool_eval_protocol, codegen_subset, capacity_confound,
moe_diagnostics, budget_and_splits, replication_release), the absence of
banned marketing formulations, the survival of the technical caveats into the anonymous
build (anonymous_missing:<label> for the seven needles), and the presence of the
audit call and its skip flag in the export script. Verifies FR-16, NFR-4 and NFR-7.
TC-7 — Structural checks on the evaluation script and its result files. For each held-
out evaluation script, assert by parsing the source — not by reading its documentation
— that it exposes a seed argument, locks the dataset split to the test split, samples with
a non-zero temperature, consumes a checkpoint path, and writes the declared metadata
keys including the split, the sampling flag, the seed and the model source. For each result
file, assert that the schema version is 2, that the evaluation status is one of completed or
failed, that the required configuration and summary fields are present, that the recorded
split is the test split, that the attempted count equals the sum of correct and incorrect,
and that a failed evaluation carries a failure reason and a completed evaluation carries
non-zero attempts. Verifies FR-8 and NFR-4.


                                            47
TC-8 — Build the report from source. Compile the main report and its supplementary
through the full LaTeX chain and flag an empty journal field in the bibliography. Verifies
FR-16.
TC-9 — Dry-run the completion gate against a declared lane. Present a provider
package to the gate and require it to validate the declaration without executing anything:
the expected outcomes are acceptance with the declared evidence class, or a specific
refusal naming the missing element. A package must never be reported as a benchmark
result by this procedure. Verifies FR-13, FR-14 and NFR-7.
TC-10 — Registry query and null discipline. Query the registry for a labelled entry
and its delta record, and confirm that a field absent from the source is returned as unre-
ported rather than as a zero value, and that a same-label pair with divergent behaviour
triggers the flip-risk verdict. Verifies FR-9, FR-10, FR-11 and NFR-10.

3.7     Software Requirements

The runtime is Python 3.11 with PyTorch and the Hugging Face stack (Transformers,
PEFT, Datasets) for model handling, tokenisation and adapters; the RL back-ends are
TRL, veRL through its HybridFlow path, and OpenRLHF, driven through the shared
launcher, with the Tinker client SDK for the managed path. Experiment tracking is
a weights-and-biases project; telemetry is stored as JSONL and as persisted tensors;
analysis is carried out by the documented scripts named in the Phase-1 specification.
Semester 4 adds five software components to the requirement set. First, the audit suite:
platform_local/run_all_audits.py together with the nine audit modules it im-
ports and the shared data model in utils/audit_utils.py, which defines the issue,
result and suite types and the METRIC line format; the suite requires a Python interpreter
with no third-party dependencies beyond the standard library for its structural checks.
Second, the completion gate and the per-lane harnesses under zvf-program/, which re-
quire Ed25519 signature verification and JSON Schema validation against the published
provider-package, provider-grant and trust-root schema versions. Third, the registry: a
schema, an entry store and a query interface, together with the provenance records un-
der platform_hybrid/registry/. Fourth, the report toolchain: a LaTeX distribution
capable of the multi-pass build with BibTeX, plus a markdown assembler that concate-
nates the chapter files and, when available, renders the document through pandoc. Fifth,
the judgement-layer harness under zvf-program/jev_lab/, which records its own re-

                                           52
ceipts and is used only for classification, faithfulness checking and value ordering.



3.8     Constraints and Assumptions

Rewards are binary and verifiable. The specification assumes a programmatic checker
— exact match for the mathematics tasks and unit tests for the code subset. Nothing in
the requirement set addresses learned reward models, and none of the diagnostics should
be read as applying to them.
Held-out sets are small. Several Phase-1 evaluation sets have 𝑛 between 8 and 20,
and the controlled Semester-4 comparisons use 200 to 500 items (Table 1.1 gives the n
behind each headline), with the consequence that learning-gain magnitudes are noise-
limited by construction. This is a constraint on what the results can mean, not a defect of
the analysis: the small-𝑛 figures are reported with their denominators and are not used
to support comparative claims.
The managed back-end is a black box at the layer level. Per-layer gradients are not
exposed on the managed path, so claims about the relationship between reward spread
and gradient magnitude are confined to the small-model white-box configuration and
are directional only.
Prompt exposure and response sampling are separate controls. A shared determin-
istic prompt schedule is a design requirement, not a property of every executed compari-
son. The primary arithmetic group-size sweep holds 16 prompts per step fixed, while its
number of sampled completions changes with G. The original PPO/GRPO comparison
consumes different prompt streams and evaluates mostly different items with unequal
train–evaluation overlap (§6.2.3); the later GSM8K comparison fixes 64 generations but
uses 8, 32 or 64 prompts per step (§6.5.5). Even a matched prompt schedule does not
remove response-sampling noise or establish that a ZVF difference is caused by training
dynamics. These controls and exceptions must accompany each contrast.
Framework parity is incomplete. The requirement in FR-2 covers two seven-library
rosters, but only two of the seven cross-launcher entries produced completed runs in this
release, and the remaining comparison entries are dry-run placeholders. Comparative
framework claims are therefore constrained to the rosters and the completed runs actually
recorded, and the repository states the dry-run status explicitly.
The scale study reports descriptive limits rather than a scaling law. The cross-scale
analysis spans more than seventy runs across seven libraries and five model families,
and its central result is a negative one: no reliable positive cross-scale slope is recovered
and no saturation exponent is identifiable. The specification inherits that boundary —
the requirement is to report identifiability limits, not to assert a law.

                                             53
External dependencies are assumed unavailable until proven otherwise. Payload
grants, hosted evaluations and quota increases are treated as absent until a response
arrives; a lane without the required external benchmark scope is closed as externally
blocked with a reopen condition rather than left pending indefinitely.
Arithmetic and string lookups stay in code. Whatever judgement layer is used for
triage, classification or value ordering, verification-type checks are performed by de-
terministic code. This rule was banked after the judgement layer returned an incorrect
answer to a parity probe in a healthy window, and it constrains the architecture: no re-
quirement in this chapter may be verified by a language model where a deterministic
check is possible.
Named variants are labels, not specifications. The assumption that a method name
identifies a method is false on the available evidence: entries sharing a label diverge
substantially in measured behaviour, which is why the registry’s delta records and flip-
risk verdicts are specified as requirements rather than as documentation niceties.




                                          54
         Part II

Design and Implementation




           55
Chapter 4

Proposed Methodology

This chapter sets out the methodology of the work rather than its results. It presents, in
order: the design commitment to attribution and the four de-confound pillars that oper-
ationalise it (§4.1); the per-step telemetry that makes a run’s internal state observable
at the granularity at which the mechanism acts (§4.2); the formalisation of the Zero-
Variance Fraction as a descriptive diagnostic rather than a metric (§4.3); the eight-study
portfolio P1–P8 and the question each study asks (§4.4); and the design of the E1–E14
evaluation campaign, including its single trained actor, pinned native evaluators, frozen
sampling, receipt model, terminal-state taxonomy and decontamination handling (§4.5).
Throughout, the reporting discipline is part of the method: figures are tied to named ev-
idence, unavailable primary artefacts are distinguished from surviving summaries (Ap-
pendix B §B.11), and lanes without an admissible result are recorded as blocked (Fig-
ure 4.1).



4.1     The Design Commitment to Attribution

The premise of the project is that an RL-for-LLM result is not identified by the name of
its algorithm. Two runs both labelled GRPO may differ in loss form, reference-policy
handling, sampler, precision, base-checkpoint identity, data order and group-size sched-
ule, and the label alone does not say which of those differences produced the outcome.
The project’s response is a design commitment rather than a post-hoc covariate adjust-
ment: hold the experimental stack fixed except the intended contrast, then disclose any
coupled changes that prevent attribution to that factor alone. This commitment is the
methodological spine of the whole thesis, and it has a practical corollary that shapes
every experiment reported later: a study is defined by the single axis it varies and is only
interpretable to the extent that every other axis was genuinely pinned (Figure 4.2).
The commitment is implemented in a unified launcher, platform_local/unified/la

                                                                 57
uncher.py, which consumes a single configuration object and dispatches it to thin per-
framework adapters — trl_integrations/, verl/, platform_modal/openrlhf/,
platform_hybrid/skyrl/ and platform_tinker/atropos/ — with the reference
GRPO loop implemented once in platform_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo.py. The re-
ward grader and the decoding path are shared code. Shared grading and decoding con-
figuration are intended controls. They are not sufficient to identify a framework effect:
the executed framework comparison retains a Base-versus-Instruct checkpoint differ-
ence and unresolved managed settings (§5.2), and completed-run parity exists only for
Tinker and TRL. That comparison therefore describes the recorded stacks, not an iso-
lated causal effect of framework. The commitment also bounds what a result may claim.
A completed run supports a statement about that stack, at that revision, with that grader;
it does not license a statement about “GRPO” in the abstract, and the project’s reporting
standard (§4.4, P5) is built around that distinction.
Four pillars organise the commitment, with the actual controls and coupled changes
stated for each contrast:

                                           58
Pillar 1 asks whether a reported GRPO advantage survives when the advantage estimator
is the main thing that changes. The comparison is designed on Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B
two-digit addition over 40 steps and five seeds paired by seed, evaluated on 200 prompts
per arm drawn fresh from the training generator rather than from a disjoint split (the
two arms’ item sets largely differ; §6.2.3), and the exact loss form of both arms is in
Table 4.2. Because that task is near ceiling, the design can bound an estimator effect
but cannot separate “no effect” from “no headroom”; the result is reported in Chapter 6
§6.2.3. The Qwen3-8B row in the project’s own effect-size table is excluded from Pillar
1 as a provenance conflict and is not treated as an estimate. Pillar 2 turns a run’s internal
signal state into a first-class measurement; it is formalised in §4.3. Pillar 3 varies the
group size G while holding model, data, reward and schedule fixed, and is the axis on
which the preference-density argument of P3 rests. Pillar 4 asks the question the other
three cannot answer — whether any of this changes held-out behaviour — by comparing
a pre-RL checkpoint with its post-RL successor on data the training loop never saw.
The headline configuration of the internal report sources is LoRA rank 32, Adam with
𝛽1 = 0.9 and 𝛽2 = 0.95, learning rate 1 × 10−4 , and the seed set {42, 123, 456,
789, 1024}. It is not held fixed across the programme. The canonical dispatch ma-
trix uses rank 16 and learning rate 1 × 10−6 , the stack probe uses 1 × 10−5 , the P4
scale extension uses rank 4, and the Tinker runners default to 3 × 10−5 ; Table 4.2
lists the values each study actually used. The instrumented 79-run roster described
in that source spans the Qwen3, Qwen3.5, Llama-3.x, DeepSeek-V3.1 and Nemotron-
120B families, from 0.6B to approximately 671B total parameters, including mixture-
of-experts anchors at the frontier end of that range; the wider P1 cross-scale roster
adds a 1T anchor (Kimi-K2) and so spans 0.6B to 1T (Chapter 6 §6.1; source: plat-
form_hybrid/paper/sections/p1_abstract.tex). The statistical apparatus is declared in the
shared methods source but was not pre-registered (Chapter 9 §9.2; the analysis log
records 206 iterations). It consists of Welch t-tests and Mann–Whitney U tests for two-
sample contrasts; Cohen’s d with Hedges–Olkin intervals; 95% bootstrap confidence in-
tervals with 10,000 resamples; rliable aggregate metrics (interquartile mean, median and
optimality gap); and Bonferroni and Benjamini–Hochberg corrections over a declared
test family. Power was computed rather than assumed: at 80% power the minimum de-
tectable effect for an equal-n comparison of five seeds against five is d_min = 2.024,
improving to d_min = 1.530 when a five-seed group is compared against a pooled group
of fifteen. Those figures are a standing reminder of how coarse a five-seed design is, and
the project reports several null results that are null precisely because the design could
not have detected anything smaller.
Two limitations belong in this section rather than in a later discussion. First, the frame-
work roster is broader than the framework evidence: although seven RL libraries are


                                             59
named in the study design, only Tinker and TRL produced completed runs, and the
veRL and OpenRLHF entries in framework_comparison.json are dry-run placehold-
ers rather than measurements. Cross-library claims are therefore narrower than the ros-
ter implies. Second, the variance decomposition that motivates the whole attribution
commitment is itself four separate marginal one-way ANOVAs — library/framework,
training algorithm, model family and model scale — each run against the same 42 exper-
iments with valid performance observations. On a corpus where the factors are collinear
a marginal 𝜂2 absorbs the variance of each factor’s correlates, so the design reads the
decomposition only as evidence that framework and algorithm vary a great deal across
this corpus, which is what motivates holding them fixed — not as an estimate of any fac-
tor’s independent contribution, which a marginal one-way analysis on a non-orthogonal
design cannot identify. The values are reported in Chapter 6 §6.1. The historical twenty-
test multiple-comparison family that accompanies it pre-dates the Tier-A/B/C partition
and is retained as a reproducibility audit trail, explicitly not as a corrected survival claim.



4.2     Per-Step Telemetry

Attribution is only as good as the run’s observability. The intended per-step telemetry
record contains the Zero-Variance Fraction; gradient utilisation; the collapse decom-
position that separates all-correct from all-wrong zero-variance groups; and, alongside
them, reward, loss, entropy, completion length and the KL divergence to the reference
policy when one exists and that measurement is exposed; actual availability is runner-
specific. Records are written to JSONL and mirrored to Weights & Biases, so a run
can be analysed locally without a network dependency and reviewed remotely without a
local environment. The reason ZVF and its decomposition are logged as separate fields
rather than collapsed into a single starvation number is that the same aggregate fraction
can describe opposite situations: a group of completions that are all correct and a group
that are all wrong both contribute zero within-group variance, and the remedy for each is
different. Logging the decomposition preserves that distinction at the point of collection
instead of trying to recover it afterwards (Figure 4.3).
The quantity being logged is the group-relative advantage actually used by the runner:

                                                              s
                  𝑟𝑖 − 𝜇𝑔             1 Õ                         1 Õ

                  𝜎𝑔 + 𝜀              𝐺 𝑗                         𝐺 𝑗

standard deviation, with 1/G as written above. Every GRPO-style runner in Table 4.2
computes it that way. Two places use the unbiased 1/(G − 1) form instead: the refer-

                                                             61
ence zvf() of §4.3, whose ddof=1 sample variance leaves exact ties unchanged but can
change classification at a positive tolerance, and the batch-wide whitening of the PPO
arm in the same-stack run. The ZVF-definition figure (Figure 4.4) also shows the un-
biased form, which gives 𝜎𝑔 ≈ 0.535 for its example group where the population form
gives 0.5. When 𝜎𝑔 = 0 every 𝐴𝑖 is zero, so the whole group contributes no gradient
through the advantage term. Whether anything else acts depends on the executed loss:
KL regularisation and auxiliary or critic losses can remain nonzero. Clipping alone does
not create a policy-surrogate gradient when the advantage is zero. The zero-gradient
statement is therefore confined to the reward-relative surrogate in verified group-centred
implementations; it is not a claim that the whole optimiser step vanishes. Table 4.2
distinguishes verified absence, configuration evidence and unknown managed settings.
Two further design choices distinguish this telemetry from conventional training curves.
The first is that the raw per-group reward tensors are retained, not only the summaries
computed from them. This makes a published number auditable by recomputation: any
downstream diagnostic, such as p2_collapse_analysis.py, can reproduce a reported
statistic from the stored tensors rather than trusting a logged summary. The convention
has been exercised: study P2 recomputes three stored GSM8K group tensors (Qwen3-
8B sampling runs, G = 8) from their raw values rather than reading the figures back
from a log line, and the recomputed values are reported in Chapter 6 §6.3.1. The second
is that the logging layer is treated as part of the apparatus and is expected to fail. An
early Weights & Biases integration used the library’s thread-unsafe global run object
from multiple worker threads, which produced repeated “Run is finished” summary-
write failures; the fix was to give each run its own process in campaign.py. The defect is
recorded because telemetry that silently drops writes is indistinguishable from telemetry
showing a healthy run, and the remedy — process-level isolation per run — is a structural
property of the campaign rather than a patch.



4.3     Formalising the Zero-Variance Fraction

We distinguish the exact-tie fraction, ZVF0 , from the tolerance-based diagnostic, ZVF𝜀 .
The first counts groups with identical observed rewards; the second also counts near-
ties. Unless explicitly labelled exact-tie, ZVF here uses the following sample-variance
threshold. Formally, for a batch 𝐵𝑡 of prompt groups at step 𝑡, each group 𝑔 contributing
𝐾 sampled completions with rewards 𝑟 𝑔,1 , . . . , 𝑟 𝑔,𝐾 ,

                                1 Õ hc                                     i
                      ZVF𝑡 =             1 Var(𝑟 𝑔,1 , . . . , 𝑟 𝑔,𝐾 ) ≤ 𝜀
                               |𝐵𝑡 | 𝑔∈𝐵
                                        𝑡




                                              62
where Varc is the unbiased sample variance and 𝜀 = 10−6 for rewards normalised to
[0, 1]. The exact-tie formulation used in some internal sources instead writes ZVF𝑡 =
 1 Í
|𝑃𝑡 |  𝑝∈𝑃𝑡 1[Var𝑐∼𝐶 ( 𝑝) 𝑟 (𝑐) = 0], with 𝐶 ( 𝑝) the set of sampled completions for prompt
p. These formulations agree for the stored binary rewards at G = 8 and 𝜀 = 10−6 , but are
not interchangeable for perturbed or continuous rewards. In particular, the micro-jitter
test changes exact ties while leaving this tolerance-based diagnostic unchanged (§6.3.1).
The reference implementation is deliberately small, so that the diagnostic cannot acquire
hidden behaviour:

def zvf(rewards_2d: np.ndarray, eps: float = 1e-6) -> float:
    # rewards_2d shape: [num_groups, K]
    var_per_group = rewards_2d.var(axis=-1, ddof=1)
    return float((var_per_group <= eps).mean())

Gradient utilisation is then defined as its complement, GU𝑡 = 1 − ZVF𝑡 . The comple-
ment is a descriptive accounting identity, not a claim about optimiser behaviour. Its
scope must be read against the runner actually used, so the loss form of every study
is listed in Table 4.2, read from the runner code rather than from the internal reports’
prose. Three facts in the table govern how ZVF is interpreted. First, an exact-tie group
has zero reward-relative surrogate gradient only where the runner actually centres ad-
vantages within that group. Near-ties counted by ZVF𝜀 need not have exactly zero ad-
vantages. TRL’s dump records 𝛽 = 0.04 and an on-device reference model, its launcher
leaves 𝛽 unspecified, the PPO roster inherits adaptive-KL defaults, and the managed
dump has 𝛽 = null. None of these is evidence of zero executed KL; unknown remains
unknown. Even verified zero-KL runs can update through optimiser state, regularisa-
tion or auxiliary losses. Second, the inspected custom-loss Tinker reference runners
are REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline (the actor’s historical source binding is
incomplete; Appendix D): the loss is −𝐴𝑖 times the summed token log-probability, av-
eraged over completions, with no importance ratio and no clipping, and this thesis calls
them that rather than GRPO in the strict sense. Third, the runners labelled GRPO are
not one loss: the same-stack and Dr. GRPO studies use a clipped ratio with two inner
epochs, the Tinker runners do not, and the Phase-1 four-method run divides by no 𝜎 even
in its “grpo” arm. The scope must consequently be checked per row, including auxiliary
losses, masks and source-binding gaps, rather than inferred from the algorithm label
(Figures 4.4 and 4.5).
