Budget matching is defined per experiment rather than inferred from a common step
count. The primary arithmetic G sweep uses 16 prompts per step for 40 steps, so G = 2,
4, 8 and 16 require 1,280, 2,560, 5,120 and 10,240 generated completions respectively;
it is a fixed-prompt, fixed-step sweep, not a matched-token comparison. The original
PPO/GRPO comparison fixes 128 completions per step but changes prompt exposure;
the later GSM8K comparison fixes 64 completions per step with the same issue. The
separate Qwen3-8B panel fixes 2,560 rollouts while changing optimiser steps, and the
curriculum re-test explicitly fixes a 30k-token budget. Equal rollout counts do not es-
tablish equal generated tokens, wall-clock cost or optimisation opportunity. Each result
is interpreted against the budget it actually controls.
Three properties make the definition more than a restatement of the training loop. First,
it is non-tautological under a simple null: if completions were independent Bernoulli
draws with per-prompt success probability 𝑝 𝑔 , then E[ZVF𝑡 ] = E 𝑝 𝑔 [ 𝑝 𝑔𝐾 + (1 − 𝑝 𝑔 ) 𝐾 ], a
quantity that depends on the model’s accuracy distribution and the group size rather than

                                                            68
on the observed batch. The identity pass@𝐺 − 𝑝 𝐺 = 1 − ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝) follows algebraically for
the model expectations under the same i.i.d.-Bernoulli model and is therefore reported
as an algebraic remark rather than as evidence. Second, it is checked against that null
on instrumented runs: a Qwen2.5-0.5B sweep over G ∈ {2, 4, 8, 16}, three seeds and 40
steps — twelve runs and 480 logged steps — compares per-step observed ZVF with the
Bernoulli-null prediction 𝑝 𝐺 + (1− 𝑝) 𝐺 . Because the null for each step is computed from
the same step’s mean reward, a high correlation is partly mechanical, so the comparison
is designed as a shape check rather than a validation, and the baseline used is the per-
step average of the closed form (results in Chapter 6 §6.3.3). Third, at larger scale the
same instrument is run on Qwen3-8B against GSM8K test problems through the Tinker
managed API at G = 8, temperature 1.0, over 200 problems and three seeds (sampling
only, no training; these are the same three stored tensors whose ZVF P2 recomputes),
with the zero-variance groups decomposed into all-correct and all-wrong and compared
with the same null. Deviations from the null in either direction are not read as evidence
about dependence between completions.
That the quantity is portable across libraries is a separate, weaker claim, and
the project states its limits explicitly.    A normaliser at platform_modal/
scripts/zvf_ compute_ cross_ framework.py maps each framework’s re-
ward and mask fields onto the common definition — TRL’s rewards with
batch_size / group_size and completion_mask; Tinker’s rollout.reward with
rollout.group_id and rollout.eos_mask; OpenRLHF’s reward_score with
prompt_index and attention_mask; veRL’s data_source/reward with uid and
response_mask — and emits JSONL records of the form {step, zvf, batch_
reward_mean, n_groups, K}. The scope correction attached to that parser is that
it normalises reward logs and does not make its runner canonical GRPO; because the
project does not bundle raw per-group rollout JSONL for every framework, no fresh
four-way pointwise ZVF overlay is claimed, only the narrower implementation-level
portability claim. The threshold 𝜀 is fixed in advance, and its sensitivity is reported
with the P2 results in Chapter 6 §6.3.1. Boundary conditions are declared where the
diagnostic does not apply: dense or continuous reward signals, K = 1, SFT-saturated
baselines and non-outcome-reward RL all fall outside the setting in which the definition
is meaningful.
The methodological point of this section is that ZVF is a diagnostic and not a metric. The
design commits to that position before the results rather than inferring it from them, and
it takes four precautions. First, because the statistic is unsigned, a high value is read as
consistent with either a solved or an unsolved task and never as a score in one direction;
the 368-run audit that shows both extremes is reported in Chapter 7 §7.1 and §7.3. Sec-
ond, a pooled association between ZVF and performance is always broken down by task


                                            69
type before it is read, because the task mix of a pool can set the sign of the association
(results in Chapter 6 §6.3.4). Third, the historical pooled association is retained only as
an audited source result: it mixes synthetic cells and heterogeneous outcomes, includ-
ing late training reward, and cannot validate held-out prediction. Fourth, no step-level
p-value is computed on the 480 autocorrelated steps of the instrumented sweep, because
treating correlated within-run steps as independent samples would manufacture signif-
icance. One further boundary is recorded for the same reason: a variance-mitigation
table in the internal report sources is a declared simulation projection and not evidence,
and its rows are read as synthetic rather than as independent library deployments. The
result of these choices is a diagnostic that is useful for describing where within a batch
the learning signal is and is not, and that is explicitly not proposed as a quantity to be
maximised, ranked or reported as a headline score.



4.4     The Eight-Study Portfolio

Semester 3 delivered the shared code and the initial experimental foundation; the
Semester 4 programme — the research direction, the expanded experiments, the analysis
and the P1–P8 internal reports — is the present author’s solo continuation, and the roster
records 206 analysis iterations behind the numbers reported in this thesis. The portfolio
is organised as eight studies, each written up as an unpublished internal report, each
asking one question and each carrying its own claim boundary; the titles quoted below
are the internal reports’ working titles. This section states the questions and designs;
the results are in Chapters 6–7 and, for P8, Appendix F. The questions and boundaries
are stated here because they are methodological commitments: a study’s question de-
termines which comparisons it may make, and the boundary states what it declines to
conclude.
P1, “Scaling Laws for GRPO Post-Training of Language Models: A Cross-Library,
Cross-Scale Study”, asks whether GRPO post-training outcomes scale with model scale
in a way that can be fitted and extrapolated. Its evidence base is more than seventy runs
across seven RL libraries and five model families from 0.6B to 1T parameters, fitted with
saturating forms and tested for a cross-scale slope. The frontier anchors — Qwen3-
235B-MoE, DeepSeek-V3.1, Kimi-K2 and Nemotron-120B — were run through the
Tinker managed API in a single-seed, descriptive-only regime, so the report’s claim
boundary is a descriptive limits-and-identifiability audit rather than a positive scaling
law; matched multi-seed cross-scale runs are an explicit non-claim. Results are in Chap-
ter 6 §6.2.
P2, “The Zero-Variance Fraction: A Descriptive Diagnostic for Signal Starvation in


                                            70
GRPO”, asks how much of a training batch carries usable group-relative signal, and
whether that fraction predicts the final outcome. The study characterises ZVF descrip-
tively, treats the identity of §4.3 as algebraic rather than empirical, audits a historical
pooled association whose mixed synthetic membership and heterogeneous targets can-
not establish held-out prediction, and marks the variance-mitigation table as a simula-
tion projection; the retired roster item that previously carried a causal framing now lives
under archive/absorbed/R02_main_zvf/. Results are in Chapter 6 §6.3.
P3, “Group Size in GRPO: Contrast Density and the Bridge to DPO”, asks how train-
ability varies with the group size G and whether GRPO admits an all-pairs preference
interpretation. Its measured sweep covers G ∈ {2, 4, 8, 16} on the near-ceiling arithmetic
task, with seed-level equivalence tests against G = 2 and a one-way test of held-out ac-
curacy across G. No directly trained matched-budget G = 32 cell exists, so every G = 32
training-performance value in P3 comes from an explicitly labelled reconstructed token-
budget grid and is held as a hypothesis rather than a measurement. The claim boundary
is that the data may not be read as a universal optimum, and the all-pairs preference
reading resembling DPO is offered as an interpretation and not as a proven equivalence.
Results, including a conditional utility audit over 505 tasks, are in Chapter 6 §6.4.
P4, “Length Bias and Held-Out Generalization in GRPO and Dr. GRPO”, asks whether
the verbosity trap associated with GRPO appears in the measured regime and whether
either method changes held-out accuracy. Its controlled comparison runs GRPO against
Dr. GRPO on a near-ceiling arithmetic task and in a short-horizon GSM8K chain-of-
thought regime with a 200-token generation cap, alongside a multi-seed Qwen3-8B pre-
RL versus post-RL held-out control. The 200-token cap bounds the claim: under a cap
that already controls length, absence of the verbosity signature would be the expected
outcome rather than a positive finding. Results are in Chapter 6 §6.5.
P5, “Report the Stack, Not the Label: RL-for-LLM Results Are Stack-Conditioned”,
asks how much of a reported result is attributable to the stack rather than to the algo-
rithm label, and what a reportable stack must contain. Three exhibits carry the argu-
ment: a nominally matched-configuration backend audit, a same-label “DAPO” com-
parison between true dynamic sampling on an open trainer and an asymmetric-clip sur-
rogate on a closed stack, and a twelve-cell head-to-head of four labelled algorithms
on one fixed stack; their results are in Chapter 7 §7.1. The study proposes an eight-
item minimum reportable stack (seven run-manifest fields and one evaluation item) —
loss form; reference-policy and KL handling; sampler, backend and precision includ-
ing base-checkpoint identity by revision or hash; per-step ZVF and gradient-utilisation
trajectory; group-size schedule; a disjoint held-out split; decontamination plus a parser-
robustness probe; and held-out pass@k curves alongside pass@1 — and formalises an
RL-reproducibility threat model with flip-risk grades R0–R5. This is the standard this


                                            71
thesis is written to.
P6, “GRPO-Registry: A Machine-Readable Catalog of Group-Relative RL Stacks and
Their Variant Deltas”, asks whether stacks can be compared mechanically rather than
through prose. It defines stack records carrying the seven manifest fields of the MIN-
REPORT-RL standard, a per-component variant-delta status, three variant-delta records
verified against the DAPO, Dr. GRPO and GSPO papers, twenty seed stack entries (28
on disk at this revision; §7.2), and a reference CLI with a 0–100 MIN-REPORT auditor
badge and a stackdiff command that maps a pair of entries to an R0–R5 flip-risk
verdict. The study is explicitly a resource study rather than a second ranking, and it cites
P5’s seventeen-fold exhibit only to motivate the schema. Results are in Chapter 7 §7.2.
P7, “From Diagnostic to Controller: A Signal-Starvation Theory of GRPO and an Adap-
tive Group-Size Intervention”, asks whether the diagnostic of §4.3 can be turned into a
control signal. It models usable learning signal per prompt as 𝑆 = 𝑝(1 − 𝑝)(1 − ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝))
with ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝) = 𝑝 𝐺 + (1 − 𝑝) 𝐺 , yielding three testable consequences: that the expected
ZVF indicator equals 𝑝 𝐺 + (1 − 𝑝) 𝐺 ; that larger G lowers ZVF; and that gradient magni-
tude tracks p(1 − p). Each consequence is paired with a measurement that could falsify
it: the 368-run audit, a model-by-group-size grid, and a toy-scale open-backward-pass
experiment whose result is admitted as directional only. The study promotes pairwise-
contrast density, PCD = (G − 1)/G · E[p(1 − p)], as the candidate control signal, subject
to an exact-tie micro-jitter sensitivity test, which does not falsify tolerance-based ZVF,
and then audits an adaptive group-size controller against fixed recipes and DAPO-style
dynamic sampling on a four-arm open trainer and in a counterfactual controller trace.
Results are in Chapter 7 §7.3. The methodological commitment that governs this study is
stated at its head: adaptive group size is not promoted, and no result establishes prospec-
tive superiority over static G = 16, Dr. GRPO or dynamic sampling.
P8 occupies the eighth slot with a study outside the RL programme, “LLM vs. XGBoost
in Credit-Card Fraud: Sensor and Scribe, Not Scorer”. Its question is whether a large
language model should be used as the fraud scorer or as a component around one, and it
is designed as a comparison of a boosted-tree model with a Qwen3.5-4B scorer on held-
out splits, with the scorer’s cost as a triage component recorded alongside its accuracy.
Results are in Appendix F. The roster evolved after the semester-4 title list was fixed, and
the present position is recorded as it stands: the fraud study is parked outside the ZVF
thesis, contributes no RL evidence, and is retained for its measurement discipline; the
active P8 slot in the current report roster is an exploratory workshop artifact. No result
from P8 is used anywhere in this thesis to support an RL claim.




                                            72
4.5     The Design of the E1–E14 Evaluation Campaign

The evaluation campaign is the applied half of the programme and is deliberately nar-
row in a way that makes its results interpretable: it evaluates one actor against four-
teen benchmark suites that were not part of its training data under a receipt, budget
and decontamination regime, instead of training many actors and comparing them. The
single actor is the base model Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B at commit 995ad96e…, with
adapter campaign-adapter at commit withheld-adapter-revision…, in bfloat16.
How that adapter was trained is reconstructed in Chapter 8 §8.1.1. “Held-out” is used
here in a narrow sense: the actor was trained only on API-Bank-RLVR and SWE-Gym,
and none of the fourteen suites is in that training data. It does not mean that every suite
was decontamination-checked. The content check below covers LAB-Bench, Agent-
Dojo and Omni-MATH, the E11 receipt records its own verified decontamination, and
the E10 receipt itself declares heldout_claim: false. One actor fixes the object of
study: every reported per-suite figure describes the same frozen policy, so a difference
between two suites is a property of the suite and not of a model swap. The design con-
sequence is that the campaign cannot support model-comparison claims at all, and it
makes none (Figure 4.6).
Evaluators are the suites’ own scorers, used natively rather than reimplemented,
and they are pinned by revision.          The surviving native runners live under
zvf-program/flagship/, including e1_swe_bench_pro_full_eval.py, e11_ve
rilog_eval_decontamination.py, eval_pavlov_sdab.py, eval_pavlov_xla
m.py, mle_bench_eval.py and eval_apex_agents.py. The evaluation-side revi-
sions pinned for the decontamination audit are LAB-Bench 998a8e0a40cf116c80e1
b0e7a805ebb5fb9fa838, AgentDojo 089ed468cf3ed0322acc66b0211f26d9d90d
bf60 and Omni-MATH 40ba231d8f16e29ecd40e6407e2c8640145a8f62, with the
training-side envelope pinned at API-Bank-RLVR bf67c42626f02c305514b1df16dc
abc5fc616333 (597 rows, MIT) and SWE-Gym bb94ed9e39bbeb96a7fcbfb533b80
f25a7fd59cb (2,438 train rows, MIT). Using the native scorer is a substantive choice
and not a convenience: when the Omni-MATH lane produced two rows whose judged
outputs were truncated before any equivalence verdict, the native scorer’s omission of
missing-verdict rows was adjudicated as correct official behaviour rather than a parser
bug, and the lane closed with no re-judge because re-judging would deviate from the
official scorer and generate new evidence with no effect on the score.
For the training-selection protocol, sampling is frozen rather than merely recorded: tem-
perature 0.1, top-p 0.95, one sample per row, a maximum of 128 response tokens, with
the service seed fixed where the provider supports it and otherwise recorded in the re-
ceipt. Prompt and response limits are 1,200 and 128 tokens respectively, and the same

Figure 4.6: Design of the E1–E14 native-benchmark evaluation campaign. One frozen
actor is assigned to all fourteen lanes. Executed scopes use their named evaluators
and revision records; unavailable original contracts remain blocked. Each lane lists its
original-contract suite and, where one exists, its replacement scope side by side; Fig-
ure 8.1 names the replacement scope where one exists. Figures and terminal states
come from the 2026-09-19 ledger (E1_E14_FINAL_RESULTS_2026-09-19.md and
finish/Pending_Experiments.md; eleven named deterministic checks pass, scope
in App. B), updated with the six replacement scopes scored on 2026-09-27 (★; finish
_pending_2026-09-27/<lane>/result.json), which are separate receipts and are

                                                                    74
source requires the strict verifier implementation to be hashed before paid work, with a
perfect call defined as a verifier score of exactly 1.0 and no parser, target, split or verifier
change permitted mid-campaign. Freezing the sampler addresses the failure mode in
which a benchmark score quietly improves because decoding became more favourable
between runs; it also fixes the comparison between the base reference and the adapter,
which is evaluated side by side on untouched manifests. The 128-token setting belongs
to that protocol only. The E1–E14 lanes were decoded under each suite’s own settings,
which range from 1,024 to 8,192 output tokens at temperatures 0 to 0.2, and are recorded
per lane in the serving table of Chapter 8 §8.1.2. The actor’s own training run also de-
parted from the protocol in several fields (§8.1.1).
The receipt model is freeze-before-paid-work, and it is the part of the campaign design
that carries the most methodological weight. Before any paid phase a run must record
an immutable base-model revision, tokenizer revision, container or environment digest
and adapter revision; the selection slice and the disjoint holdout are re-created from a
pinned dataset revision and sealed, are write-once, are not mounted in training, and the
word “holdout” is reserved for a suite or manifest only once its split, task-ID, license,
runtime and decontamination receipts are complete. The selection slice is used for arm
decisions and is therefore unsuitable for confirmatory claims; the final evaluation mani-
fests are never used to choose an arm, alter a prompt, or re-run a failed suite with a more
favourable split. Tracking is verified before spend rather than after: an online Weights &
Biases run must be initialised and its Tinker run identifier recorded before the first paid
step, every step records loss, reward, configuration, seed, split identity and cumulative
cost, and every checkpoint is published to Hugging Face with a unique repository or
revision URL, with a failure of any of these stopping the run fail-closed. If a receipt
is missing, the phase is blocked rather than silently shortened. At handoff the protocol
requires four separate evidence classes to be reported apart: completed receipts; opera-
tional and provenance receipts; prospective gates and budget projections; and admissible
final results, the last existing only if every gate passed.
That last distinction has teeth in practice, and the campaign’s disposition record shows
it. The portfolio-level protocol defines a predeclared equal-domain macro average over
sixteen domains, a conjunctive coverage gate, an integrity gate, a global improvement
gate requiring the lower bound of a one-sided 95% interval for the portfolio difference
to exceed zero, a domain no-regression guard with a predeclared five-percentage-point
tolerance, and a safety guard bounding any violation increase by two percentage points
— and it states that a cost cap may not be met by dropping domains or suites, and that a
suite or domain which is missing, underpowered, contaminated or outside the cost cap
must be labelled incomplete or blocked, “not negative and not positive”. The portfolio
training phase was never launched, so the campaign does not report a portfolio result; the


                                              75
recorded outcome is a set of per-lane evaluations of the one adapter, and the protocol’s
own instruction that a partial subset is not a portfolio result and may not be relabelled
as one is honoured throughout.
Lane dispositions are recorded in a seven-state taxonomy rather than as pass or fail:
COMPLETE, for lanes with verified full-coverage results and no open gate (E8 public,
E10 benign, E11); COMPLETE_TERMINAL_NOTE, for a lane complete under the na-
tive protocol with a recorded terminal note (E14 replacement); CLOSED_EXTERNAL,
for lanes with no local or paid path, closed by written closure records (E3, E7, E8-
private, E10-private, E12, E14-private); PENDING_QUOTA, for lanes gated on cloud
quota resolution alone (E6, E9); AMENDMENT_ACCEPTED_LAUNCH_PENDING
(E2, E13); REBUILD_READY_LAUNCH_PENDING (E1 wave10, E5 successor27);
and CLOSED_PARTIAL (E4). Those are the states of the 19 September ledger. The
three pending states were emptied on 27 September, when the E1, E2, E5, E6, E9 and
E13 replacement scopes were run to completion (§8.4). This report assigns them COM-
PLETE on coverage, with two qualifications recorded against the label: E6 still has 108
tasks awaiting a judge, and E1’s coverage spans two contracts reported separately. The
taxonomy exists so that a lane which has not been measured is not silently absorbed into
a percentage. Carried alongside it is the project’s evidence rule, stated in the ledger of
record and repeated in every results artefact: original-contract and replacement-scope
numbers are never pooled. The rule is not cosmetic. The original-contract E1 lane and
its replacement scope are different suites with different task populations, and a table that
added them would produce a number that corresponds to no benchmark.
Decontamination is handled as a declared deterministic procedure with its own stated
limits. The check hashes complete UTF-8 fields and then normalised fields with SHA-
256; for fields of at least eight tokens it hashes each consecutive eight-token sequence
and computes exact set Jaccard similarity for every training and evaluation pair sharing
a shingle; and it tests whether an entire normalised evaluation field is a token-bounded
contiguous substring of a training field. Against the pinned training envelope, the per-
suite inventories were 1,967 LAB-Bench tasks over 13,113 text fields with 5,105 eligible
for eight-token near checks, 97 AgentDojo tasks over 71,966 fields with 9,382 eligible,
and 4,428 Omni-MATH tasks over 13,284 fields with 9,394 eligible; the count of near
pairs at or above 0.80 was zero in all three suites, and the maximum eight-gram Jac-
card similarity was 0 for LAB-Bench and AgentDojo and 0.13414634146341464 for
Omni-MATH, below the declared 0.80 threshold, with all 1,338 candidate pairs shar-
ing an eight-gram examined. Short exact matches of zero to three normalised tokens
were retained and enumerated rather than dropped — the longest examples being seven
characters in the LAB and Omni inventories and at most seventeen raw characters in
AgentDojo, such as “Paris” and “Conference Room A” — and the audit states plainly


                                            76
that these collisions do not demonstrate duplication of a task but do make an unqualified
“zero overlap” claim incorrect. The audit’s scope limits are recorded with equal care:
it does not reconstruct which training records were consumed: 512 is the size of the
training pool recorded in the E11 decontamination receipt (selected_training_row_
count), from which the 40-step run drew 80 prompts at two per step, and the transform
and consumed-row inventory that would identify them were not recorded; it establishes
a content comparison against the verified historical source envelope rather than prov-
ing global pretraining cleanliness, semantic disjointness, character-edit disjointness or
image cleanliness; ASCII normalisation loses mathematical punctuation and non-ASCII
text; and fields shorter than eight tokens fall outside the near-match and containment rule.
The audit itself is receipted: it re-verifies Weights & Biases run withheld-id against t
raining_identity_verified.json, matches the 23,733 original source-field docu-
ment hashes to the historical E11 receipt, adds 2,507 decoded nested API-Bank strings
to reach 26,240 source documents, and records 43,977,748 bytes downloaded against a
500 MiB cap, with the checker not having enumerated the roughly 2.58 billion possible
document pairs.
The campaign records incomplete execution explicitly. Some September lanes remained
blocked by infrastructure or readiness conditions and were completed on 27 September
after changes to the execution environment. The per-lane protocol deviations and results
remain separate (§8.4). Private account state, access routes and operational approvals
are omitted from the public derivative.
The campaign also carries its integrity accounting in the same register. Eleven of eleven
deterministic checks passed on 2026-09-19, covering exact divisions, receipt presence,
sealed-artifact hash equality and lost-source absence probes. A judgment layer was
added for classification, faithfulness and value-ordering questions and then partly in-
validated by an external service outage: both blocker-classification batteries returned
degenerate distributions after the incident, their receipts are retained as incident evi-
dence, and the per-lane blocker column therefore rests on document evidence and not on
model judgment. A design rule was banked from the healthy window and is followed in
this thesis: arithmetic and string lookups stay in code, and model judgment is reserved
for classification, faithfulness and value ordering, because a parity probe was answered
incorrectly even while the service was healthy.
What the campaign does not claim follows directly from the design. It does not claim
improvement over any baseline anywhere, because no lane carries a test establishing it.
The one trained-versus-base comparison is the paired small-scale rerun of Table 8.B,
with 5 to 151 identical items per lane, and its outcome is reported in Chapter 8; the
campaign’s role in this thesis is to fix the evaluation protocol, the receipt discipline and
the disposition taxonomy that any future improvement claim would have to satisfy. It


                                            77
does not claim that the fourteen suites constitute a portfolio result. It does not pool
original-contract with replacement-scope figures, and it does not average across suites.
Its one trained actor means it says nothing about model comparisons. And where the
record is incomplete — a lost execution source, an unavailable external benchmark scope
— the incompleteness is written into the ledger rather than resolved by inference.



4.6     C1 Frozen Policy Diagnostic and Reference Review

C1 completed 4,096 fixed-order screening questions with eight independently seeded
responses per question. Eligibility required all eight outputs to be parseable, uncapped
and assigned zero exact-match reward by frozen parser v2. After the full screen, the
first 64 of 87 eligible questions received 24 additional responses each. Selection was
truncated deterministically; the 23 other eligible questions were not followed up. The
primary endpoint is whether at least one of the 24 fresh responses receives parser-defined
exact-match reward one. No question was removed or relabeled after review. [S1, S13]
The new workload contains 32,768 screening responses and 1,536 fresh follow-up re-
sponses, totaling 34,304 new draws. A disjoint historical smoke cohort contributes 1,024
retained draws imported once, giving 35,328 recorded unique draws. The historical
smoke is an operational quality record, not part of the primary denominator. The 512
initial responses for the selected 64 questions are already included within screening, and
must not be counted again. [S1, S10]
The model and tokenizer were Qwen/Qwen3-8B at revision b968826d9c46dd6066d
109eabc6255188de91218, with BF16 and thinking disabled. The dataset was ope-
nai/gsm8k, main configuration, train split, revision 740312add88f781978c0658806
c59bc2815b9866. Generation used temperature 0.7, top-p 0.8, top-k 20, min-p 0, a
1,024-token generation cap and 4,096-token context. The recorded execution used one
NVIDIA A100-SXM4-80GB, vLLM 0.11.0, PyTorch 2.8.0 and Transformers 4.57.1,
with eager execution enabled and prefix caching disabled. Fixed request seeds do not
imply bitwise reproducibility across hardware or allocations. [S12, S13]
Screening had 504 parser failures and 19 cap hits among 32,768 outputs. By design the
selected initial groups had neither. Follow-up had ten parser failures and four cap hits
among 1,536 outputs. Those quality counts do not certify semantic correctness. The
primary denominator remains 64 regardless of later quality flags. Descriptive prefixes
of eight, sixteen and twenty-four fresh draws yield 9/64, 10/64 and 11/64 parser events.
They are nested observations, not independent comparisons or evidence of an optimal
rollout budget. [S1]
The Wilson interval uses a question-level binomial working model within the selected


                                           78
cohort. It is not design-based or population coverage: both outcome-dependent eligi-
bility and fixed-order truncation restrict the estimand. The observed 87/4,096 eligibility
count similarly describes this fixed screen, not benchmark-wide mathematical failure
prevalence.
The first review solved the questions before comparing reference answers, then inspected
the required model outputs. A second reference review was separately masked from the
first judgments and model outputs. Final reconciliation compared the exact question,
reference and annotations, retaining disagreements rather than forcing consensus. All
stages were assistant-assisted. Separate masking and repeat review do not provide inde-
pendent human validation or rule out shared model biases. [S2, S6]
The first output audit covers all 64 cases, all 512 initial responses and 143 required
fresh responses: 655 responses in total, including all 38 primary-success outputs. The
final mechanical coverage receipt verifies those identities and stored logic; it is not an
independent mathematical adjudication. Six initially missing reasoning-caveat fields
were clarified in a separately attributed supplement without altering the original fields,
scores or verdicts. No coverage or annotation defect remains unresolved in the final
receipt. [S7]
For the eleven event questions, reconciliation read all 88 initial outputs and all 38
primary-success fresh outputs in full. All 126 explicit final-number extractions are faith-
ful and uncapped. Two unit-sensitive non-events add sixteen inspected initial outputs.
This is not a claim to have validated reasoning in every one of the 1,536 fresh responses.
The first audit identified five frozen-v2 extraction false negatives and no extraction false
positives within its reviewed set; these selected-case observations do not establish pop-
ulation parser precision. [S2, S3, S5, S7]
The conservative witness rule requires a reference value supported in both rounds with-
out material ambiguity, eight faithful uncapped initial claims independently judged
wrong, and at least one faithful uncapped fresh primary-success claim independently
judged numerically correct. Sound final arithmetic and sound visible reasoning are
recorded separately. No post-review filtering changes the 64-question denominator.
Scientific source labels are keyed in Appendix G. The reported generation configura-
tion and manifest (S12 and S13) have unavailable exact-byte checksum bindings in this
package; their settings above are reported provenance rather than a fresh verification of
those source bytes. Operational smoke provenance (S10) is not supplied as a scientific
source in this derivative. This design is distinct from the historical V2 diagnostic.




                                            79
Chapter 5

Implementation Details

This chapter describes what was built. Its organising claim is that the engineering con-
tribution of the project is a dispatch and evidence layer rather than a training algorithm:
the algorithmic content of GRPO is inherited from the literature, but the work of making
six reinforcement-learning frameworks and six compute backends addressable through
one Tinker-style interface — and of recording the evidence needed to trace outputs to
their configuration and artefacts, while marking incomplete historical bindings — is
where the implementation effort sits. The chapter proceeds from the abstraction (the
adapter architecture), through the concrete training loop, to the two engineering episodes
that most shaped the codebase (the parallel logging defect and the Colab white-box path),
and finally to the evaluation harness and the recovery re-implementations undertaken af-
ter an execution-source loss in the final phase of the project (Figure 5.1).



5.1     Two Rosters, One Dispatch Surface

A reader approaching the project’s comparison material will encounter two different
framework rosters, and conflating them is the single most likely misreading of the im-
plementation. The internal reports’ shared methods note states the distinction explic-
itly: the cross-launcher LLM-RL scaffold referenced in the framework-gap discussion
is a seven-library roster — Tinker, TRL, SkyRL, veRL, OpenRLHF, Atropos, and HF
reference launchers — that targets LLM post-training launchers specifically, while the
cross-RL-library evidence reported elsewhere uses a different roster. Critically for this
chapter, the same note records that “only Tinker and TRL produced completed runs in
this release; veRL and OpenRLHF entries in the framework_comparison.json arte-
fact are dry-run placeholders”. The veRL and OpenRLHF entries in that artefact carry
peak and last-ten-average reward values, but they are the output of the harness resolving
a launch plan, not the output of a training run, and they must never be pooled with real

Atropos occupies a different position again. Rather than being a cell in the dispatch ma-
trix, it is integrated through a dedicated package under platform_tinker/atropos/,
a tinker_atropos module containing config.py, api.py, trainer.py, types.py,
dataset.py, stats_utils.py, and an environments/ directory with per-task envi-
ronment servers. Its integration model is the Atropos managed-server pattern: a three-
terminal workflow in which run-api starts the Atropos API, launch_training.py
--config configs/default.yaml starts Tinker training against a TINKER_API_KE
Y, and an environment server — a built-in one or any external Atropos environment
pointed at the same config file — serves rollouts. The README’s quickstart example
runs a fifty-step training run on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct against a GSM8K environment,
and the environment contract is that the env and openai sections are consumed by the
environment server while the tinker section is consumed by the trainer and ignored by
the environment. This separation is what allows an arbitrary existing Atropos environ-
ment to be reused against the Tinker backend without modification to the environment.



5.2     The Adapter Architecture: What versus Where

The dispatch surface is built on one orthogonal decomposition, stated in the back-
ends module docstring: “A Backend knows where to run one experiment cell
(local GPU, Modal, Colab, vast.ai, GCP, HF Spaces); the framework dimension
(trl/tinker/verl/openrlhf/skyrl) is the what. plan() resolves a cell into a concrete
LaunchPlan without spending compute, so --dry-run and the matrix test work
uniformly”. The LaunchPlan dataclass carries backend, framework, command,
driver_file, output, env, and notes, with a format() method for printing;
the default _execute runs subprocess.run(plan.command, shell=True, check=
True). Because plan resolution is separated from execution, the entire matrix can be
validated without a GPU, an API key, or a dollar of spend — a property that the dry-run
placeholders discussed above depend on, and which is also the reason those placeholders
must be labelled as such (Figure 5.2).
The matrix itself ships as data. platform_local/unified/matrix.json declares
schema tinkerrl-framework-backend-matrix-v1 with thirty cells — five frame-
works (trl, tinker, verl, openrlhf, skyrl) crossed with six backends (local, modal, colab,
vast, gcp, hfspaces), five framework cells per backend and six backend cells per frame-
work — and a canonical_experiment block that pins the single configuration every
cell is meant to instantiate: Qwen/Qwen3-8B on GSM8K with GRPO, thirty training
steps, num_generations 8, per-device batch size 2, gradient accumulation 8, learning

rate 1e-06, LoRA rank 16 and alpha 32 over all linear modules, beta 0.0, epsilon

naming a framework, a backend, the launch command, the driver file and an output lo-
cation; for example, the trl/local cell runs python -m platform_local.unified
--backend local --framework trl and dispatches in-process to platform_loca
l/trl_integrations/trainer.py.
The launcher implements the dispatch. UnifiedLauncher holds three registries:
FRAMEWORKS mapping skyrl, tinker, verl, openrlhf, and trl to human-readable
labels; BACKENDS mapping local, modal, colab, vast, gcp, and hfspaces to
labels; and ALGORITHMS covering grpo, ppo, reinforce, dapo, and dpo. Dispatch is
outer-on-backend and inner-on-framework, which is why the per-framework handlers
can each be written against the framework’s own idioms. _run_tinker shells to
python -m platform_tinker.tinkerrl.grpo_cli --preset <spec.task>;
_run_openrlhf delegates to platform_modal/openrlhf/trainer.py:run_
openrlhf_training({}, output_dir="./checkpoints/openrlhf"),                   which
drives    openrlhf.cli.train_ppo_ray --advantage_estimator group_norm;
_run_trl generates a resumable GRPO training script through trl_integrations
.trainer.generate_trl_train_script into a temporary directory and executes
it; _run_verl imports verl.config and verl.trainer directly; and _run_skyrl
requires a SKYRL_CHECKOUT environment variable and emits uv run --extra gpu
--extra tinker -m skyrl.tinker.api. For the local and colab backends the
launcher runs dispatch_framework() in-process, a path the code comments identify
as the one that breaks the Colab entry’s self-recursion — an implementation detail
worth recording because it indicates that the backend and framework dimensions
are not perfectly orthogonal in practice, and the launcher carries framework-specific
knowledge precisely at the points where they intersect.
Fairness across frameworks is addressed by configuration auditing rather than by as-
sertion. The platform_hybrid/experiments/framework_config_dumps/ supple-
ment ships one exact hyperparameter dump per framework for the canonical Qwen3-8B
+ GSM8K matched-configuration run, and the accompanying README recomputes the
field partition from those dumps: of 44 tracked leaf fields, 25 are identical across all four
frameworks (LoRA rank, alpha, target modules and dropout; GRPO group size, rollouts
per group and max new tokens; sampling temperature, top-p, top-k and do-sample; op-
timizer name, learning rate, weight decay, betas and epsilon; reward type; and the task,
seed and source-git-sha metadata), while 11 are Tinker-managed — serialised as null
# managed_by_tinker in the Tinker dump — covering kl.beta, kl.surrogate,
importance-sampling granularity, reference-model offload and recompute, tokens per
optimizer step, reward broadcast precision, gradient checkpointing, micro-batch size,


                                             84
gradient accumulation, and tensor-parallel size. The same README records two dis-
claimers that matter more than the identical count: model and model_sha256 are not
identical, because the Tinker run uses Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base while TRL, veRL and
OpenRLHF use the instruction-tuned Qwen/Qwen3-8B, and model_sha256 is a place-
holder rather than a verified checksum. Remaining fields are framework-specific and
documented rather than harmonised — KL beta is 0.04 for TRL and veRL against
0.02 for OpenRLHF, importance-sampling granularity is token-level for TRL and veRL
against sequence-level for OpenRLHF, and reference-model placement differs across all
four (on_device, managed, separate_process, and sharded_ray). The appendix’s
original “byte-identical” framing is retracted on the strength of this partition; the ac-
curate statement is that a majority of tracked fields are matched, a known Base-versus-
Instruct confound survives, and eleven fields are not under user control at the Tinker API
level at all.



5.3     The Tinker GRPO Loop

The reference implementation of the training loop lives in platform_tinker/tinker
rl/grpo.py and is exposed through grpo_cli.py. The loop follows the standard four-
stage shape recorded in the Phase-1 report under “The Tinker GRPO Loop”: sample a
group of G completions per prompt, compute group-relative advantages, form the loss,
and take an optimiser step. Advantages are normalised within the group to mean zero
and unit standard deviation, using the population 𝜎 (normalize_rewards, 𝜀 = 10−8 ).
The loss is not the clipped, KL-regularised surrogate of the original GRPO paper. It is
REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline,

                                  1 Õ Õ
                       𝐿(𝜃) = −       𝐴𝑖   log 𝜋𝜃 (𝑦𝑖,𝑡 | 𝑥, 𝑦𝑖,<𝑡 )
                                  𝑁 𝑖    𝑡

averaged over the N completions in the batch, with no importance ratio, no clipping, no
KL term and no reference policy. The sampler is refreshed only at checkpoint bound-
aries (every save_every steps), so rollouts between refreshes come from slightly stale
weights with no importance correction. The implementation uses the Tinker SDK’s
ServiceClient with create_lora_training_client, forward_backward_cust
om with loss_type_input="logprobs" so that per-token log-probabilities flow into
a custom loss, optim_step with AdamParams, and sample() for rollouts; resumption
uses create_training_client_from_state_with_optimizer.
The single most consequential property of this loop for the project’s research questions
is the degenerate case. When the group’s reward standard deviation 𝜎_g is zero, every


                                            85
advantage A_i is zero, and because the loss has no KL or auxiliary term, the group
contributes exactly zero gradient. This is the mechanism behind the zero-variance-
fraction diagnostic that runs through the project; it is recorded in the Phase-1 report
as the observation that motivated the collapse analysis, and it is the reason zero_loss
and zero_reward are first-class outputs of the training CLI rather than derived quanti-
ties. Zero reward is counted as an average reward equal to zero; zero loss is counted as
a step loss below 1e-6.
The CLI exposes eight presets — tooluse_synth, tooluse_baseline,
tooluse_heldout, tooluse_xlam, gsm8k, math100, pavlov_xlam, and
pavlov_portfolio — each resolving a dataset factory and a reward function
through a DATASET_FACTORIES registry and a REWARD_MAP (ExactMathReward for
GSM8K, StrictToolCallReward for pavlov_xlam, PavlovNonXLAMReward for
pavlov_portfolio). Tracking and campaign gates are enforced before a run starts,
through config.validate_tracking() and config.validate_campaign_gate
(), and the CLI refuses to launch without a TINKER_API_KEY. Each seed’s result is
printed as a structured record containing run_id, sampler, avg_first5, avg_last10,
peak_reward, zero_loss, zero_reward, heldout_reward, and reward_trace,
so that a run’s evidence is emitted at the point of execution rather than reconstructed
afterwards. The eight presets replaced an earlier collection of standalone grpo_*.py
scripts, with legacy_main retained to translate the old flag surface.



5.4     The Parallel Runner and a Real Logging Defect

Sweep execution is handled by parallel_sweep.py and campaign.py, with the run-
ner’s design shaped directly by a defect found during development. The defect is doc-
umented in the group-size sweep findings and is worth quoting in full, because it is
the clearest illustration in the project of why logging infrastructure deserves the same
scrutiny as training code:

      The threaded runner trains all G in parallel correctly, BUT
      wandb uses a thread-unsafe global run — with 4 threads calling
      wandb.init(reinit=True), the G8/G16 summary writes failed (“Run
      is finished”) after another thread finished its run. Per-step wandb.log
      mostly survived; the summary dict didn’t. Fix for next parallel batch:
      isolate each run in its own PROCESS (multiprocessing / subprocess per
      config), not a thread — or use wandb-core service mode with explicit
      per-run objects. Training itself was unaffected (all step data is in the run
      log).

                                          86
Two aspects of this record are instructive. First, the failure was silent with respect to
training: gradients were correct, steps completed, and per-step metrics were written.
Only the run summary — the aggregated dictionary from which a results table would
be built — was lost, which is precisely the class of failure that corrupts downstream
reporting while leaving the primary artefact intact. Second, the remedy adopted was
structural rather than retry-based: isolating each run in its own process removes the
shared mutable global entirely, whereas retrying the summary write would have left
the race in place. The finding also notes the alternative of wandb-core service mode
with explicit per-run objects, which would preserve in-process execution at the cost of
depending on service-mode semantics that the project chose not to rely on.
The same findings file documents the group-size sweep that exposed the defect, and
its numbers are reported here with the qualifications the source attaches to them. The
sweep ran G ∈ {2, 4, 8, 16} on Qwen3.5-4B for six steps on GSM8K at seed 0
under the W&B group p3-groupsize-sweep, with total token counts of 9,663, 19,964,
34,084, and 68,055 respectively. At G = 2 the run showed a 50% batch-level collapse
with a +0.000 gain; at G = 4 a 17% collapse with a +0.125 gain. The source flags
the G = 4 gain explicitly as supported by one example at n = 8 on a single seed and
therefore suggestive rather than proven, and records that the independent verification
of the sweep returned the verdict SUSPECT. These are not results this thesis claims;
they are the observations that motivated the group-size investigation and that the later
controlled work supersedes, and they are reported in the register in which they were
recorded.



5.5     The Colab White-Box Path (Per-Layer Gradient
        Profile)

The per-layer gradient profile — numbered P1 in the Phase-1 series, and distinct from
the P1 cross-scale study of Chapter 6 — asks a question the managed Tinker backend
cannot answer. Per-layer gradient norms require access to the model’s internals during
the backward pass, and the managed service exposes no such interface. The implementa-
tion therefore leaves the managed path entirely and runs on a Colab L4 GPU in platfor
m_hybrid/experiments/openings/p1_layer_profile.py, using Qwen2.5-1.5B-
Instruct with LoRA adapters on the q/k/v/o projections, five GRPO-style advantage-
weighted steps, and per-layer LoRA gradient norms recorded at each step. The white-box
path is thus a deliberate architectural exception: the project accepts the loss of managed-
scale training in exchange for gradient visibility, and confines the exception to a question
that only gradient visibility can address.


                                            87
The first-pass result appeared strong. Across 28 layers, the top-k layer set identified at
step 1 overlapped the final top-k set perfectly, with a measured overlap of 1.0; the top
25% of layers accounted for a 0.476 share of gradient mass; layer 0 carried a gradient
norm of 63 against 6–17 elsewhere, and a secondary mid-late band appeared at layers
17–23. A scaled re-test disciplined this result. The re-run used p1_layer_profile_sc
aled.py on Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, with 24 real GSM8K problems, ten steps, G = 4, 36
layers, and seeds {0, 1}. The stored step-1 overlap fell to 0.111, and the top-25% concen-
tration measured 0.39 with a standard deviation of 0.013. The source’s verdict is unam-
biguous: the original predictability finding “was an artifact of the toy 1.5B/hardcoded-
arithmetic setup”. A later four-seed pass at the same configuration (seeds {0, 1, 2, 3})
supersedes the two-seed figure and gives a mean overlap of 0.083 ± 0.048 (mean ± SD),
with concentration 0.39. The implementation compares the top nine of 36 layers at step
1 with the top nine by gradient norm summed over all ten steps, including step 1; it does
not compare with an independent final-step target. For two independent uniformly se-
lected nine-layer sets, expected intersection divided by nine is 9/36 = 0.25, so describing
0.111 or 0.083 as the chance expectation was incorrect. The observed overlap is below
that simple reference, without establishing a general anticorrelation or a statistically cal-
ibrated null.
A companion freeze test asked whether the layer profile could be exploited: if a small
band of layers dominates the gradient, freezing the rest should preserve the benefit at
lower cost. It did not. The full 28-layer configuration trained 2.18M parameters and
produced a mean held-out gain of +0.0625 (seed 0 +0.125, seed 1 0.0). Freezing to a hot
band comprising layer 0 plus layers 12–27 — 17 layers, 1.32M parameters, or 0.61 times
the full count — produced +0.000. The source concludes that “aggressive freezing
loses the benefit… The freeze lever does not work at this scale”, and records that the
comparison is underpowered at n = 8 held-out examples over 2 seeds. Both P1 outcomes
are negatives: the apparent structure did not survive scaling, and the intervention it
suggested did not survive testing. They are reported as findings rather than suppressed,
because a layer-importance claim that collapses under a scaled re-test is exactly the kind
of result the project’s evidence discipline exists to surface.



5.6     Software Modules

The module inventory recorded in the Phase-1 report remains the accurate map of the
codebase. The unified launcher, platform_local/unified/launcher.py, maps
one configuration onto a chosen backend; platform_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo.py
is the reference GRPO loop against the managed backend; platform_local/trl
_integrations/ and the verl/ integration hold the per-framework adapters, with

                                             88
platform_modal/openrlhf/ and platform_hybrid/skyrl/ completing the roster,
and platform_tinker/atropos/ carrying the Atropos integration described above.
Analysis code is separated from training code by intent: p2_collapse_analysis.p
y recomputes zero-variance fraction and collapse statistics from stored reward tensors,
so the diagnostic is reproducible from evidence rather than from a live run; p1_lay
er_profile.py implements the white-box gradient probe; parallel_sweep.py and
campaign.py drive sweeps; and p8_detector.py is an exploratory method-trace clas-
sifier evaluated by row-wise cross-validated AUROC.
The p8 detector deserves an explicit caveat in an implementation chapter. It is an ex-
ploratory artefact evaluated by row-wise cross-validation, and its AUROC is a measure
of separability on the traces it was given, not a benchmark result against an external
suite; it should not be read alongside the E-lane scores reported later in this thesis. Be-
yond these modules, the platform_local/unified/ package carries canonical.py
for the canonical configuration and peft_utils.py for adapter handling, and the back-
ends package implements one module per backend (local.py, modal.py, colab.py,
vast.py, gcp.py, hfspaces.py) behind the common base.py interface.



5.7     The E1–E14 Evaluation Harness

Evaluation is governed by a completion gate, zvf-program/flagship/e1_ e14
_completion_gate.py, schema e1-e14-completion-gate-v1. The gate main-
tains five evidence classes — COMPLETE_EXACT, PARTIAL_EXACT, PARTIAL_RECOVERY,
BLOCKED_EXTERNAL, and LOCAL_SETUP_READY_PROVIDER_INPUT_REQUIRED — and
maps lane source statuses onto them, with unknown states failing closed rather than de-
faulting to a classification. Its LANE_SPECS table binds each lane to a replacement scope,
a task count, and the runner or adapter that produces its evidence: E2 FrontierSWE at
17 tasks (pavlov_frontier_swe_eval_adapter.py, frontier_swe_eval.py), E3
SDAB at 80, E4 BankerToolBench at 100 (run_harbor_via_tinker_bridge.py),
E5 APEX-Agents at 480, E7 BinaryAudit at 46 (modal_non_e11_readiness.py), E9
MLE-bench at 75 (e9_mle_bench_streaming.py), E12 AppBench at 6, and lanes
E6, E8, E10, E13 and E14 with their respective runners. The gate’s own header states
its scope precisely: it neither invokes an adapter nor accepts a package as a benchmark
result (Figure 5.3).
The results the gate classifies are recorded in outputs/E1_E14_FINAL_RESULTS_20
26-09-19.md, verified by eleven named deterministic checks on 2026-09-19 (scope in
Appendix B) and bound to surviving receipts. The single Tinker-trained actor is base

The per-lane results the gate classified are reported once, in Chapter 8 (Tables 8.A–8.C),

One further result from this phase belongs in the implementation record because of what
it demonstrates about the harness. Under separate authorization, the E4 BankerTool-
Bench suite was re-run from scratch on the base model through the Modal bridge’s
/v1/responses path at --n-concurrent 2. All 100 of 100 trials completed, with
one NonZeroAgentExitCodeError, and the mean reward was 0.0 — every trial in the
zero bucket. The earlier report attributes the mechanism to agents terminating after 2–
14 steps (median about 6) without producing output in deliverables/, causing the
verifier to short-circuit to zero on a “No deliverables found” condition, and that 27 or
more trajectories ended in role-token degeneration of the form user user assistant
… repeated. This bridge path set no stop sequences, which produces exactly this degen-
eration, but the available comparison does not identify the bridge contribution to the full
zero (Section 8.5) of the cap over 3h29m, against 16.35M prompt and 2.18M comple-
tion tokens. Reporting a mean reward of 0.0 with its mechanism attached, rather than
reporting a low score and moving on, is the standard this chapter’s evidence rules are
meant to enforce.



5.8     The Bridge and Sampler Path

Runners reach a Tinker-trained model through zvf-program/flagship/moda
l_ tinker_ openai_ bridge.py, a Modal-deployed OpenAI-compatible bridge
whose own header states that “the deployed bridge is infrastructure, not a bench-
mark result. Its default paid-inference budget is zero”. The bridge declares app
name campaign-serving-bridge, model alias campaign-actor over model id
Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, and pins its sampler at [operational identifier withheld], with
the Hugging Face side pinned by commit withheld-adapter-revision. Runtime
limits are 16,384 maximum completion tokens against a 65,536-token model context,
with an 8,192-token context prefix reserved; DEFAULT_MAX_USD is Decimal("0.00"),
so every paid path must be opted into explicitly at launch. The pinned dependency set
is fastapi 0.116.1, huggingface-hub 1.27.0, tinker 0.30.0, transformers 5.5.4,
and wandb 0.21.0.
The architectural point of the bridge is that it converts a Tinker sampler into an OpenAI-


                                            91
compatible endpoint. A harness written against the OpenAI chat-completions API —
which most agentic benchmark harnesses are — can therefore be pointed at a Tinker-
trained actor without modification, and the E4 rerun described above used exactly this
path in its /v1/responses form. The same pattern appears in the actor-side configu-
ration of the E1 recovery wave, where the actor is addressed as an OpenAI-compatible
chat-completions service at http://127.0.0.1:18015/v1 (container port 8000) un-
der model name campaign-actor-bf16, with max_tokens 8192, temperature 0, seed
809, top-p 0.95, and chat_template_kwargs {"enable_thinking": false}. The
bridge and the local actor endpoint are two deployments of the same interface contract,
and keeping that contract stable across deployment modes is what allows one harness to
serve both.



5.9     Recovery Re-implementations under zvf-program/

In the final phase of the project an execution-source loss — deletion of the histori-
cal temporary execution workspace directory — removed the finish-era recovery and
build code, confined in scope to the E1 wave10 controller, the E9 restored dependen-
cies, and the E6 validator, while the replacement-lane native runners survived under
zvf-program/flagship/. The project’s lost-source policy is that sealed requests,
validation receipts and test logs pin any faithful re-implementation. Four such re-
implementations are worth describing, because together they show what the evidence
discipline looks like when it is expressed as code rather than as convention. None of
them produced a benchmark number; their value is that their safety properties are testable
offline.
The four recovery re-implementations.

The companion E2 launch_gate.py is deliberately trivial: it checks only that the


                                                            93
amendment acceptance record and the surviving CORE-Bench harness entry points ex-
ist, which keeps the gate’s claim small enough to be true.
Read together, these four artefacts are the implementation answer to a question the re-
sults chapters raise: how a project maintains evidential integrity when its execution
environment is partly lost and partly unreachable. The answer taken here is to re-derive
the controllers from the sealed requests and the surviving test records, to keep every
re-implementation offline-testable, and to make the no-retry, no-replay, hash-pinned,
fail-closed properties explicit in code so that they can be demonstrated rather than as-
serted. The cost of that discipline is visible: four controllers, three of them complete
and one a deliberate stub, produced no new benchmark numbers in this phase. That is
the intended trade.
None of the four controllers ran the lanes in the end. On 27 September 2026 a single lead
authorisation superseded the sealed-reservation and owner-binding gates they enforce,
and each lane was run with a thin runner that calls the benchmark’s native loop and
grader directly. The runners live under outputs/finish_pending_2026-09-27/<la
ne>/code/, and each lane’s result.json lists the bypass as a deviation (§8.4). These
fail-closed properties describe holding the lanes, not measuring them.




                                           94
Part III

Results




   95
Chapter 6

Results I: Core GRPO Studies (P1–P4)

This chapter reports the four core GRPO studies that make up the first half of the P1–P8
internal reports: the cross-library and cross-scale behaviour of GRPO training reward
(P1), the Zero-Variance Fraction as a diagnostic of signal starvation (P2), the effect of
group size on trainability (P3), and the length-bias and held-out-generalisation behaviour
of GRPO against its Dr. GRPO variant (P4). Each study is reported here in the form the
evidence supports, which in every case is more conservative than the hypothesis that
motivated it. Three of the four pillars return a negative or an explicitly bounded result,
and the fourth returns a diagnostic whose measured association with the outcome it is
meant to predict is weaker than the literature would suggest (Figure 6.1).



6.1     Scope, Sources and Evidence Boundary

The four pillars share one methodological commitment, which is the organising prin-
ciple of the whole project: each comparison names the intended contrast, the controls
actually held fixed and any coupled changes that limit attribution. Where checkpoint,
prompt exposure, sampling or budget differ, the result describes those executed config-
urations rather than an isolated algorithm effect. The reader should treat this chapter
as the primary result surface for P1–P4; the corresponding historical LaTeX sources
are preserved, while this chapter distinguishes their reported outputs from corrections
recomputed from the named data and inspected analysis code.
Each study draws on a different evidence base, and the differences matter. P1 pools
70+ runs across seven reinforcement-learning libraries and five model families spanning
0.6B to 1T parameters, but the frontier-scale anchors within that pool are single-seed
runs executed through a managed training and inference API, so the cross-scale anal-
ysis is descriptive and its uncertainty is wide. P2’s measured magnitudes come from
a controlled five-seed panel plus a matched-budget rollout budget check; a portion of

                                                                                       97
its comparative table is a declared simulation projection rather than a set of indepen-
dent library deployments, and that distinction is preserved below. P3’s measured sweep
covers G ∈ {2, 4, 8, 16}; every G = 32 and above number in the pillar is a joint-fit re-
construction, not a trained arm. P4’s controlled comparison is sixteen runs across two
algorithms and two tasks; its roster-level audit is drawn from a separate, larger pool that
mixes models, tasks and libraries.
The corpus-level motivation for the fixed-stack commitment (Chapter 4 §4.1) is
a variance decomposition computed as four separate marginal one-way ANOVAs,
each run against the same 42 experiments with valid performance observations: li-
brary/framework 𝜂2 = 0.546, training algorithm 𝜂2 = 0.558, model family 𝜂2 = 0.471,
model scale 𝜂2 = 0.322. These four values sum to 1.897. A set of shares that sums above
one is not a partition of a common variance budget, and on a corpus where the factors
are collinear a marginal 𝜂2 absorbs the variance of each factor’s correlates, so the val-
ues show that framework and algorithm vary a great deal across this corpus and do not
estimate either factor’s independent contribution. The historical twenty-test multiple-
comparison family that accompanies the decomposition silently includes single-seed
rows whose Welch statistics are mathematically undefined, and reduces to a single sur-
viving comparison under the restricted family; it is retained as a reproducibility audit
trail and not as a corrected survival claim.
Throughout this chapter, “statistically indistinguishable” means that the stated differ-
ence test did not detect a difference at its declared level; it does not mean demonstrated
equivalence. Equivalence is reported only when both one-sided tests reject differences
outside a stated margin. Failure to establish equivalence and failure to detect a difference
are separate outcomes, and can occur together.



6.2     P1 — Cross-Library and Cross-Scale GRPO Be-
        haviour

The question P1 sets out to answer is whether the benefit of GRPO post-training scales
with model size. The framing is deliberate: the hypothesis of interest is a Chinchilla-
style power law in parameter count N, and the P1 report’s declared claim boundary is that
it reports a descriptive limits and identifiability audit rather than a positive scaling law.
The short answer is that no reliable scaling law is recovered from the reward curves, and
that the evidence base is too small and too heterogeneous to falsify a plausible scaling
hypothesis even if one were present.




                                             98
6.2.1    The anchor pool and the fitted saturation forms

The fitted anchor pool spans roughly 2.4 orders of magnitude in parameter count. This
is a subset of the full 0.6B–1T roster: for the cross-scale fits, only anchors with a usable
per-step reward trace and a known parameter count were retained. Two properties of
that pool limit what the fits can support, and both are stated here rather than left to the
reader. First, the anchors are not measured on a common footing: five of the twelve are
interrupted runs whose recorded values are means over three to five logged steps, against
twenty to thirty for the remaining seven, so the dependent variable is a short-horizon
snapshot for part of the pool. Second, scale is collinear with training recipe across these
anchors rather than independent of it: the learning rate, LoRA rank and related settings
change together and only at the scale boundary, so a fit through the pool cannot separate
an absent scale effect from recipe changes offsetting scale changes. The null reported
below is therefore a statement about this pool as configured, and not an estimate of how
reward scales with parameter count in general. The frontier-scale anchors — Qwen3-
235B-MoE, DeepSeek-V3.1 at the 685B class, Kimi-K2 at 1T, and Nemotron-120B
— were obtained through the Tinker managed training and inference API in a single-
seed, descriptive-only regime, because this project accessed them through the managed
service rather than operating their full base weights on its own hardware.
Each trace was fitted with a saturating-exponential form 𝑅(𝑡) = 𝑅max (1 − 𝑒 −𝜆𝑡 ), with the
saturation rate 𝜆 constrained by the optimiser to the interval [0, 10]. The fitted results are
the first place the negative result becomes visible. Across the five characterised anchors,
the ordinary-least-squares trend slope on the raw reward trace, 𝛽_OLS, satisfies |𝛽_OLS|
≤ 0.0037 in every case — that is, the traces are flat to within a few thousandths of a
reward unit per step. Qwen3.5-4B fits a mean reward of 0.817 with a peak of 1.000
and 𝜆 pinned at the bound of 10.0; Qwen3-8B fits 0.285 with a peak of 0.625; Llama-
3.1-8B-Instruct fits 0.869 with a peak of 1.000; DeepSeek-V3.1 fits 0.844 with a peak of
1.000. Four of these five traces have 𝜆 pinned at the fit boundary — that is, the optimiser
wanted a faster saturation than the constraint permitted — and the P1 report’s verdict is
that for those traces no single saturation exponent is identifiable at all.
The exception is Nemotron-120B, which fits 𝑅max = 0.182 with 𝜆 = 0.99, well inside
the bound, and which the pipeline classifies as a collapse rather than a saturation. The
autopsy table records a peak at step 3, a fitted 𝑅_max
                                                  ˆ      of 0.875, a late-phase mean 𝑅_late
                                                                                     ¯
of 0.208, a drop of −0.667 from peak to late phase, a zero-reward step fraction of 0.55,
and a post-peak OLS slope of −0.0036 per step. Across the rest of the pool, the zero-
reward step fraction is at most 0.067. The Nemotron run is therefore better described
as a distinct collapse phase than as a point on a smooth trend, and this is the reason the
pillar’s headline object is a local, stack-conditioned taxonomy of endpoint ceilings rather
than a functional form in N.

                                             99
6.2.2    The cross-scale test and its nulls

Three cross-scale statistics were computed against log10 N: the slope of the mean reward,
the slope of the peak reward, and the slope of the reward variance. None is distinguish-
able from zero. The mean-reward slope is −0.024 per decade with a bootstrap mean of
−0.117 and a 95% interval of [−0.796, +0.313], Spearman 𝜌 = −0.07 (a later statisti-
cal audit, results/berkeley/adding_error_bars_summary.json, re-bootstraps
the same anchors to a slope of −0.023 and [−0.796, +0.323]; neither interval excludes
zero); the peak-reward slope is +0.037 with a bootstrap mean of −0.010 and an inter-
val of [−0.623, +0.197], 𝜌 = +0.22; the variance slope is −0.002 with an interval of
[−0.063, +0.027], 𝜌 = −0.14. A residual-resampling bootstrap with 1,000 draws ap-
plied to the Nemotron anchor returns 𝑅_max¯      = 0.310, 𝜆¯ = 3.26, P(𝜆 ≥ 9.5) = 0.277
and a t80 of 17.87 steps with an interval of [0.16, 161.4] — an interval wide enough to
contain almost any behavioural claim. A 70/30 anchor holdout returned slope estimates
of +0.0000, +0.0000, −0.0000, −0.0000 and +0.0016 across the five holdout configura-
tions. Fitting a power law over the full twelve-anchor set from 4B to 1T returns p ≥ 0.46
for every exponent tested.
Two model-selection checks were run to see whether the saturation story was at least the
best available description of the traces. Fitting constant, linear and saturating forms to
each trace, AIC and BIC select the constant model on every single trace, with a ΔAIC of
at most 2 over the runner-up. That is, a flat line describes these reward curves at least as
well as any saturating curve does. A changepoint test with a permutation null estimated
change locations 𝜏ˆ of 27, 28, 5, 15 and 18 steps across five traces, with permutation
p-values of 0.493, 0.789, 0.742, 0.909 and 0.137; none is significant. An effective-
plateau-horizon analysis at tolerance 𝜀 = 0.20 found that only two of the five traces
— Qwen3.5-4B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct — reach a horizon at all, both at 27 steps,
because the others never satisfy the tolerance.
The source reports a compute-adjusted proxy slope of +482 ± 490 per decade. Its proxy
is parameter count multiplied by estimated steps to saturation, 𝑁 𝑡 80 ; per-step token
counts were unavailable, so it is not a measured token or FLOP budget. The ± term
is described as a wide interval in the source but its precise construction is not specified
in that paragraph. With four saturation-bound traces, the source regards the proxy as
uninformative, and this thesis does not use it to make a compute-scaling claim.


6.2.3    Where the geometric template fails

The three-phase learning template used throughout the project — a slow start, a period
of rapid improvement, and a plateau, with collapse as a degenerate tail — was tested di-


                                            100
rectly against the anchor pool as an internal consistency check. Only 2 of the 12 anchors
match the template’s geometric prediction. The template itself survives as a descriptive
vocabulary: it names the failure modes the pipeline detects, and the collapse criteria
(peak ≥ 0.4, late mean below 40% of peak, zero-reward fraction ≥ 0.30) are used in the
pillar’s flagging throughout. What does not survive is the claim that these traces realise
the template as a progression.
One further contrast deserves note because it is our own measurement rather than
a synthesis. Under a same-stack comparison of PPO and GRPO on a saturated
Qwen2.5-0.5B two-digit addition task, evaluation accuracy is 0.990 for GRPO and
0.992 for PPO, and the paired algorithm difference is −0.002 with p = 0.374
(paired t on n = 5 seeds, each seed’s held-out accuracy on 200 items; source:
platform_hybrid/paper/sections/frontier_synthesis_scaling.tex; recomputed from plat-
form_hybrid/experiments/results/samestack_ppo_grpo.json). The 200 evaluation items
are fresh random draws from the training generator, not a disjoint split. The pairing is
by seed only, not by item: each arm drew its evaluation set from the shared random
stream after training, and because GRPO consumes 16 problem draws per step against
PPO’s 128, the two arms of a seed were evaluated on almost entirely different items.
Replaying the stream for the five seeds, the GRPO and PPO evaluation sets share only 6,
6, 4, 7 and 3 of 200 items, and the evaluation items that had also been drawn for training
number 12, 16, 13, 15 and 13 of 200 for GRPO against 90, 86, 80, 83 and 74 for PPO,
roughly 7% against 40% (corrected; see Table E.1; replay of platform_hybrid/exper
iments/modal/modal_samestack_ppo_grpo.py). The paired difference is therefore
a difference of per-seed accuracies on different item sets with unequal train–evaluation
overlap, not an item-paired contrast. At a 0.98–1.00 ceiling this does not change the
null reading, but it is a further reason the null cannot be read as equivalence. This is the
project’s closest de-confound: the runner, clipped surrogate and step budget are shared,
but the arms also differ in prompts per step (16 prompts × 8 completions for GRPO
against 128 distinct prompts for PPO; Table 4.2). The two estimators are statistically
indistinguishable, and because both sit at the ceiling the null cannot separate no effect
from no headroom. They are not shown to be equivalent. The five per-seed differences
are −0.005, +0.005, −0.005, 0.000 and −0.005, each one item of 200, at a 0.98–1.00
accuracy ceiling. A paired TOST with a ±0.005 margin gives p = 0.104, so equivalence
at that margin is not established, although it is at ±0.010 (p = 0.008). The exact sign-flip
permutation p on the same five pairs is 0.625. It is reported here rather than in P2 or P3
because it is the algorithmic counterpart of the P1 null — the cross-scale story is not
the only axis on which GRPO’s advantage fails to separate from its alternatives. The
project’s historical effect-size table also carries a Qwen3-8B PPO-versus-GRPO row. It
is marked a provenance conflict and not estimable, and its Welch test, recorded in the


                                            101
historical twenty-test family at p = 0.7605, is carried as a provenance record rather than
as an estimate, so no statistic is quoted for it here.


6.2.4    A withdrawn architectural contrast

An earlier version of this analysis reported a MoE-versus-dense gap across six pairs
of anchors, with a mean gap of +0.338 and a permutation p = 0.023. That claim has
been withdrawn. The defect is architectural: the Nemotron-120B anchor is a Latent-
MoE, not a dense model, so the original six-versus-six allocation misclassified it. With
the classification corrected, the same test returns a one-sided permutation p ≈ 0.178. A
leave-Nemotron-out recomputation returns a gap of +0.278 with p ≈ 0.049, and this too
is withdrawn, because removing the misclassified anchor from one side of the compar-
ison does not repair the design. The withdrawal is recorded here because the project’s
convention is that retired claims remain visible with the reason for retirement attached.
A related cross-anchor statistic is retained, with its own caveat. Across the twelve an-
chors, seven have |Δ_1T| ≤ 0.2, where Δ_1T measures how far the anchor’s endpoint
deviates from the three-phase template’s terminal prediction; the largest deviation is
Qwen3.5-27B at +0.562 and the largest negative is Nemotron at −0.50. A Cohen’s 𝜅
analysis of the categorical phase assignments is also reported in the source: across three
labelling methods, the historical output records 𝜅 = +1.000 on the five-anchor set be-
cause the helper returns 1.0 when the denominator is zero. Both methods label every
trace “plateau”, so observed and chance agreement are each 1 and Cohen’s 𝜅 = (1 −
1)/(1 − 1) is mathematically undefined; only raw agreement of 5/5 is supportable there.
On the twelve-anchor set the reported heuristic–AIC 𝜅 is +0.625, while the changepoint
comparisons lie between −0.029 and +0.077. The five-anchor result therefore estab-
lishes agreement on a constant label, not validation of a phase classifier. Neither this
agreement nor the cross-scale null identifies categorical capability structure as a better
predictor than log10 N; such a claim would require a common predictive comparison.



6.3     P2 — The Zero-Variance Fraction

The second pillar asks a mechanistic question: when GRPO stops learning, is the cause
visible in the reward statistics of the groups it samples? The diagnostic proposed is the
Zero-Variance Fraction (Figure 6.2).

Figure 6.2: Mean Zero-Variance Fraction by method in a simulation projection (a) and
