                                                                              103
6.3.1    Definition and measurement

For a prompt x sampled from the training distribution, GRPO emits G completions and
scores each with a verifier. Centring those rewards within the group produces the advan-
tage signal. If all G completions receive the same reward — all correct, or all incorrect
— every centred reward is zero and the group contributes no contrastive signal to that
step’s update. The exact-tie fraction records how often this occurs; the default sample-
variance tolerance in §4.3 also counts near-ties. They agree for the binary rewards below.
Gradient utilisation is defined descriptively as GU = 1 − ZVF.
What a zero-variance group does to the update depends on the loss form. The source
states the general case: in an objective with a KL penalty or auxiliary loss, a zero-
variance group does not make the total gradient zero, and ZVF measures only the fraction
of groups contributing zero centred reward contrast. An exact-tie group contributes
zero to a verified group-centred policy surrogate, but the full update can include KL,
critic or other auxiliary losses and optimiser state. Table 4.2 contains both nonzero-
KL configuration evidence and unknown managed settings; neither may be relabelled
zero. Near-ties counted by the 𝜀 = 10−6 diagnostic need not have exactly zero policy
gradients. ZVF therefore describes reward contrast, not measured useful compute or the
whole update.
The statistic is validated by recomputation. Three stored GSM8K group tensors (Qwen3-
8B, G = 8, sampling only, 200 problems per seed) were re-read from disk and their ZVF
recomputed from the raw per-group rewards; the recomputed values are 0.130, 0.190
and 0.155, pooled 0.1583, matching the reported values exactly. This is the mechanical
check that the quantity in the tables is the quantity the code computes. The earlier ≤0.4%
𝜀-sensitivity statement did not distinguish exact ties from thresholded variance and is
replaced here by a reproducible sweep over these same 600 groups. With independent
seeded 𝛿 ~ U(0,10−4 ) jitter, exact ties fall from 95/600 to 0/600. At sample-variance
𝜀 = 10−9 , 71/600 groups remain below threshold; at 𝜀 = 10−8 through 10−2 , including
the declared 10−6 , all 95/600 remain. Unperturbed binary rewards retain 95/600 across
this range. At G = 8, jitter alone has sample variance at most 2.86 × 10−9 , so it cannot
cross 10−6 in an originally tied group. PCD changes by +2.984 × 10−7 . The example
diagnoses exact-tie sensitivity, not failure of tolerance-based ZVF; it does not establish
robustness to larger perturbations or other reward scales.


6.3.2    Source-reported magnitudes across libraries

The synthetic sensitivity panel has nine rows at K = 8 with five simulated seeds and a
100-step rollout budget, spanning vanilla GRPO and eight named variance-mitigation


                                           104
methods. Vanilla GRPO records a mean ZVF of 0.481 against a mean last-10 accuracy of
0.379, with zero of five seeds collapsing, zero of five drifting, and five of five plateauing.
The remaining rows are: AERO 0.220 ZVF with 0.399 accuracy; CPPO 0.295 with
0.392; NGRPO 0.300 with 0.387; SCAFGRPO 0.317 with 0.409; MCGRPO 0.146 with
0.322; GIFT 0.114 with 0.326; AREAL 0.121 with 0.328; and ES 0.114 with 0.316. The
four lowest-ZVF rows each record one of five seeds drifting and four of five plateauing.
The variance-mitigation rows require a caveat that the source states explicitly and that
this chapter preserves: they are a declared simulation projection used for sensitivity anal-
ysis, and they “should be read as synthetic, not as independent real-library deployments”.
The caveat covers the whole panel. The vanilla GRPO row and its collapse/drift/plateau
counts come from the same dry-run file, experiments/results/variance_mitigat
ion.tsv, as the other eight rows, and Chapter 2 §2.3 already records that GRPO base-
line row as synthetic. None of the nine rows is a measured deployment. Anyone citing
the nine-row ordering as evidence that a named method mitigates failure would be citing
a projection.
Two further results sharpen the picture, and the first mixes measured and projected rows.
First, across 60 runs the late-phase ZVF of GRPO runs sits in the range 0.87–0.97, while
early-phase ZVF for variance-mitigation GRPO and for the largest group size sits in the
range 0.04–0.13; smaller group sizes start higher, between 0.25 and 0.61. The lag-1
autocorrelation of the ZVF trace is approximately 0.94, describing smoothness in this
mixed pool without establishing a measured early-warning capability. Of those 60 runs,
45 are the five-seed variance-mitigation projection rows above (corrected; see Table E.1).
The measured part is the remainder, which includes the 12-run group-size sweep. The
source does not report the ranges or the autocorrelation separately for the measured
subset, so these figures describe a pool that is mostly synthetic.
Second, the historical source reports ZVF crossing a warning threshold only 1 to 3
steps ahead of the plateau. This short lead from the pooled source does not establish
measured-only early-warning validity. Its proposed monitoring rule remains unvali-
dated: report ZVF alongside mean reward, and treat a rise of more than 0.3 sustained
over ten consecutive rollout steps as a warning. The project reports this limitation against
its own proposed diagnostic rather than in a footnote.


6.3.3    The all-correct wall and the mastery–incapacity aliasing

The mechanism behind large ZVF values on easy tasks is a saturation effect that the
project calls the all-correct wall. A matched-budget panel was run at 2,560 rollouts
per arm — G = 2 over 160 steps against G = 16 over 20 steps — on Qwen3-8B with
GSM8K, LoRA rank 4 and batch size 8. The late G = 2 arms reach a mean training

                                            105
reward of approximately 1.0 with ZVF between 0.75 and 1.0; the G = 16 arms hold ZVF
at or below 0.25 at the same rollout budget. The reading is that when the policy solves
the task, nearly every group becomes all-correct, nearly every centred reward is zero, and
the estimator loses its contrast even though the reward signal is at its maximum. Signal
starvation in this regime is driven by ease, not by difficulty — which is the opposite of
the intuition that motivates variance-mitigation work.
This produces the pillar’s most important structural caveat. ZVF is unsigned and sym-
metric: an all-wrong group (𝐾𝑥 = 0) and an all-correct group (𝐾𝑥 = 𝐺) score identi-
cally. The statistic therefore cannot distinguish mastery from incapacity, and this alias-
ing is a theoretical limitation on its interpretation; the historical pooled correlation does
not separately test the claimed held-out association. Under an i.i.d.-Bernoulli model
the closed form is ZVF( 𝑝 𝑥 , 𝐺) = ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝 𝑥 ) = 𝑝 𝐺                 𝐺
                                                     𝑥 + (1 − 𝑝 𝑥 ) , and it follows in one
line. If the G rewards of a group are independent Bernoulli(p) draws, the group has
zero variance exactly when all G rewards are equal. That happens when all are 1, with
probability 𝑝 𝐺 , or all are 0, with probability (1 − 𝑝) 𝐺 . The two events are disjoint, so
𝑃(zero variance) = 𝑝 𝐺 + (1 − 𝑝) 𝐺 . At 𝑝 = 21 this is ℎ𝐺 = 21−𝐺 , so ln ℎ𝐺 falls by ln 2 for
each unit increase in G. The project’s source document for this closed form and the alias-
ing analysis is headed as reasoning contributions and proposed theorems from ChatGPT
Pro Extended and Gemini Deep Think, “NOT experiments this paper ran”. That prove-
nance is kept on record, and AI assistance is declared in the front matter, but the result
does not rest on it: the derivation above is elementary and is the argument this thesis
relies on. What the project itself measured is the empirical ZVF against the closed form,
evaluated at the per-step training reward rate because ZVF is a training-batch quantity
(corrected; see Table E.1). Recomputed from the per-step training rewards in platform
_hybrid/experiments/results/groupsize_zvf_sweep.json (3 seeds × 40 steps
per arm), the per-step-average null is 0.828, 0.709, 0.597 and 0.522. The measured mean
ZVF is 0.838, 0.764, 0.691 and 0.631, so the residuals are +0.010, +0.055, +0.094 and
+0.109: measured ZVF sits above the null at every G. Gradient utilisation rises across
the same range from 0.162 to 0.369. The magnitude and interpretation of the gap depend
on the baseline; averaging over heterogeneous prompt success probabilities is part of the
formal null in §4.3. Evaluating the closed form at the pooled training rate understates
the mean of the per-step plug-in predictions, because ℎ𝐺 is convex: ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝 𝑡 ) ≤ ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝 𝑡 ).
The per-step-average plug-in baseline is 0.60 at 𝐺 = 8, rather than the 0.33 obtained at
the pooled mean. A held-out rate is a different quantity and has no corresponding order-
ing guarantee. Even the per-step baseline uses a batch-average estimate instead of the
unknown per-prompt probabilities in §4.3, so prompt-difficulty heterogeneity and esti-
mation error remain. Against the per-step-average baseline the measured ZVF at 𝐺 = 8
(0.69) exceeds the null by approximately 0.09. The change from the pooled baseline to


                                            106
the per-step baseline is the Jensen effect; it does not by itself explain the remaining 0.09
residual. The source consequently “do[es] not read it as strong evidence that the inde-
pendence null is a lower bound”. This chapter uses only the per-step-average baseline.
It is a weak consistency check on shape rather than evidence about dependence.
The formalisation’s own summary of the same sweep, twelve runs and 480 logged steps,
reports per-step observed ZVF correlating at r = 0.92 with the Bernoulli-null prediction
𝑝 𝐺 + (1 − 𝑝) 𝐺 , with mean ZVF of 0.84, 0.76, 0.69 and 0.63 at G = 2, 4, 8 and 16
respectively. That correlation is a shape check, not a validation: the null for each step
is computed from the same step’s mean reward, so a high r is partly mechanical. At
larger scale the same instrument was run on Qwen3-8B against GSM8K test problems
through the Tinker managed API at G = 8, temperature 1.0, over 200 problems and three
seeds (sampling only, no training; these are the three stored tensors of §6.3.1), yielding
a mean ZVF of 0.16 composed of 12.7% all-correct groups, 3.2% all-wrong groups and
84% mixed groups; the raw correlation with the mechanical null is approximately r =
0.93 with rank 𝜌 ≈ 0.65, and the i.i.d. null over-predicts (approximately 0.28 against
the observed 0.16). The direction of the deviation from the null is therefore opposite in
the two runs: above the null on the 0.5B sweep, below it on this Qwen3-8B sampling
run. The source does not state which baseline produced the 0.28, and neither deviation
is read as evidence about dependence between completions.
A recomputation on the three stored Qwen3-8B tensors (600 groups, pooled success
rate 0.694) separates the candidate explanations and does not reproduce the 0.28. At
the pooled rate the homogeneous binomial null is ℎ8 (0.694) = 0.054, against 0.158 ob-
served, so the null under-predicts by a factor of about three. A beta-binomial fitted to
the per-group success counts (𝛼 = 3.61, 𝛽 = 1.62, intraclass correlation 0.16) predicts
ZVF 0.170, and the homogeneous binomial is rejected against it (likelihood-ratio statis-
tic 282 on one degree of freedom). Most of the excess over the homogeneous null is
therefore prompt-difficulty heterogeneity: to a good approximation ZVF is the mean of
ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝𝑖 ) over prompts with heterogeneous success probabilities 𝑝𝑖 , which is the Jensen
argument above applied across prompts rather than across steps. A per-group plug-in
residual of +0.1224 that appears in a P2 working note is non-negative by construction
and is not evidence of dependence (recomputation: 16 October 2026 audit of result
s/tinker_gsm8k_zvf_s{42,123,456}.json). Contemporaneous work studies the
same quantity under other names — the Advantage Collapse Rate [31] and gradient
starvation in binary-reward GRPO [32], the latter proving that heterogeneous prompt
difficulty pushes the degeneracy rate above the i.i.d. Bernoulli prediction — so ZVF is
not claimed here as a new diagnostic; the contribution is its recomputation from stored
tensors and the reporting discipline around it.




                                            107
6.3.4    Prognostic value

The historical residual analysis does not establish predictive validity on measured held-
out data. It reports a naive correlation between mean ZVF and its outcome column of
r = +0.22, with n = 17 per-experiment rows. After mean reward is regressed out of
the outcome, the residual correlation with ZVF is r = +0.205 (historical p = 0.43, n
= 17 experiments). The associated intervals are not used as inferential evidence here.
These are retained as historical mixed-source calculations, not measured-only evidence.
The source’s own conclusion is that “we therefore do not treat ZVF as an incremental
predictor above reward mean”.
The source-lineage audit resolves two further defects. Of the seventeen finite cells, nine
pool variance-mitigation simulation rows. Moreover, the residual script’s outcome is
last10_avg, mixed source outcomes including late training reward, despite its held-out
wording. The remaining cells mix sampling, training and tool-use records. Residual-
ising one variable against mean reward does not turn this mixture into an independent
held-out test; it is also a semipartial calculation because ZVF itself is not residualised.
Neither this coefficient nor its historical interval supports a measured-only predictive
claim.
Pooling across task types shows why a pooled association cannot be read as a score.
Over N = 15 experiments spanning five model families, the association between ZVF
and performance is Pearson r = −0.769 (p = 0.0008) with Spearman 𝜌 = −0.784 (p =
0.0005), but it is confounded by task type: tool-use experiments reach ZVF = 100%
at approximately 0% reward while GSM8K experiments average ZVF = 8.5%. Within
GSM8K the association weakens to r = +0.40 (p = 0.09, N = 19), and then to r = +0.13
(p = 0.79) when restricted to the seven model-varying runs. The N = 19 is a separate and
larger pool of Tinker GSM8K runs, not a subset of the N = 15 pooled experiments, so the
two analyses are not nested. A statistic whose sign is set by which tasks are included in
the pool is not a score. On the instrumented runs ZVF also does not track final accuracy
(r ≈ 0.09, n = 12).
The historical source reports stronger association with catastrophic collapse than with
graded outcome. It states Spearman 𝜌 = 0.56 (Fisher 95% CI [0.19, 0.79], n = 23 pooled
cells) and point-biserial 0.62. It labels the outcome correlation as final held-out accu-
racy, 𝜌 = 0.27, and the registry’s bootstrap 95% CI is [−0.37, 0.88]; the Fisher CI is
[−0.16, 0.61], p = 0.21, n = 23. These are historical reported values, not current inferen-
tial evidence. The lineage audit identifies eighty source rows grouped into twenty-three
cells. Six cells have missing ZVF and are dropped before the rank correlation, while
the report prints the pre-drop count. Thus the displayed coefficient uses seventeen fi-
nite cells, including nine simulation cells. The outcome field is a mixture of stored


                                           108
source metrics, including late training reward; it is not a common held-out endpoint.
The claimed sample size and measured-held-out interpretation are withdrawn, as are in-
tervals justified with the pre-drop sample size. The new Colab audit preserves the source
rows, exclusions and grouping in an executable report. A three-channel risk index built
on the diagnostic — magnitude, critical slowing down via a rolling lag-1 autocorrela-
tion with window 15, and drift slope, blended 0.3/0.5/0.2 with a max-fusion variant —
was evaluated over a 52-row cross-experiment panel comprising 45 variance-mitigation
seeds, 2 tool-use collapse anchors and 5 scaling-law collapse anchors, with 11 failures
and 41 safe runs. Cross-experiment AUROCs are 0.663 for magnitude, 0.758 for crit-
ical slowing down, 0.579 for drift, 0.763 for the blended risk index and 0.929 for the
max-fusion index; within-methods, the same values fall to 0.073, 0.335, 0.293, 0.396
and 0.805 respectively. None of these AUROCs is a measurement on real training runs,
and this chapter reports them as a synthetic-panel exercise. All 45 variance-mitigation
rows come from the declared simulation projection (§6.3.2). The seven collapse anchors
enter with channel values fixed by the analysis script, not measured: mean ZVF 1.0 or an
imputed 0.95, lag-1 0.95 or 0.85, and slope 0 or 0.015. The 41 safe rows are therefore all
synthetic. The max-fusion index was selected after the per-axis results were seen. For
the 0.929 figure, a Hanley–McNeil 95% CI with 11 positives and 41 negatives is [0.82,
1.00]; that interval describes this constructed panel only. No AUROC is computed on
the measured rows alone, because they contain too few failures to estimate one. The gap
between the cross-experiment and within-method columns is a warning about the in-
dex’s transportability: most of the cross-experiment discrimination is coming from the
fact that collapse clusters by experimental family, not from a within-family predictive
signal. The declared thresholds are a risk above 0.55 to treat as failure-positive, below
0.30 as safe, and the interval between as requiring investigation.
The pillar’s own conclusion names three load-bearing limitations: the diagnostic is me-
chanically coupled to reward sparsity, group size and baseline accuracy; the reported
stronger collapse association comes from the mixed-provenance table above and has
not been established on a separately identified measured-only cohort; and the unsigned
statistic aliases mastery with incapacity. A proposed replacement, the pairwise contrast
density, is introduced in the synthesis document with a target association of 𝜌 ≳ 0.45; the
project states that this bar is not yet met by the replacements and that the head-to-head
protocol remains future work.



6.4     P3 — Group Size and Contrast Density

The third pillar studies G, which plays two roles simultaneously in GRPO: it is a
variance-reduction knob, like a Monte-Carlo baseline, and it is a preference-density

                                           109
knob, since centring rewards within a group induces implicit pairwise contrasts —
nonzero only for mixed-reward groups. Practitioners often treat larger G as strictly better
by analogy to variance reduction, which conflates the two roles and ignores cost. The
measured evidence does not justify a group-size optimum. Accuracy point estimates are
non-monotone in G, but the differences are not resolved statistically, so non-monotonic
trainability is not established (Figure 6.3).

Figure 6.3: Group size 𝐺: one single-seed sweep, one three-seed sweep and one re-
construction. (a) Qwen3-8B on GSM8K through Tinker, one seed, 30 steps: peak and
last-10 in-training reward (not held-out); 𝐺 = 8 is highest, but with no seed replica-
tion no optimum can be established. (b) Qwen2.5-0.5B on arithmetic, 3 seeds, 40 steps:
held-out accuracy and mean train reward, bars ±1.96 SE (approximate 95% CI). The
held-out apex at 𝐺 = 8 (0.990) overlaps 𝐺 = 4 (0.988) and 𝐺 = 16 (0.978); over-
lap is descriptive, not a test, and the arms are statistically indistinguishable. (c) Mean
zero-variance fraction on the same sweep, falling by 0.207 from 𝐺 = 2 to 𝐺 = 16.
(d) Reconstructed retention of 𝐺 = 4 relative to 𝐺 = 32 against token budget (dashed
line and open markers: reconstructed values, not matched-budget training runs; bars
are the file’s conditional reconstruction intervals). The point estimate reaches the Wu
et al. 0.976 retention level at 1 of 4 budgets (𝑇 = 1M), but the equivalence test at that
margin is not passed at any budget. Sources: paper/expected_results.json (a); experi-
ments/results/group_size_effect.tsv (b–d).




                                                                                110
6.4.1    The measured sweep

The primary sweep measures G ∈ {2, 4, 8, 16} on a near-ceiling two-digit addition task
with Qwen2.5-0.5B, three seeds and 40 steps, evaluated on 200 fresh draws from the
training generator. Held-out accuracy (200 fresh problems per run) traces an inverted
U with its apex at G = 8. Values are mean ± SE over n = 3 seeds: 0.9900 ± 0.0029
(SD 0.0050) at G = 8, 0.9817 ± 0.0044 (SD 0.0076) at G = 2, 0.9883 ± 0.0017 (SD
0.0029) at G = 4 and 0.9783 ± 0.0060 (SD 0.0104) at G = 16. At n = 3 seeds the arms
are statistically indistinguishable: a one-way ANOVA across the four arms gives F(3, 8)
= 1.82, p = 0.22, recomputed from platform_hybrid/experiments/results/gro
upsize_zvf_sweep.json. The minimum detectable effect at 3 versus 3 seeds is d =
3.07, so only very large differences could have been detected. A reanalysis of the same
measured sweep, using B = 4000 bootstrap resamples with analysis seed 20260702 rather
than a new training run, records G = 2 and G = 16 at 0.9817 and 0.9783, a difference
of +0.0033 (G = 2 minus G = 16) and an interval of [−0.008, +0.015]; G = 4 and G = 8
add +0.010 and +0.012 respectively, and G = 4 versus G = 32 was not measured on that
sweep.
The retention figures are the cleanest way to state the same result. G = 2 retains 100.3%
of G = 16. A later unified synthesis at the same configuration reports “retention” ratios
and signal-to-noise ratios with intervals. The “retention” is a ratio of last-10 training
reward, not of held-out accuracy, so this chapter calls it a training-reward ratio. The
point values are G = 2 1.0000, G = 4 0.9941, G = 8 0.9960 and G = 16 1.0068, with SNR
1.46, 1.76, 1.94 and 2.16. All four ratios sit above the Wu et al. [20] 97.6% reference
bar. At the seed level (paired TOST on per-seed last-10 training reward against G =
2, margin ±0.02, n = 3 seeds), the TOST p-values are 0.012 at G = 4, 0.043 at G = 8
and 0.056 at G = 16 (corrected; see Table E.1). Equivalence to G = 2 at that margin is
therefore supported, narrowly, for G = 4 and G = 8, and is not established for G = 16.
                                                                                 √
The signal-to-noise ratio grows sublinearly with G: it reaches about 52% of the G ideal,
and about 48% above a flat baseline. Reward itself is statistically flat across the sweep,
in the range 0.971–0.984. The marginal ZVF gain from G = 8 to G = 16 is 0.6906 →
0.6312, a fall of 0.059, which is smaller than the G = 2 → G = 4 fall of 0.8380 → 0.7635,
a fall of 0.075. Diminishing returns on the contrast axis are visible in the measured data,
which is what the reconstruction later formalises.
The pillar’s own framing of this regime is important and is stated in its abstract: near
the ceiling, variance is compressed, so the finding is “no detectable G effect under satu-
ration”, not evidence of equivalence on harder, unsaturated tasks. That is, the flat reten-
tion curve is a property of an easy task, and the pillar explicitly declines to generalise
it. The measured equivalence tests were run up to G = 16, and at the seed level they


                                           111
establish equivalence only up to G = 8; there is no directly trained, matched-budget G
= 32 cell in the primary sweep. The retention-versus-budget axis is where the picture
changes: R(1M) = 0.976, R(4M) = 0.833, R(16M) = 0.750 and R(64M) = 0.727, fitted
by 𝑅(𝑇) = 0.729+0.247 𝑒 −𝑇/5.76M with 𝑅 2 = 0.948 and an asymptote of 0.729. The four
points are themselves reconstructed (§6.4.2), and a three-parameter curve through four
points leaves one residual degree of freedom. The fit is therefore illustrative only, and
no confidence interval is reported for its asymptote (corrected; see Table E.1). The Wu
reference line and this reconstructed curve cross at T = 1M and diverge by 25 percentage
points at T = 64M. The operative conclusion is scoped to the 0.5B arithmetic task: no G
is distinguishable there, so G = 2 is the cheapest setting at statistically indistinguishable
accuracy. It is not a recommendation for harder tasks, and it does not conflict with the
single-seed Qwen3.5-4B observation of §6.4.3 that G = 2 under-trains with a 50% batch
collapse, which is a different model and task.


6.4.2    The same-stack four-axis audit and the reconstructed budgets

The strongest de-confound for P3 is a four-axis factorization audit of the same training
stack. On the algorithm axis, the residual effect is small: 𝜂2 = 0.023, from the same
five-seed same-stack contrast as §6.2.3. The GRPO−PPO difference is −0.002, paired
by seed only: the two arms of each seed were evaluated on different items, with PPO’s
evaluation items overlapping its training draws far more (§6.2.3; corrected; see Table
E.1). The factorization file reports a Monte-Carlo permutation p of 0.62; the exact sign-
flip p over the five pairs is 0.625, and the paired t p of 0.374 in §6.2.3 is the primary
test for this contrast. Equivalence on the algorithm axis is not established, because the
TOST at ±0.005 in §6.2.3 does not establish it (corrected; see Table E.1). On the group-
size axis the file reports 𝜂2 = 0.54 and 0.63, with Cohen’s d = −1.47 for the G = 2 →
16 comparison. The 0.54 is eta2_G_last10, a share of last-10 training reward, not
of terminal held-out accuracy; 0.63 is for gradient norm. Recomputed on the 12 sweep
runs (3 seeds per G), the training-reward share gives F(3, 8) = 3.08, p = 0.090. The
corresponding held-out share is 𝜂2 = 0.41, F(3, 8) = 1.82, p = 0.22. The source itself
grades the G-axis result only SUGGESTIVE. The supportable reading is that the group-
size axis shows a large but not statistically significant share of training-reward variance
at n = 3 seeds per arm, and that the algorithm label is not shown to matter on this stack.
A Miller-style [64] error-bars audit covered the pillar’s four headline contrasts. Two of
them are held here at hypothesis-generator status rather than reported as precise (cor-
rected; see Table E.1): the gradient-utilisation ratio at T = 1M, with a point estimate of
5.03 and no interval that n = 4 budgets can support, and the negative retention-versus-
training-token slope, which is fitted to the four reconstructed budgets of the grid below,


                                            112
                                         √
not to trained arms. The SNR-versus- G slope has an interval of [+0.148, +0.583],
which contains the ideal value of +0.500 and is therefore graded NULL. The native Wu-
style equivalence claim that G = 2 ≈ G = 16 is also graded NULL. This chapter therefore
reports none of the four P3 headlines as precise, and the pillar’s own summary is that
the data do not justify a universal optimum.
Everything at or above G = 32 in the pillar comes from an explicitly reconstructed token-
budget grid, retained only as a hypothesis generator. The reconstruction table gives, for
G = 4 versus G = 32, a T = 1M comparison of 0.41 against 0.42 with a difference of −0.01,
a 97.6% retention ratio and a “generalizes” verdict of yes, a point-estimate criterion of
retention at least 90% — the equivalence test at the 97.6% margin, with a reconstruction
interval of [0.873, 1.092] at T = 1M, is not passed at any of the four budgets; at T = 4M,
0.55 against 0.66 with −0.11, 83.3% and no; at T = 16M, 0.63 against 0.84 with −0.21,
75.0% and no; at T = 64M, 0.64 against 0.88 with −0.24, 72.7% and no. The source
labels these as conditional reconstruction intervals, not fresh-run confidence intervals.
The T = 64M accuracy row across G = 4, 8, 16, 32 and 64 reads 0.64, 0.80, 0.85, 0.88
and 0.87 — the apex moves with budget, which is precisely the pattern that makes a
universal G optimum incoherent, and precisely the pattern the project declines to claim
as measured. A fit of the critical-budget form |Δ(𝑇)| = 𝑎(1 − 𝑒 −𝑇/𝜏 ) gives â = 0.30
and 𝜏ˆ ≈ 12.1M tokens, with a 5% critical budget of 2.21M tokens and an interval of
[1.29M, 4.77M]. A difficulty-gated adaptive rule derived from this analysis reports a
mean selected group size of 7.0 and a projected 56.2% rollout saving against always
using G = 16, with 5 of 20 cells under-predicting by more than 5 percentage points.
These are projections, and the chapter marks them as such.
A separate conditional utility audit over 505 tasks, scoring each group size by (1 −
      √
ZVF)/ G, selects G = 4 over G = 5 by 0.00177 with a 95% bootstrap interval of [0.00082,
0.00270]. The margin is positive but small, and it is conditional on cohort and objective.


6.4.3    The curriculum and matched-token null results

Two interventions that P3’s design space naturally suggests were run and both returned
nulls. They are reported here rather than elsewhere because they are group-size inter-
ventions, and because their negative results are load-bearing for the chapter’s overall
argument.
The first is a difficulty curriculum that filters collapsed groups during training. The inter-
vention works as designed on its own terms: it drives the reported zero-gradient fraction
from 0.50 to 0.00 in accepted training batches. Rejected candidates still consume sam-
pling resources, so this does not eliminate wasted rollouts across the whole collection
procedure. It does so at approximately 4.81 times the sampling cost, and its held-out

                                            113
gain is identical to the baseline’s — both arms move from 0.80 to 0.85 on a held-out set
of n = 20 (one seed, eight steps), a gain of +0.05 in each case, which is one item of 20. At
a matched 30k-token budget the curriculum ties the baseline at +0.028 against +0.028,
and at five times the cost it loses. The verdict recorded is Null. An important provenance
note attaches to this result: an independent verification pass conducted on 6 July 2026
flagged the curriculum claim, along with a single-seed G = 4 “win” of +0.125, as SUS-
PECT and overclaimed, before multi-seed re-testing failed to reproduce either apparent
advantage reliably. The group-size sweet-spot result is itself a null for the same reason
— the +0.125 advantage at G = 4 rested on a single held-out example and did not survive
multi-seed re-testing, and G = 2 under-trains with a 50% batch collapse and a +0.0 gain.
The second null family concerns loss formulations intended to remove the length bias.
On the held-out metric, the standard sum formulation is nominally the best of the three
tested: +0.125 against +0.000 for mean-normalisation and +0.042 for the surprise for-
mulation. These are measured on held-out sets of n = 12–20 per arm, so each difference
corresponds to about one or two items and none is distinguishable from noise. The
length change under the standard arm is recorded as Δlen = −16. The verdict is Null. A
fourth null, on a step-1 predictive layer-freeze, is reported in the same table because it
was proposed as a cheap way to stabilise group-relative training: it achieves a per-step
overlap of 1.0 on a 1.5B hardcoded-arithmetic toy, but only 0.11 on both seeds at 3B
on real GSM8K, while the concentration statistic still holds at approximately 0.39. A
later four-seed pass at 3B supersedes that two-seed figure with a mean overlap of 0.083
± 0.048 (mean ± SD, n = 4 seeds), below the 0.25 expected overlap for independent
uniform top-nine sets among 36 layers. The target is the aggregate across all steps, in-
cluding step 1, rather than an independent final-step target (§5.5). Verdict: Null, and a
reversal of its own toy-scale positive under scaling.
Across these four checks, no reliable held-out improvement was established in the tested
configurations. The matched 30k-token comparison specifically supports the curriculum
null; the other small-sample checks do not jointly constitute a matched-cost intervention
study. These results leave the diagnostic’s prescriptive value unresolved, which is why
P2 is reported as a monitoring tool rather than a validated optimisation objective.



6.5     P4 — Length Bias and Held-Out Generalisation

The fourth pillar tests the most widely repeated specific criticism of GRPO: that it re-
wards verbosity, inflating completion length in a way that Dr. GRPO corrects. The
pillar’s headline is a scoped null, and the scope is the point (Figure 6.4).

Figure 6.4: The post-hoc peak-then-decay rule (peak before 65% of logged steps and
terminal reward below 0.90× peak) on the roster and on the controlled comparison.
(a) The four of eleven roster GRPO runs that carry the flag (single seed each; legend
gives peak→terminal reward and t/p = terminal÷peak; stars mark peaks). (b)–(d) The
controlled sixteen-run comparison, seed mean (line) and min–max across seeds (band);
dashed vertical line at 65%, dotted horizontal line at the arm’s last-10 mean. (b) Arith-
metic, Qwen2.5-0.5B, 40 steps, 5 seeds per arm: every seed peaks at 1.0 and stays there,
so the rule fires on 0 of 10 runs. (c, d) GSM8K-CoT, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, 30 steps,
3 seeds per arm: seed peaks (stars = flagged, open circle = not flagged) fall at 33–63%
of training and the last-10 mean (0.26 GRPO, 0.25 Dr. GRPO) is far below every peak,
so the rule fires on 3 of 3 GRPO and 2 of 3 Dr. GRPO runs. Completion length falls
in all sixteen runs, so the pillar’s length-bias flag (length rising while reward stays flat
or falls) is zero everywhere: the peak-then-decay rule marks noisy reward in both al-
gorithms, not length inflation. Flags recomputed from the per-step logs in drgrpo_vs_
grpo.json and drgrpo_gsm8k_cot_full.json.




                                                                                   115
6.5.1    The controlled null

Sixteen controlled runs were analysed: ten on an arithmetic task with Qwen2.5-0.5B
over 40 steps, and six on GSM8K chain-of-thought with Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct over
30 steps, split evenly between GRPO and Dr. GRPO. The arithmetic half is saturated:
evaluation accuracy is 0.987 for GRPO and 0.992 for Dr. GRPO (paired p = 0.35, five
seeds), and completions average 4.7 tokens, so its accuracy and length nulls are nulls
at a ceiling. Only the GSM8K-CoT half has headroom. At these 30–40-step horizons,
neither algorithm exhibits the verbosity-trap signature on either task: the pillar’s length-
bias flag, which fires when completion length grows while reward stays flat or falls,
is zero on every one of the sixteen runs. That flag is not the roster audit’s peak-then-
decay rule of §6.5.2. Recomputed from the per-step logs, the peak-then-decay rule (peak
before 65% of training, last-10 mean reward below 90% of peak) fires on none of the ten
arithmetic runs but on five of the six GSM8K-CoT runs, three GRPO and two Dr. GRPO,
because the reward trace on that task is noisy and peaks early; length still falls in all five.
The summary correlations are as follows, given as mean ± SD across seeds (n = 5 arith-
metic, n = 3 GSM8K-CoT). On arithmetic, the rank correlation between training step
and completion length is −0.405 ± 0.176 for GRPO and −0.307 ± 0.181 for Dr. GRPO;
on GSM8K-CoT it is −0.622 ± 0.301 and −0.355 ± 0.255. The step-versus-reward cor-
relations are +0.709 ± 0.082 and +0.753 ± 0.074 on arithmetic, and +0.046 ± 0.162 and
+0.025 ± 0.091 on GSM8K-CoT. The length-versus-reward correlations are −0.419 ±
0.155 and −0.293 ± 0.182 on arithmetic, and −0.501 ± 0.258 and −0.618 ± 0.118 on
GSM8K-CoT. Mean reward over the last ten steps is 0.988 for both algorithms on arith-
metic, and 0.265 for GRPO against 0.252 for Dr. GRPO on GSM8K-CoT. The direction
of the length trend is negative everywhere: all sixteen length slopes are negative, with
the arithmetic GRPO slope at −0.029 tokens per step and the GSM8K GRPO slope at
−0.481, and the count of runs with a positive horizon is zero. On the harder task, GRPO’s
final-ten-step mean completion length is 184.4 tokens against Dr. GRPO’s 189.3 — a
difference of +2.7% in the direction opposite to the critique.
The pillar’s abstract states the scope of this null explicitly. The claim is bounded by the
200-token cap used in that regime, and the summary sentence is that “the verbosity-trap
signature is absent in this regime”. The pillar is not claiming that GRPO never inflates
length; it is claiming that within a capped, short-horizon regime it does not, and it names
the length-confounded regime in which a Dr. GRPO advantage should appear as future
work.




                                             116
6.5.2    The roster-level verbosity-trap audit

Set against the controlled null is a descriptive audit over the whole experiment roster, and
here the picture differs. The audit defines four statistics per trace with at least five steps:
an instability index 𝜎(r_tail)/|𝜇(r_tail)| over the final 50% of training; a monotonicity
score, the fraction of consecutive step-pairs with strictly improving reward; a regression
severity, the largest normalised drop from peak; and a binary verbosity-trap flag, set
when the peak occurs before 65% of training and the terminal reward falls below 90%
of peak.
On that definition, the eleven GRPO runs in the roster have a mean instability index
of 0.267 ± 0.335, against 0.785 ± 0.297 (mean ± spread across runs; the source does
not label the ±, and its size relative to the mean implies an SD) for the seventeen PPO-
labelled runs. Four of the eleven GRPO runs, 36% (Wilson 95% CI [15%, 65%], one run
per configuration), carry the flag on this definition, including both Qwen3-8B runs and
the Nemotron-120B run. That count is reported here as a description of the roster, not
as evidence of length bias, because the flag does not isolate the behaviour it is named
for. Three properties of the rule prevent the stronger reading. Its thresholds are post
hoc: neither the 65% peak position nor the 0.90 terminal-to-peak ratio is pre-registered
anywhere in the repository, and the rule cannot be traced to a commit that predates the
traces it judges. It is not specific to length: the metric it tests is in-training reward, and
the project’s own source reads the flag as “a generic instability detector” rather than a
length measure, so a run can carry it without any change in response length. And its base
rate is high: applied to the wider trace pool rather than this eleven-run subset, the rule
fires on the majority of runs — including PPO-labelled runs and the Dr. GRPO control
in the one matched comparison — so 36% sits at or below the rate the rule produces on
traces with no decay at all. The project’s own length data point the other way: under
the 200-token cap, length drifts from 194.4 to 183.5 tokens, i.e. shorter. The run-level
audit therefore does not establish length inflation, and the pillar’s negative finding rests
on the controlled comparison below rather than on this roster count. The Nemotron run
records the highest instability index in the pool at 1.194, with a regression severity of
1.0 and a terminal-to-peak reward ratio of 0.57. The two DeepSeek-V3.1 GRPO runs,
by contrast, show markedly lower instability, with a mean of 0.143. The PPO rate of
12/17 has a Wilson 95% CI of [47%, 87%]. These are descriptive uncertainty intervals
for heterogeneous roster counts; their overlap is not a formal test of a rate difference, and
the pool composition prevents an algorithm-level stability comparison.
Two caveats on that comparison must travel with it. First, the PPO pool is dominated by
classic-RL library runs (SB3, CleanRL, Tianshou) that failed to train at all, with last-10
rewards below 5%; a near-zero reward trajectory mechanically produces a high instabil-
ity index. Restricted to LLM-native PPO, the PPO Llama instability is 0.105, lower than

                                             117
GRPO Llama’s 0.228, and the source therefore explicitly declines to claim that GRPO is
more or less stable than PPO at the algorithmic level. Second, the audit’s reward metric
is the in-training reward, not a held-out one, and the audit pool is single-seed per config-
uration: each Tinker configuration was run once and is reported descriptively. The only
multi-seed matched base-versus-RL control on Qwen3-8B is the five-seed comparison
discussed below.
An earlier draft of this report’s summary attributed to P4 the claim that Dr. GRPO is
the only algorithm without the verbosity-trap pattern. That claim could not be located
in any P4 source. What the sources state is that 36% of the audit’s GRPO runs carry the
flag; the audit does not report a per-algorithm flag rate for Dr. GRPO, and in the con-
trolled sixteen-run comparison the length-bias flag is zero for both algorithms while the
peak-then-decay rule fires on both (three of three GRPO and two of three Dr. GRPO
runs on GSM8K-CoT). The finding recorded here is therefore the sourced one: the
peak-then-decay pattern is not specific to GRPO, it appears in both arms of the con-
trolled comparison without any length inflation, and the project makes no claim that
Dr. GRPO is uniquely free of it. The narrowest related statement the sources do support
is a length-drift one — that Dr. GRPO is the only algorithm whose length keeps drifting
upward after the initial compression phase — which is a different statistic and is not the
verbosity-trap flag.


6.5.3    Elevated analyses

Because the controlled 30–40-step null is a null, the pillar runs a sequence of progres-
sively more sensitive analyses to establish whether the trap is present but invisible at that
horizon. Four of these deserve report, and all four are exploratory. They were run across
iterations 12, 20 and 24 of the analysis log after the controlled null was known. The seed
counts are small: n = 5 arithmetic and n = 3 GSM8K seeds, and three seeds admit only
10 distinct bootstrap resamples. Several of the intervals resample autocorrelated steps
or windows within runs rather than seeds. The p-values below are uncorrected unless
stated, and roughly fifteen tests are reported across the three iterations.
A sliding-window trap-onset analysis with window W = 10 relaxes the flag from “peak
before 65% of training” to “local peak followed by decay within a window”, and finds
traps in 2 of 10 arithmetic runs (20%), 4 of 6 GSM8K runs (67%) and 6 of 16 runs
overall (37.5%) — every one of which is a local re-expansion rather than a terminal
divergence. This is the analysis that most nearly matches the roster-level trap rate. It is a
point estimate of trap prevalence in the controlled data, not an upper bound (corrected;
see Table E.1). The Wilson 95% CIs are [5.7%, 51%] for 2/10, [30%, 90%] for 4/6 and
[18.5%, 61%] for 6/16, with runs as the unit.


                                            118
A paired bootstrap on the Dr. GRPO minus GRPO difference in rank correlations gives,
on arithmetic, Δ𝜌(step, len) = +0.098 with an interval of [+0.073, +0.119] — exclud-
ing zero, which suggests that Dr. GRPO compresses length relative to GRPO on the
easy task (seed-paired bootstrap, n = 5); Δ𝜌(step, reward) = +0.043 with an interval of
[−0.024, +0.112], which is not significant; and Δ𝜌(len, reward) = +0.126 with an inter-
val of [+0.045, +0.201], again excluding zero. On GSM8K the same three quantities are
+0.267 [+0.164, +0.419], −0.020 [−0.069, +0.065] and −0.117 [−0.265, +0.015]. The
exploratory reading is that Dr. GRPO’s length attenuation is task-dependent, and that on
the harder task it deepens rather than weakens the length–reward coupling.
A decile-binned coupling analysis on GSM8K shows the mechanism directly: comple-
tions in the first length decile, spanning 153–180 tokens, earn a mean reward of 0.351,
while completions in the eighth decile, spanning 194–195 tokens, earn 0.233. Longer
completions are rewarded less, which is the opposite of the verbosity-trap prediction.
A 100-step cross-validation on the arithmetic metrics file returns 𝜌(step, len) = +0.41
and 𝜌(step, reward) = +0.27, but the absolute length range across that run is 0.08 tokens
against a cap of 5 tokens — a positive correlation over a range too small to be a length
effect.
The length–ZVF coupling analysis at iteration 12 is the most mechanistically interesting
of the elevated results, because its sign flips between tasks. On arithmetic, 𝜌(len, zvf)
is −0.449 [−0.584, −0.324] for GRPO and −0.291 [−0.428, −0.139] for Dr. GRPO; on
GSM8K it is +0.286 [+0.231, +0.348] and +0.187 [+0.148, +0.225]. The Dr. GRPO
minus GRPO difference is +0.158 [+0.075, +0.239] on arithmetic and −0.099 [−0.132,
−0.041] on GSM8K. Conditioning ZVF on length bins makes the arithmetic structure
concrete: the shortest bin has ZVF 0.92 and the longest 0.62, a drop of −0.30 [−0.34,
−0.26], whereas on GSM8K the corresponding values are 0.30 and 0.41, a rise of +0.11
[+0.10, +0.13]. On the easy task, shorter completions are more often all-same-reward;
on the hard task, longer ones are.
The mechanism analysis at iteration 20 fits, per run, a regression of step-level mean
length on step-level mean reward. On arithmetic, Dr. GRPO reduces the slope magni-
tude: GRPO −3.65 ± 0.08 against Dr. GRPO −3.22 ± 0.15 (mean ± SD over 5 seeds).
The paired differences below are recomputed from platform_hybrid/experiment
s/results/length_bias_mechanism_per_run.tsv as Dr. GRPO minus GRPO,
paired by seed, n = 5 (corrected; see Table E.1). Each is given with a paired-t 95% CI,
the paired-t p and the exact sign-flip permutation p; the permutation p cannot fall below
0.0625 at n = 5. The OLS slope difference is +0.43 [+0.18, +0.68], paired t p = 0.009,
permutation p = 0.0625. The Theil–Sen slope difference is +1.11 [+0.33, +1.88], p =
0.017, permutation p = 0.0625. The 𝜌(L, R) difference is +0.10 [−0.02, +0.22], p =
0.080. The lag-1 autocorrelation is essentially unchanged, −0.01 [−0.27, +0.26], p =


                                          119
0.94. On GSM8K (n = 3) the OLS difference reverses sign, −4.41 [−37.7, +28.9], p
= 0.63, far too wide to interpret. Across the fourteen exploratory tests of iterations 20
and 24, Benjamini–Hochberg correction leaves the arithmetic OLS and Theil–Sen dif-
ferences at q = 0.116 each, the smallest q in the family, so no test in it survives correction
(corrected; see Table E.1). The historical script also labels coefficient-product ratios of
42% and 55% on arithmetic and 28% and 12% on GSM8K as “proportion mediated”.
These are not supportable causal mediation estimates. The script multiplies a marginal
correlation by a partial correlation and divides by another correlation; its “direct” quan-
tity is also a partial correlation, so the terms are not common-scale path coefficients and
need not add to the total. The same-step observational reward, length and ZVF series
also do not establish temporal or causal identification. The four percentages are retained
only to identify the historical output, with the mediation interpretation withdrawn.
The drift analysis at iteration 24 closes the sequence. Windowed 𝜌 drift on arithmetic is
+0.326 for GRPO with p = 0.035, decomposing into an early −0.330 and a late −0.003,
against +0.187 for Dr. GRPO with p = 0.399; on GSM8K the corresponding values are
+0.019 with p = 0.905 and −0.084 with p = 0.769. The sign-flip analysis of late windows
(w = 10) gives mean late non-negative fractions of 0.527 and 0.382 for arithmetic GRPO
and Dr. GRPO and 0.048 and 0.000 for GSM8K GRPO and Dr. GRPO. Tested against
one half with an exact two-sided sign-flip permutation over seeds, the p-values are 0.688,
0.625, 0.250 and 0.250 (corrected; see Table E.1). The unit is the seed, not the within-
run window: each seed contributes one fraction, so n = 5 on arithmetic and n = 3 on
GSM8K, where 0.250 is the smallest p the test can return. The GSM8K Dr. GRPO
cell, in which not a single late window flips sign on any of the three seeds, therefore
reaches only that floor. The source’s p < 0.001 for it was a one-sample t-test on three
identical values, whose zero variance gives t = −∞. Recomputed over the same fourteen
exploratory tests, Benjamini–Hochberg leaves no test below 0.05: the smallest q is 0.116
(the arithmetic OLS and Theil–Sen differences), and it stays 0.116 for every fourteen-test
selection from the sixteen iteration-20 and iteration-24 tests (platform_modal/scrip
ts/length_bias_iter24.py; platform_hybrid/experiments/results/length
_bias_iter24_signflip.tsv). The iteration-24 drift p-values above are unchanged.
The iteration-20 and iteration-24 analyses are therefore exploratory throughout, with no
multiplicity-surviving result.


6.5.4    Held-out generalisation and the scale extension

The pillar’s held-out results are the part of the chapter closest to a conventional capabil-
ity claim, and they are reported with corresponding care. The only multi-seed matched
base-versus-RL control on Qwen3-8B is a five-seed comparison on Qwen3-8B-Instruct,


                                            120
which moves from 82.0% to 83.3% with p = 0.26. Stated as a mean improvement over the
pre-RL checkpoint, the gain is +0.013 with a paired p = 0.256 at n = 5 — small and not
statistically significant, and a matched pre-RL control for the Qwen3-8B-Base check-
point is not available. In summary, on this near-ceiling configuration, GRPO does not
produce a held-out improvement that can be separated from noise at the available seed
budget, and that GRPO and Dr. GRPO produce held-out gains that are indistinguishable
from each other at that budget.
The lower-accuracy configuration behaves differently and more conventionally. On
Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct with GSM8K-CoT, both algorithms do produce significant pre-
to-post held-out gains: +6.2 percentage points for GRPO and +5.0 for Dr. GRPO (n
= 3 seeds × 200 held-out items per arm). The unit of analysis is the seed. A one-
sample t on the three per-seed deltas gives p = 0.0051 for GRPO (deltas +0.055, +0.060,
+0.070) and p = 0.0099 for Dr. GRPO (+0.045, +0.060, +0.045). As supporting de-
tail, per-seed McNemar exact p-values are 0.071, 0.036 and 0.016 for GRPO and 0.122,
0.017 and 0.122 for Dr. GRPO; no pooled McNemar p-value is used, because the same
200 items recur across seeds (corrected; see Table E.1). The McNemar detail also
overstates what is attributable to training, because the greedy pre-RL evaluation is not
deterministic: the six runs evaluate the same base model on the same 200 items be-
fore any training, yet their stored pre-RL correctness vectors take two distinct forms,
40/200 and 41/200, which disagree on 7 of the 200 items, including the GRPO and
Dr. GRPO runs of the same seed (recomputed from platform_hybrid/experime
nts/results/drgrpo_gsm8k_cot_full.json). About 3.5% of items can there-
fore flip with no training at all, so some discordant pairs arise without training. The
one-item difference in total accuracy between the two observed vectors does not bound
the per-run measurement error at ±1 item. Seed-level t-tests summarise the variabil-
ity present in these runs, but without repeated unchanged-checkpoint evaluations they
cannot separate training-seed variation from evaluation noise or guarantee that the lat-
ter is adequately represented; the per-seed McNemar p-values remain supporting de-
tail only. The two algorithms’ gains do not differ (paired by seed, p = 0.25) (recom-
puted from platform_hybrid/experiments/results/drgrpo_gsm8k_cot.json; source: plat-
form_hybrid/paper/sections/p4_results_intro.tex). This is the project’s clearest evidence
that the pipeline trains something real; it is reported alongside the near-ceiling null pre-
cisely because the contrast between the two configurations is what the pillar’s bounded-
null interpretation rests on. Across the same comparison, the pooled mean completion
length drifts from 194.4 to 183.5 tokens, that is downward.
A scale extension tests whether the null survives when the cap is lifted. Qwen3-8B with
LoRA rank 4 was run on GSM8K for 30 steps at G = 8 and batch size 4, three seeds
per algorithm, with the completion cap raised fivefold to 1,024 tokens. All six arms


                                            121
compress: GRPO moves 1004 → 905, 981 → 944 and 996 → 900 tokens, and Dr. GRPO
moves 999 → 931, 972 → 902 and 1000 → 878 — declines of between 3.8% and 12.2%
in every arm. Late ZVF is 0.45, 0.70 and 0.72 for Dr. GRPO against 0.47, 0.47 and
0.55 for GRPO, and step-0 completions are approximately 98% of the cap. The step-0
saturation is the caveat that limits this extension: with completions already at 98% of the
ceiling at the first step, the run starts truncated, and length compression under a binding
cap is not the same phenomenon as length control in an uncapped regime. The pillar
reports the extension as consistent with the null, not as an independent confirmation of
it.
Finally, the P4-facing headline in the Miller-style error-bars audit is the iteration-136
arithmetic late-training-efficiency contrast. Late efficiency is training-reward gain per
unit of length change. The contrast is Dr. GRPO minus GRPO, paired by seed, n = 5,
with mean difference +0.020. Recomputed from length_bias_iter136_step_coup
ling.tsv, the effect size is 𝑑 𝑧 = +2.68, the mean paired difference divided by the SD of
the differences. The exact one-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank p is 0.031, which is 1/32, its
floor at n = 5 (corrected; see Table E.1). The paired t that matches 𝑑 𝑧 gives t(4) = 5.96,
two-sided p = 0.004. The two-sided exact sign-flip p is 0.0625, again the n = 5 floor:
all five pairs point the same way, yet the non-parametric test cannot reach 0.05 two-
sided. The contrast was one of eight directional hypotheses in the iteration-136 family
(four per task). Under Holm correction across the eight, the family’s own Wilcoxon p
becomes 0.25. This chapter therefore grades it suggestive, not decisive (corrected; see
Table E.1): a consistent direction across five seeds on a training metric, with two-sided
non-parametric p = 0.0625 at n = 5. The audit’s three NULL verdicts fall on Pillar-1
and Pillar-3 headlines rather than on this one. The pillar also records its own limita-
tions plainly: a single-benchmark held-out evaluation, a single-seed top-10-checkpoint
frontier comparison, weak length identifiability, and marginal paired tests.


6.5.5    Same-stack rerun on the unsaturated configuration

The same-stack PPO/GRPO and group-size nulls of §6.2.3 and §6.4.1 sit at evaluation
accuracy near 0.99, where a null cannot separate no effect from no headroom. To test
the contrasts where headroom exists, they were rerun on 2 October 2026 (v1) on the con-
figuration of §6.5.4: Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, GSM8K with a boxed-answer correctness
reward and a 200-token chain-of-thought cap, LoRA rank 16 on the attention projec-
tions, learning rate 1 × 10−5 , 30 steps, two inner epochs of the clipped surrogate with
per-response length normalisation, on one A10G per run. Three arms share that stack
and a fixed count of 64 generations per step, but differ in advantage estimation, critic
training and distinct-prompt exposure: GRPO with G = 8 (8 prompts × 8 completions),


                                           122
GRPO with G = 2 (32 × 2), and PPO with a learned value head on the prompt-end hidden
state (64 × 1, value loss 0.5 × MSE, batch-normalised advantage). Each run evaluates
the same 200 GSM8K test items greedily before and after training, so the evaluation set
is disjoint from the training split. Five paired seeds were run per arm; the unit is the
seed, and the analysis, including a ±2-point equivalence margin, was fixed in the script
before launch.
Same-stack rerun v1 (superseded by Table 6.2) on Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct GSM8K-
CoT, five paired seeds, 200 test items, 200-token cap.

In v1 both GRPO arms improve on every seed, which reproduces the +6.2-point GRPO
gain of §6.5.4 at a similar size. The PPO arm did not learn: its training reward drifts
down and its post-training accuracy (0.195) is below its starting point (0.200). After
training, GRPO at G = 8 exceeded PPO by +0.050 [+0.015, +0.085] (paired 𝑡, p = 0.016);
the difference is positive for all five seeds, which puts the exact two-sided sign-flip p at
its n = 5 floor of 0.0625, and Holm adjustment of the sign-flip p across the three v1
contrasts gives 0.19. GRPO at G = 8 and at G = 2 differ by −0.005 [−0.038, +0.028] (p
= 0.69); that interval is wider than the ±0.02 margin, so equivalence is not established
either (TOST p = 0.13). This v1 PPO contrast is superseded by the v2 rerun below and
is not a finding of this thesis.
Two design features of v1 prevent its PPO contrast from being read as an estimator com-
parison. First, the 200-token cap was binding: mean training completions are 190.16
(G8), 191.62 (G2) and 194.16 (PPO) tokens under cap 200, with PPO last-ten length
196.19, and the starting model scores only 0.200 under this cap, so the v1 gains largely
measure learning to finish within the budget. Second, the critic was cold: a randomly
initialised linear value head trained for 30 steps at the policy’s learning rate, with no
warm-up, no generalised advantage estimation and no tuning, whose initial value loss
of 0.5–13 sat far above the ~0.08 floor, so PPO’s advantages were close to noise. The
64-prompt PPO batch also saw eight times as many distinct prompts per step as GRPO
at G = 8. Fixed generation counts are not measured equality of generated tokens or total
compute.
v2 rerun (supersedes the v1 PPO contrast).                                     A second version (v2; plat-

configuration, policy learning rate, 30 steps, 64 generations per step and the clipped sur-
rogate, and changes four things: the generation cap is 512 tokens with per-completion
truncation logged; the PPO critic is a zero-initialised linear probe on the detached
prompt-end hidden state with its own optimiser (learning rate 10−3 ), four warm-up roll-
out batches from the initial policy before any policy update, and a baseline for each
batch predicted before the probe sees that batch’s rewards; the reward scores only the
last \boxed{} answer, numerically normalised; and evaluation uses the full 1,319-item
GSM8K test split, greedy, with the completions stored. An independent re-grade of the
stored completions agrees with the stored scores on every item (0 disagreements over

writing seven of ten PPO seeds had completed, and the pre-specified exposure-matched
