With the cap and critic fixed, all three arms gain 3.6–4.4 points from a base accuracy
of 0.654, and PPO learns. The GRPO G8 − PPO difference shrinks from v1’s +0.050
to +0.0076; its interval includes zero, and a TOST at the pre-specified ±0.02 margin
establishes equivalence at that margin (p = 0.016). The minimum detectable difference
at n = 7 seeds is about 0.015 (80% power, 𝛼 = 0.05), so v2 could have detected a v1-
sized effect, and a true difference of 0.05 is rejected (p ≈ 4 × 10−5 ). GRPO G8 − G2
is +0.0079 with an unadjusted interval that just excludes zero; after Holm adjustment
it is not significant (p = 0.14). The supportable conclusion is that once the token cap
and critic are fixed, this configuration shows no detectable GRPO advantage over PPO.

                                                             124
Two qualifications remain: prompt exposure still differs between GRPO G8 and PPO
until the exposure-matched arm completes, and the result is one model, one task and
one 30-step budget.



6.6     Cross-Pillar Observations and Limitations

Three observations span the four pillars and are worth stating together, because the chap-
ter’s overall contribution is better captured by their pattern than by any single number.
First, the project’s strongest results are its de-confounds, and its de-confounds mostly
return nulls. The matched same-stack PPO-versus-GRPO contrast returns a seed-paired
difference of −0.002 with p = 0.374 (n = 5 seeds, arms evaluated on largely different
items, at a near-ceiling accuracy, not shown equivalent by TOST at ±0.005); the four-
axis factorization audit attributes 𝜂2 = 0.023 to the algorithm axis and 𝜂2 = 0.54 of last-10
training-reward variance to the group-size axis (F(3, 8) = 3.08, p = 0.09, n = 12 runs); the
controlled length-bias comparison returns zero length-bias flags across sixteen runs. The
arithmetic PPO contrast retains prompt-exposure and evaluation-overlap confounders,
while the primary G sweep fixes prompts and steps rather than completion or token
counts (§4.3). Those two contrasts use saturated 0.5B arithmetic at evaluation accuracy
0.98–0.99. The sixteen-run length comparison additionally includes six GSM8K-CoT
runs, whose 200-token cap binds a different limitation (§6.5.1). These designs found no
detectable advantage on their respective contrasts, with the stated confounds and limits.
The group-size axis carries the largest variance share, but at n = 3 seeds per arm it
does not reach significance on either training reward or held-out accuracy. It is also
a hyperparameter rather than an algorithm choice. The chapter’s empirical conclusion
is therefore conditional on saturation: on a 0.5B arithmetic task at evaluation accuracy
near 0.99, no algorithm-label effect was detectable, which cannot distinguish “no effect”
from “no headroom”; the sampling configuration is the more plausible lever but was not
shown to matter at this seed budget. Rerun on the unsaturated Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-
CoT configuration (§6.5.5), the first version’s +0.050 gap for GRPO at G = 8 over a
cold-critic PPO under a binding 200-token cap did not survive the corrected v2 rerun
(512-token cap, warm-started critic, 1,319 test items): GRPO G8 − PPO is +0.0076
[−0.0034, +0.0185], equivalent within ±0.02 (Table 6.2, provisional), and G8 − G2 is
not significant after Holm adjustment.
Second, the diagnostics and interventions are less prescriptive than their motivating lit-
erature suggests, and the project says so. ZVF reproducibly counts homogeneous or
near-homogeneous reward groups under its declared definition. The historically re-
ported catastrophic-collapse association (𝜌 = 0.56, 95% CI [0.19, 0.79], n = 23 cells)


                                            125
and nominal graded-outcome association (𝜌 = 0.27, 95% CI [−0.37, 0.88], historical n
= 23) come from a mixed-source table and are withdrawn as evidence; they are retained
only as identified historical values. Both calculations drop six missing-ZVF cells, leav-
ing seventeen finite cells, nine synthetic, and mixes outcome definitions (§6.3.4); they
cannot establish diagnostic reliability on measured runs. Independently of those corre-
lations, the definition is unsigned and cannot distinguish mastery from incapacity. The
difficulty curriculum, group-size sweet spot, length-bias loss formulations and step-1
layer freeze establish no reliable held-out advantage in their respective checks; only the
explicitly matched-token comparisons support a matched-cost reading (§6.4.3), and two
of them (the curriculum and the G = 4 win) were flagged as SUSPECT by an indepen-
dent verification pass before multi-seed re-testing failed to reproduce either advantage
reliably. Reporting an intervention as a null after an independent reviewer flagged it as
overclaimed is, in this project’s convention, part of the result.
Third, the single-seed regime is the dominant shared limitation. The cross-scale anchors
are single-seed and descriptive. The roster-level verbosity-trap audit is single-seed per
configuration, and its in-training reward metric is not a held-out one. The P3 sweep,
the P4 controlled comparison, both same-stack comparisons (the v2 rerun at seven to
ten seeds), the five-seed Qwen3-8B held-out control and the P4 scale extension operate
at n ≥ 3, and the Qwen3-8B control is the only multi-seed matched base-versus-RL
comparison on Qwen3-8B; the Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT pre/post runs and the small-
sample pairs of Table 8.B are the other base-versus-trained comparisons. Every headline
in this chapter is therefore stated at the strength that its own evidence base supports,
which is in several cases weaker than the strength at which it would need to be stated to
generalise beyond the named stack. The reconstructions (G = 32 and above in P3, the
T = 64M grid, the difficulty-gated saving projection) are held at hypothesis-generator
status for the same reason: they are joint-fit extrapolations, not trained arms, and the
project declines to let them carry a recommendation.
Taken together, the four pillars establish no general algorithm ranking: the unsaturated
v1 PPO gap did not survive the corrected v2 rerun, which finds no detectable GRPO
advantage, while the group-size contrasts remain unresolved at these seed budgets. The
method’s most widely repeated specific failure mode — length inflation — is absent in
the regimes where it was tested directly and is not established by the roster flag, which
is not specific to length. That is a narrower and more cautious set of conclusions than
the P1–P4 internal reports set out to establish, and the chapter reports it as such.




                                           126
Chapter 7

Results II: Reporting, Registry and
Controller (P5–P7)

The three internal reports covered in this chapter form a chain rather than a set of inde-
pendent results. P5 makes a claim about the epistemic status of reinforcement-learning
results for language models and proposes a checkable reporting standard to match it. P6
turns the standard into a machine-readable artifact with a validator, a badge and a diff
tool. P7 takes the diagnostic that the standard requires authors to publish — the zero-
variance fraction (ZVF) — and asks whether it is mechanistically predictable and ready
to control rollout allocation. Each report is explicit about where its claim stops, and this
chapter reports their evidence in the same register.



7.1     A Minimum Reporting Standard for Stack-
        Conditioned Results (P5)

P5’s position is that results in this field are stack-conditioned, and that reporting the
framework or algorithm label without the stack is therefore insufficient to support the
claims that are routinely made from it. The evidence for that position is not a single
demonstration but a family of levers, each showing that varying one component of the
stack, with the label held fixed, moves or flips the reported outcome or its telemetry.
The cleanest lever is a managed 12-cell head-to-head: four group-relative labels (GRPO
[11], DAPO [22], Dr. GRPO [21], GSPO [23]) run on one stack — Tinker-managed,
Qwen3.5-4B, GSM8K, three seeds per arm. Mean last-10 training reward spans 0.710
to 0.744, a band of 0.034, with per-arm values of 0.744 (DAPO), 0.742 (Dr. GRPO),
0.723 (GRPO) and 0.710 (GSPO); mean ZVF over the same arms is 0.578, 0.500, 0.567
and 0.511. The labels move telemetry by more than they move outcome, and neither


                                            127
movement is large. The band is not evidence of equivalence, however. With three seeds
per arm and 15 steps per run, the pooled within-arm seed SD of last-10 reward is 0.105,
about three times the band; unpaired TOST fails for every pair of labels at margins of
±0.05, ±0.10 and ±0.15, and the smallest margin at which all six pairs would pass is
±0.19. Paired by seed, which fixes the prompt stream, the residual SD falls to 0.017:
the seed accounts for 96% of the variance and the label for 2.5% (F(3, 6) = 2.62, p =
0.15), yet two pairs (DAPO − GSPO and Dr. GRPO − GSPO) have 90% intervals that
exclude zero and four pass TOST at ±0.05. The exhibit therefore shows that seed-to-seed
prompt variation dwarfs the label effect at this budget, not that the labels are equivalent
(recomputed in the 16 October 2026 audit from platform_hybrid/experiments/ti
nker-runs/results/week_h2h/h2h2_*.json).
The largest lever in the corpus is an outcome flip of 5.0% to 85.6% final training reward
under a fixed label when the backend is changed. That figure must be read with its prove-
nance caveat attached, because the same inspection that surfaced it also found that the
nominal backend comparison bundled a different base checkpoint: Qwen3-8B-Base on
the managed Tinker run against the instruction-tuned Qwen3-8B on TRL. The number
is therefore evidence that item 3 of the standard must record backend and checkpoint
identity; it is not a causal estimate of the backend alone. A partial decomposition is
possible with an existing run of the instruction-tuned Qwen3-8B on the same Tinker
harness, whose last-10 training reward is 0.338. The Base → Instruct change alone, on
one harness, accounts for −0.519 of the −0.806 gap, or 64%. The residual −0.288 is not a
backend effect either: the reward function, prompt and chat template, decoding settings,
batch composition, data order, loss implementation and optimiser also differ between
the Tinker and TRL runs; the Tinker Instruct run itself used LoRA rank 32, temperature
0.8 and batch 2; and the nominally matched configuration file describes neither run ex-
actly. The TRL run logs reward 0.0 at step 1 and on 25 of its 30 steps, which is more
consistent with an answer-parsing or template mismatch than with learning dynamics
(recomputed in the 16 October 2026 audit from platform_hybrid/experiments/re
sults/framework_comparison.json and platform_hybrid/experiments/mast
er_results.json, experiments campaign_v2_w1_qwen3-8b-base, wave6_w6_bas
e_r32_t0.8_b2 and modal_trl_trl_qwen3_8b). A second same-label lever points
the other way: the label “DAPO” produced mean ZVF 0.00 on an open trainer running
true dynamic sampling and 0.58 on a closed stack running an asymmetric-clip surrogate.
These two runs are not a comparable pair. They differ in model, task, group size and
step count as well as in stack; the open trainer’s 0.00 holds by construction, because dy-
namic sampling discards zero-variance groups before they are logged; and the managed
“dapo” configuration implements only an asymmetric-clip surrogate, not DAPO. The
exhibit illustrates that a label does not identify an implementation; it does not measure


                                           128
a stack effect.
These exhibits are summarised by a variance decomposition, and its design has to be read
with it. The algorithm term comes from the four-method same-stack N2 run (GRPO,
AERO, GIFT, AREAL; one seed; 40 steps each, so 4 groups of 40 per-step observations,
n = 160, df = 3 between and 156 within): 𝜂2 = 0.0454 on ZVF, with a within-method
(stratified) step-bootstrap CI of [0.0094, 0.1476], and 𝜂2 = 0.0075 [0.0019, 0.0790] on
mean reward (corrected; see Table E.1). The source interval was drawn from the pooled
steps, so it described a null band rather than the estimate’s uncertainty. The corrected
ZVF interval therefore does not support the reading that the algorithm explains at most
about 6% of ZVF variance: an algorithm share of up to roughly 15% is compatible
with these data, and the source’s DECISIVE grade on this term falls to SUGGESTIVE.
Even the corrected intervals are too narrow, because the steps are autocorrelated within
a run and those 156 within-group degrees of freedom overstate the independent infor-
mation (recomputed by platform_modal/scripts/p5p8/p5_headline_cis_full.
py; output platform_hybrid/experiments/results/p5p8/p5_headline_cis_fu
ll.tsv). No group-size term is reported from this decomposition: the group-size file,
groupsize_zvf_sweep.tsv, holds one row per G level (4 cells, one observation per
cell, df = 0 within), on which any one-way decomposition returns 𝜂2 = 1 by construction
(corrected; see Table E.1). The 98-cell mega-campaign decomposition is an unbalanced
partial grid (98 of 120 cells over model family, task slice, G, temperature and seed, with
two seeds). Its four stack axes account for 72.9% to 92.7% of the variance in the five
logged metrics; the seed axis rounds to 0.000, so no stack-to-seed ratio is quoted as an
effect size (corrected; see Table E.1).
The standard that P5 proposes has eight items — seven run-manifest fields and one eval-
uation item — and each item earned its place by exhibiting a documented lever of this
kind. Item 1 is the loss form, reported as a tuple of ratio level, clip low and high, to-
ken mask, advantage normalisation and dynamic-sampling status — the same label with
and without dynamic sampling produced opposite ZVF telemetry (0.00 against 0.58).
Item 2 is the reference policy and KL handling, which distinguishes family members
that are otherwise conflated and whose omission is unrecoverable after the fact. Item
3 is the sampler, backend and precision, including base-checkpoint identity, motivated
by the 17× exhibit above. Item 4 is the per-step ZVF and gradient-utilisation trajectory,
because a single mean aliases opposite regimes: across a 368-run audit drawn from a
790-run corpus, mean ZVF is 0.97 and 0.95 for two low-reward models (Llama-3.2-
1B and Llama-3.2-3B), falls to 0.25 and 0.29 at mid-range rewards, and rises again to
0.87 and 0.96 for two near-saturated models. The audit spans nine model labels, not
the six in that table. Read per training step, the U-shape is largely the binomial identity
plus prompt-difficulty heterogeneity rather than a separate phenomenon: ZVF is high


                                           129
wherever step reward is near 0 or 1, the mid-reward trough is occupied by particular
models and configurations (Qwen3-32B and Qwen3-8B, six runs each), and at a given
step reward ZVF exceeds ℎ𝐺 of that reward by +0.16 on average, consistent with hetero-
geneous per-prompt difficulty (§6.3.3; recomputed in the 16 October 2026 audit from
the W&B step histories). The item’s reporting rationale — that a single mean aliases
opposite regimes — stands; the U-shape is not independent evidence for the P7 theory.
Item 5 is the group-size schedule, since group size is a signal-geometry knob rather than
a throughput knob; measured mean ZVF for Qwen3-32B falls from 0.33 at G=4 to 0.18
at G=8 and 0.08 at G=16, and for Llama-3.1-8B from 0.76 to 0.65 to 0.47, while the two
boundary cases compress the effect as the theory predicts. Item 6 requires a held-out
split disjoint from the reward environment. Item 7 requires a contamination check and a
parser-robustness probe: adding micro-jitter 𝛿 ~ U(0,10−4 ) collapsed the exact-tie frac-
tion from 95/600 (0.1583) to 0/600 while pairwise contrast density changed negligibly.
Recalculation with the declared sample-variance tolerance 𝜀 = 10−6 instead leaves ZVF
at 95/600 before and after jitter (§6.3.1). The example demonstrates sensitivity of exact
ties to rounding, not failure of the tolerance-based diagnostic.
Item 8 is deliberately different in kind: held-out pass@k curves at k ∈ {1, 8, 32} belong
to the evaluation report rather than to the run manifest. The lever here is a matched base-
versus-post-RL panel — four adapters at G ∈ {2, 4, 16, 32}, one stack, the same problems
— on which pass@1 deltas scatter from −0.5 to +1.9 points (under which reading the
G=2 arm would be called a regression) while the pass@32 frontier improves by +1.5
to +2.5 points in all four arms. Neither reading reaches significance at a single training
seed, which is itself the argument: without pass@k curves and stated noise bands the
same panel supports any narrative the reader prefers.
P5 also ships the machinery that makes the standard enforceable rather than advisory. An
auditor scores a manifest against the eight items and emits a 0–100 weighted badge; on
an n=103 audit the weighted total was 55.0 with a badge span of [35.8, 56.7] and standard
deviation 3.1, awarding zero gold, zero silver, 99 bronze and 4 wood. Coverage, however,
is where the negative result lies. On 98 manifests, every one of the seven manifest fields
is present as a key 100% of the time, yet item 2 is validated in 0 of 98 cases (0.0%),
and all twelve of its sub-fields are present at 0 in each case; nine of the sub-fields across
items 1 and 2 — advantage normalisation, clip high, clip low, dynamic sampling, KL
coefficient, KL estimator, ratio level, reference snapshot and token mask — sit at 0.0%
with zero information content. Twenty-three sub-fields examined across the population
show no field at a full null rate, but twenty of the twenty-three carry at most two distinct
values, and the twenty entries collapse to ten distinct 23-bit fingerprints.
Two further analyses bound what the standard can be expected to buy. The first is a
discriminative-entropy audit: four of the seven manifest fields are vacuous on this cor-


                                            130
pus — loss form, reference policy and KL, sampler/backend/precision, and decontami-
nation notes each carry too little task-discriminative information to distinguish runs —
while the per-step ZVF path and the group-size schedule carry the information. A field-
sufficiency regression agrees: a random forest on the disclosed stack fields, scored by
out-of-fold R2 over the 98 cells (8-fold cross-validation, 8 fold seeds), predicts ZVF at
R2 = 0.832 [0.772, 0.879] and mean reward at R2 = 0.993 [0.990, 0.996]. The label-only
comparator is R2 = 0.000 by construction, because all 98 cells share one algorithm label,
so it is a floor rather than a competing model. The second is a manifest self-sufficiency
test, and it splits sharply by metric. The manifest carries essentially all of the ZVF sig-
nal (R2 = 0.776 [0.730, 0.846] against 0.773 [0.735, 0.834] for the cells, ΔR2 = −0.003
[−0.045, +0.034]) but only a minority of the reward signal (R2 = 0.280 [0.219, 0.443]
against 0.743 [0.688, 0.820], ΔR2 = +0.463 [+0.309, +0.564]); all 98 cells are therefore
labelled honest_but_vacuous on the three-axis triangulation.
The most useful negative in P5 is the placebo-replacement bound. The four-of-seven
vacuity pattern admits two readings: the schema chose the wrong items, or any stack-
descriptor schema would be vacuous on a single-stack corpus. Projecting six candidate
replacement items onto the live corpus supports a narrower conclusion: the four invari-
ant hyperparameter candidates do not repair its lack of variation. Four of the six — KL
coefficient, clip range low, advantage normalisation and mini-batch size — project to
exactly one unique value on the 98-cell corpus with a bootstrap CI excluding any posi-
tive information gain, because the corpus never varies them; temperature schedule would
add 0.995 bits to the sum of marginal field entropies, lifting that sum from 11.41 to 12.41
bits, and a model-family label is redundant with an existing column. The variance parti-
tion confirms it: stack axes explain 𝜂2 = 0.947 of ZVF variance across 25 unique buckets,
with 5.3% seed residual and an algorithm term of zero by construction. This establishes
a limitation of this corpus and these candidates, not the optimality of the schema. The
reported 11.4 bits sum marginal field entropies; they are not the joint information in
the fingerprint. The stored field table assigns 6.6147 bits to the unique per-cell teleme-
try path, 2.3121 to group size, 1.5546 to held-out split and 0.9313 to decontamination
notes; the other three fields are constant. Excluding the identifier leaves 4.7980 summed
marginal bits, which can still count shared information more than once. Hamming dis-
tance over the fingerprint is associated with the absolute difference in ZVF (Spearman
𝜌 = +0.44), PCD (𝜌 = +0.39) and mean reward (𝜌 = +0.28) across all 4,753 cell pairs,
while the badge itself is constant across cells and cannot discriminate seed-pair disper-
sion (𝜌 = +0.000, n = 48 pairs). Because pairs that share a cell are not independent,
significance comes from a Mantel test that permutes cell labels jointly on the distance
matrix (9,999 permutations): p < 10−3 for all three outcomes, and the association holds
when seed replicates are averaged into 50 configurations (𝜌 = +0.47, +0.43, +0.32). The


                                           131
Hamming distance takes only four values and is in effect a function of group size and
task, so the test shows that cells differing on those fields differ in outcome, not that the
fingerprint adds information beyond them. The source paper’s ZVF and PCD values (𝜌
= +0.529 and +0.541) could not be reproduced: its own analysis script gives +0.407 and
+0.336 on a 2,000-pair sample, and the full-matrix values are those above (corrected;
see Table E.1).



7.2     The GRPO-Registry (P6)

P6’s premise is that the deltas defining GRPO variants — DAPO’s asymmetric clip,
dynamic sampling and token-level loss; Dr. GRPO’s dropped length and standard-
deviation normalisation; GSPO’s sequence-level importance ratio [23] — live in pa-
per appendices and framework defaults rather than in any machine-readable form. The
registry is the response, and it ships as three artifacts at registry/: schema.json,
entries/*.json and query.py.
The schema defines two record types under a single oneOf, both carrying
schema_version.           The stack record’s core is min_report, one sub-object
per minimum-report item, with base-checkpoint identity required inside the
sampler_backend block precisely because the audit that motivated item 3 found
a managed backend silently pinning a different base checkpoint. Two design decisions
carry weight. The first is the distinction between an unreported field and a field reported
as absent: a null means the author said nothing, whereas an explicit false, 0.0 or
"none" means the author made a checkable claim — “a stack that says ‘we use no KL
term’ is making a checkable claim; a stack that says nothing is not.” The second is that
item 8, the held-out pass@k requirement, is attached under outcomes as an evaluation-
report field rather than folded into the run manifest, preserving the eight-item standard’s
split between what describes a run and what describes a result. Per-component delta
status is recorded on the stack side in variant_deltas_applied, with each entry’s
status drawn from {implemented, surrogate, absent, unknown}. The shipped entry
for the managed DAPO run makes the point concretely: clip_higher is recorded
as surrogate with the note that the asymmetric clip was set through the user-facing
config while the underlying managed loss form is unverifiable, dynamic_sampling is
recorded as absent, and token_level_loss is unknown because it is managed by the
runtime. An optional ci_method object (method, bootstrap count, seed, confidence
level, source) is populated on the seven derived entries with n_boot = 2000 and a
percentile 95% interval, and left null on the remaining twenty-four.
Three variant-delta records were verified against their source papers: DAPO with five


                                            132
components (clip higher at 𝜀_low = 0.2 and 𝜀_high = 0.28, dynamic sampling, token-
level loss, overlong reward shaping, KL removed); Dr. GRPO with two removals
(the 1/|o_i| response-length normalisation in the loss and the division by the within-
group reward standard deviation in the advantage); and GSPO with a length-normalised
sequence-likelihood importance ratio and sequence-level clipping.
The seed population described in the P6 report is twenty stack records in five families, all
of which pass validation. The registry/entries/ directory at this revision holds 28
stack records and 20 variant-delta files, so the population counts, badge spans and cov-
erage percentages below describe the earlier 20-record snapshot rather than the current
directory. Family A holds four framework config dumps (Qwen3-8B, GSM8K, seed 42)
scoring badges 74, 60, 60 and 62; family B holds the four open Colab trainer arms at
toy scale, scoring 96, 96, 96 and 96; family C is the managed head-to-head at 57, 62, 62
and 60; family D contributes three variant records from a same-stack four-method run at
67 each; and family E contributes five method records from a five-seed risk-index batch
at 43 each. The family sizes are therefore 4 + 4 + 4 + 3 + 5 = 20. The finding worth
stating plainly is why the ordering looks the way it does. The config dumps were as-
sembled for a reviewer-requested fairness audit and score only 57 to 74: they nail items
2, 3 and 5, expose item 1 only partially, and cover items 6 and 7 not at all. The open
Colab arms score 96 not because the runs are better but because an open backward pass,
coupled with explicit declarations that no KL term and no decontamination check were
used, makes every field reportable. Reproducibility supplements of the kind commonly
shipped with RL papers do not automatically satisfy MIN-REPORT.
The health audit is the registry’s own evidence that it works. Every record shipped with
P6 parses: 31 of 31 pass, comprising 20 stack records and 11 variant-delta records, with
zero broken delta pointers. The delta-mix table is the sharpest single result in P6: the
label “DAPO” is claimed by ten entries, of which four implement a delta, one records a
surrogate, three record it as absent and two record it as unknown — the same label cov-
ering four distinct implementations (Table tab:p6-delta-mix). Per-item coverage on
the twenty stack records is 100% for the held-out split (40/40), 95% for sampler/backend
(76/80), 90% for telemetry (54/60), 68% for the group-size schedule (41/60), 48% for
reference policy and KL (29/60), 32% for the loss form (38/120) and 20% for decon-
tamination (8/40) (Table tab:p6-coverage). The per-openness breakdown inverts
the conventional expectation: the open subset records sampler/backend at 91.7% and
telemetry at 83.3% with reference KL at 58.3%, while the managed subset records sam-
pler/backend and telemetry at 100% but reference KL at 33.3% and the loss form at only
22.9%.
The registry was stress-tested rather than asserted. A batch of adversarial perturbations
with B = 4000 bootstrap resamples — 13 categories × 31 entries × 10 mutations = 4,030


                                            133
generated, of which 800 were no-ops for their entry and were skipped, leaving 3,230
applied — moved the overall robustness statistic from 0.864 [0.756, 0.878] before the
corresponding schema bumps to 1.000 [1.000, 1.000] after them, closing three silent-
failure categories: a missing identifier pattern check (0.645 to 1.000), MIN-REPORT
leaves declared in properties but not in required (0.000 to 1.000, fixed by promoting
key presence to a schema invariant), and free-form delta references (0.000 to 1.000,
fixed by replacing delta_id with an enum generated from the entry filenames). A
separate consistency check cross-references the variant-delta records against the MIN-
REPORT blocks: of 31 triples from 13 entries, 7 are MATCH, 0 are MISMATCH, 0
are MISSING_REPORT, 5 are SURROGATE_OBS and 19 are NOT_APPLICABLE,
giving an implementation-honesty match rate of 7/7 = 1.000 with a Wilson 95% CI of
[0.65, 1.00] (a percentile bootstrap of 7/7 is degenerate at [1, 1]).
The empirical payoff of the schema is the same-stack measurement. Re-executing four
GRPO-family methods (GRPO, AERO, GIFT, AREAL) on a single identical stack —
managed sampler, G = 8, seed 0, 40 steps, 16 prompts per step — with the full per-prompt
reward tensor logged at every step, produces reward deltas of magnitude ≤ 2 percentage
points. Two of the three show within-run intervals that exclude zero on the side of a
decrease (AERO at −0.014 [−0.023, −0.005] and AREAL at −0.020 [−0.032, −0.008]).
Those intervals are paired bootstrap intervals over the last 10 of 40 steps of a single seed-
0 run per method, so they carry no seed variance and are not replication. GIFT’s delta
is +0.016 [−0.007, +0.040]. Exact sign-flip tests over the same 10 paired steps, Holm-
adjusted across the three contrasts, give p = 0.047 for AERO, 0.047 for AREAL and
0.23 for GIFT; when prompt-level variation is included (160 paired prompt-steps), the
AERO decrease is no longer distinguishable from zero (Holm p = 0.20) (corrected; see
Table E.1). The magnitude thesis survives. Whether any difference exceeds seed-to-
seed noise was not tested, so neither the claim that all differences are within noise nor
its negation is established. The largest same-stack effects are not on reward at all: AERO
and AREAL lengthen completions by approximately 31 tokens and tighten the length
coefficient of variation, and GIFT’s loss shift of −1.67×104 is a baseline constant. A
later registry extension measures the anti-herding residual 𝛿div = ZVFiid − ZVFobs on
the same tensors, reporting a stack-mean of 0.049 with a range of [0.039, 0.053]; only
GIFT shows a borderline-significant reduction against GRPO (p = 0.066, CI [−0.021,
+0.001]), which resolves a synthesis range of [0.13, 0.23] downwards — the anti-herding
diversity bonus is real but smaller than the qualitative synthesis had guessed.
The reference CLI, registry/query.py, implements four verbs using only the stan-
dard library: list; query --item reference_kl returning FULL, PARTIAL or UN-
REPORTED; badge; and stackdiff, which maps a pair of entries onto the R0–R5
ladder and reports the highest rung triggered — R0 identical manifests, R1 nuisance-


                                            134
only, R2 runtime differences, R3 loss-form or KL-handling differences, R4 differing
variant-delta sets, R5 unauditable because a differing item is null on at least one side.
The badge formula weights the seven items equally and is outcome-blind: badge(𝑠) =
            Í
⌊100 · 17 𝑖 𝑐𝑖 (𝑠)⌋. stackdiff prints a warning at R4 and above that outcome differ-
ences must not be attributed to the shared algorithm label, and the P6 report is careful to
describe the ladder as “a risk scale, not an effect-size scale.” Applied to the shipped en-
tries, stackdiff flags the open-versus-closed “DAPO” pair as R5 from manifests alone,
and across the twenty seed entries the MIN-REPORT badge spans 43–96. Two bound-
aries deserve restating. The registry reports stated provenance and conflicts; it does not
reconcile unresolved exact-ID or model conflicts, nor does it validate nominal algorithm
comparisons. And the badge is coarse relative to the corpus — twenty entries collapse to
ten distinct fingerprints, and twenty of twenty-three sub-fields carry at most two values.
The version of stackdiff described in the P6 report did not compare the model field,
so the Base-versus-Instruct checkpoint difference behind the 17× exhibit was invisible
to it; its R5 rung meant only that a differing item was null on one side, and it fired on 366
of the 378 pairs among the 28 current stack entries; and it shipped without unit tests. It
was revised on 16 October 2026, and the revised tool is the one evaluated here. It com-
pares a fixed catalogue of 48 fields and assigns a level only from fields known on both
sides that differ, so missing information no longer raises the verdict. R5 is now reserved
for outcome-critical fields — execution mode, checkpoint identity and variant, reward
definition, prompt and chat template, dataset and evaluation protocol — and missing in-
formation is reported separately as a coverage fraction, the worst severity that could still
be hidden, and an UNDERSPECIFIED flag when coverage is below 0.8 or an R5 field
is unknown. Across the 378 pairs of the 28 stack entries the distribution is R0 39, R2
1, R3 10, R4 26 and R5 302; 342 pairs are UNDERSPECIFIED, with median coverage
0.19, because 16 of the 28 entries are still hand-entered rather than generated from a run
configuration. On the 17× exhibit the revised tool returns R5 on checkpoint identity and
variant (Qwen3-8B-Base against Qwen3-8B), reward definition, and prompt and chat
template, at coverage 0.58 — which is the confound the exhibit was missing. On the
two “DAPO” records it returns R5 on checkpoint, reward, template, dataset and evalua-
tion metric at coverage 0.87, confirming that the pair is not comparable. The tool ships
with 27 passing unit tests (tests/test_registry_stackdiff.py). It remains a re-
porting check: an R5 verdict says which recorded fields differ, not how much they move
the outcome, and its predictive value for outcome gaps is untested. The committed 11
October version, whose output on the DAPO pair (KL coefficient 0.0 against null, R5
unauditable, exit code 5) is quoted in earlier drafts, is reproducible from git HEAD.




                                            135
7.3     From Diagnostic to Controller (P7)

P7 asks a narrower question than P5 and P6. Given that the zero-variance fraction is
worth reporting, is it mechanistically predictable, and is it ready to control rollout al-
location? The P7 report’s own framing is that this does not overturn the descriptive
restraint of the earlier work — it tests ZVF’s predictive value through falsifiable pre-
dictions rather than assuming it — and it flags at the outset that the weakest link in the
chain, the causal connection from within-group contrast to gradient magnitude, rests on
toy-scale directional evidence only.
The theory is compact. Fix a prompt with latent per-rollout success probability p and a
group of G conditionally independent Bernoulli rewards. Write ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝) = 𝑝 𝐺 + (1 − 𝑝) 𝐺
for the probability that the group is degenerate, and model the usable signal per prompt
as 𝑆( 𝑝, 𝐺) = 𝑝(1− 𝑝)(1−ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝)). The first factor is the contrast a non-degenerate group
can carry; the second is the probability that any signal survives centring. Both factors
vanish at the endpoints and peak at p = ½, so this proposed signal proxy is largest at
intermediate latent success probability and vanishes at the endpoints; it is not a measured
law of learning speed. Three consequences follow, each paired with the measurement
that could have falsified it. T1 states that the expected ZVF indicator is exactly ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝).
T2 states that ℎ𝐺 is strictly decreasing in G for 0 < p < 1, with exponential decay rate
−ln max(p, 1−p). T3 states that with unnormalised group-centred advantages the mean
absolute advantage in a group is exactly 2 ˆ 𝑝(1− ˆ𝑝).
The checks have different evidential status. Binning by the same group’s empirical suc-
cess rate is an algebraic identity, not an independent validation of latent-p predictions.
For T1, binning the per-group reward tensors of the GSM8K runs by empirical success
rate gives a ZVF indicator averaging 1.000 at 𝑝ˆ ∈ {0, 1} and 0.000 at every interior bin;
at run level, measured ZVF sits above ℎ𝐺 ( ¯𝑝) at every G, which the P7 report attributes to
the Jensen gap — the binomial model evaluates ℎ𝐺 at the mean 𝑝 while the prompt pop-
ulation can mix different latent success probabilities. The Jensen gap explains only part
of it. On the repository sweep the measured ZVF also sits above the per-step-average
null, which already absorbs the across-step variation in p: 0.838 against 0.828 at G =
2, 0.764 against 0.709, 0.691 against 0.597 and 0.631 against 0.522 at G = 16, recom-
puted from groupsize_zvf_sweep.json. This is the same baseline used in Chapter
6 §6.3.3 and Chapter 4 §4.3. For T2, two independent grids agree. The 368-run audit
shows monotone decline for every model with an interior success rate (Qwen3-32B 0.33
→ 0.18 → 0.08; Llama-3.1-8B 0.76 → 0.65 → 0.47), with the two boundary rows be-
having as the theory flags them: Llama-3.2-1B sits near p ≈ 0 where ℎ𝐺 ≈ 1 regardless
of G (0.98 → 0.97), and Qwen3-4B-Instruct sits near saturation where the decay rate is
tiny and seed noise dominates, producing the single non-monotone cell (0.89 → 0.84 →


                                            136
0.91). The repository-internal sweep at G ∈ {2, 4, 8, 16} shows the same decline, with
mean ZVF falling from 0.838 to 0.631 while held-out accuracy stays essentially flat near
0.98 throughout — raising G buys signal, not, on this near-saturated task, outcome. T3
is an algebraic identity for the stated advantages. The separate link to gradient magni-
tude cannot be checked on the closed stack, which does not expose per-group gradients;
the open-backward-pass measurement, corr(∥∇∥, ˆ       𝑝(1− ˆ
                                                           𝑝)) = +0.71 on a 0.5B model
with synthetic arithmetic, is reported strictly as directional evidence. Only that scalar
survives in the retained artefacts, so it cannot be recomputed, and because T3 makes
the mean absolute group-centred advantage exactly 2 ˆ  𝑝(1− ˆ
                                                            𝑝), a positive correlation with
gradient norm is in large part mechanical.
Because ZVF aliases mastery with incapacity, the P7 report promotes the pairwise con-
trast density PCD = ((G−1)/G)·E[p(1−p)] as the signal to control on. The micro-jitter
experiment in §7.1 motivates recording the metric definition: the exact-tie fraction falls
from 0.158 to 0.000 while PCD is nearly unchanged, but ZVF10−6 remains 0.158. This
experiment alone does not establish that PCD is a better control signal than tolerance-
based ZVF. The controller’s design follows from the theory: a zvf-triage callback
watches per-step ZVF with PCD as an aliasing guard, and an adaptive-G controller es-
calates group size on ZVF spikes, changing only the sampler budget so that it composes
with any group-relative objective.
The four-arm open-trainer audit is the first place where the intervention is measured
against alternatives on the same data, budget and seeds. Against a 120-rollout baseline,
GRPO gains +0.500 held-out at mean ZVF 0.25; Dr. GRPO gains +0.575 at 0.27 on the
same 120 rollouts; DAPO-style dynamic sampling gains +0.550 while driving ZVF to
0.00 at 174 rollouts, a +45% cost; and adaptive-G gains +0.575 while lowering mean
ZVF only modestly to 0.23 at 186 rollouts. Three readings are stated. Telemetry behaves
as the mechanisms dictate. Dynamic sampling buys perfect telemetry at a 45% rollout
premium and does not exceed the cheaper fixed recipe. Adaptive-G spends comparable
overhead and ties the best held-out result, concentrating its spend on flagged steps — and
the tie is small-n and single-task, so the description is “competitive with, not superior
to, the best fixed recipe.”
The controller simulation then asks a counterfactual question on the real reward tensors:
for each of the 2,560 prompt-step decisions in the four-method run, when would each
controller have fired, what group size would it have chosen, and how much contrast
would it have restored? Its zero-rescue result is conditional on the plug-in estimator,
rather than evidence that fresh rollouts cannot recover contrast. Across all four methods
and all five thresholds of the point-estimate rule, the controllers save zero prompts. Of
GRPO’s 640 prompt-step pairs, 461 have identical observed rewards — 427 all-correct
and 34 all-wrong — with 𝑝ˆ at exactly 0 or 1, and the counts are near-identical for the other


                                            137
methods (AERO 461, GIFT 493, AREAL 452). Substituting 𝑝ˆ ∈ {0, 1} into ℎ𝐺 forces
the estimated degeneration probability to one for every G. Resampling the same eight
identical observed rewards to size G = 16 also cannot create an unobserved outcome:
the resulting 0/1,867 rescue count is mechanical. Neither calculation identifies latent
p as 0 or 1. These traces contain predominantly all-correct groups, but independent
fresh responses at larger G from the historical panel are needed to estimate how often
new outcomes restore contrast there. The separate frozen-checkpoint diagnostic in §7.4
observes new responses under a different, explicitly fixed contract; it does not reproduce
this historical panel. The operating range where the trigger fires selectively is 𝜏 ∈ [0.70,
0.80], 12 to 19 fires per 40 steps at a cost ratio of 1.30 to 1.48, and the cost differences
are retrospective budget accounting under that estimator.
In the same trace the escalation asymmetry is stark: 1,723 of 1,867 escalation events
(92.3%) occur on all-correct groups and only 144 on all-wrong groups. A symmetric
ZVF trigger mostly spends on prompts whose eight observed completions were all cor-
rect; that is not proof of certain success on future draws. Nor is the share informative
about the controller: with independent binomial draws at each prompt’s observed suc-
cess rate, the expected all-correct share of degenerate groups is 91.5% (1,775 of 1,940
expected degenerate groups), so 92.3% is what the reward level of an easy prompt set
predicts. No controller ran in this trace; it is a counterfactual replay of four fixed-G runs
(recomputed in the 16 October 2026 audit). The P7 report is explicit that deleting those
fires is a counterfactual proposal only, because performance preservation has not been
measured.
Two refinements sharpen the picture and then complicate it. The Bayesian controller
replaces the point estimate with a Beta(1,1) posterior and a mid-range probability m(k,
G) = Pr[0.05 ≤ p ≤ 0.95 | k, G], escalating when m exceeds a posterior threshold. At
𝜏_post = 0.60 it fires on the same 466.75 degenerate prompt-steps as the point-estimate
rule at 𝜏 = 0.50 — with a 95% bootstrap CI of [454.25, 485.00] — but at strictly lower
cost, 8,854 rollouts against 10,240, a 14% saving; and all 466.75 of those mean fires are
on observed all-equal groups, so the plug-in rescue calculation again reports zero; this
is not a posterior-predictive zero-rescue guarantee. Above 𝜏_post = 0.65 it never fires,
because the maximum m over degenerate observations is 0.630.
The posterior-predictive eight-controller comparison produces a model-based metric re-
versal with bootstrap CIs attached. Its contrast-restoration estimates depend on the as-
sumed posterior and are not observed outcomes from fresh rollouts; these intervals do
not validate that assumption. On the cost axis the Pareto winner is Dualformer-Auto, an
automatic difficulty-gated de-escalation rule, at a mean cost ratio of 0.657 [0.599, 0.738]
against 1.000 for fixed G = 8 — a 34% rollout saving, assigned by the retrospective model
to shrinking prompts classified as degenerate at G = 8. On a different axis, contrast re-


                                            138
stored per 1,000 extra rollouts, the ordering reverses: zvf-triage at 𝜏 = 0.50 restores
90.10 units per 1,000 extra rollouts [89.40, 90.77], against 86.68 for 𝜏 = 0.70, 84.00 for
Dualformer-Auto and 80.00 for the Bayesian rule, and all four pairwise differences have
CIs excluding zero. The Bayesian controller — Pareto-dominant on the earlier axis —
is dominated here, because its principled prior fires on the hardest-to-restore degenerate
prompts. The regime decomposition explains the mechanism rather than leaving it as a
ranking: zvf-triage at 𝜏 = 0.50 fires on 100% of steps in every regime on this panel,
so it is operationally equivalent to “escalate every step,” and its apparent advantage is
mechanical exposure rather than a smarter rule. Its restoration decomposes 64.76% de-
generate, 13.69% boundary and 21.56% mid-range; the non-degenerate 35.24% share
is what the Bayesian rule sacrifices.
Two further analyses bound what this family of shrink-only rules can achieve. A per-
prompt hindsight-optimal calculation scans G’ down from a base of 8 and finds a bi-
modal optimum: G* = 8 for 72.9% of the 2,560 prompt-steps, which are observed all-
equal groups assigned zero rescue by the plug-in model, and G* = 2 for the remaining
27.1%, which are already non-degenerate at G = 8 and are cheapest to shrink. Mean
G* is 6.376, giving a cost ratio of 0.797 [0.776, 0.818] — a 20.3% saving that is pure
economy, since mean contrast restored per prompt is 0.000 and mean contrast lost to
shrinking is 0.130 ZVF units. This hindsight optimum is not a ceiling on cost (cor-
rected; see Table E.1): Dualformer-Auto’s observed 0.66 to 0.68 on the four-bin rule is
cheaper. The two optimise different objectives. The hindsight calculation keeps every
already-degenerate prompt at G* = 8 and only shrinks non-degenerate ones, whereas
Dualformer-Auto also shrinks prompts classified as degenerate in the observed G = 8
groups. The 0.797 is therefore the best cost ratio under a no-shrinking-of-saturated-
prompts constraint, not a lower bound on cost for any adaptive rule. A finite-pool cor-
rection then shows that even this bound is loose: because a finite pool never collides
more than independent sampling, the exact hypergeometric contrast-preservation prob-
ability exceeds the binomial one everywhere, by +0.0494 [+0.0481, +0.0506] on the 693
relevant prompt-steps, which means the binomial model over-predicts starvation and the
“zero contrast restored” accounting understates true preserved contrast by roughly 34.2
ZVF units.
Cross-base replication is where the retreat from “controller” to “audit” is earned. On
a second panel of five independent seeds, the trigger fires 4.20 times per seed [3.00,
5.40] at 𝜏 = 0.70, with the per-step rate falling from 0.48 to 0.28 as the panel’s overall
ZVF drops; what replicates is the sign and the operating-point logic, not a fixed firing
magnitude. The Bayesian branch is entirely silent on this panel, firing 0.0 times per
seed, because every step’s implied mid-range probability lies in [0.95, 0.999] — a real
negative finding that scopes the Bayesian controller to distributions containing boundary


                                           139
steps. And the aggregate firing rate is stable while the location is not: at 𝜏 = 0.70 with a
de-escalation threshold of 0.95, the cross-seed Jaccard overlap of the fire sets is 0.133,
only 13.3% of steps see all five seeds choose the same group size, and the mean cross-
seed Kendall-𝜏 of per-step ZVF trajectories is −0.060. The firing rate is seed-stable; the
firing location is not, so these traces do not support transferring a step-indexed firing
schedule across seeds. The replayed unification confirms the direction: on this panel
Dualformer-Auto is the only controller that saves compute (+14% [0.10, 0.18]) while
zvf-triage spends more than baseline (−28% [−0.36, −0.20]), with disjoint CIs.
Five design rules close the P7 report, and they read as constraints discovered rather than
principles asserted. Gate on PCD and alarm on ZVF: escalate only when ZVF is high
and PCD indicates interior difficulty, because high ZVF with near-zero PCD and high
reward is consistent with mastery — where ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝) ≈ 1 − 𝐺𝜀 near the boundary and
added rollouts buy order 𝜀 — while high ZVF with near-zero reward is consistent with
incapacity; finite observations alone do not identify either endpoint, so the proposed
choice between data intervention and resampling still needs prospective validation. Es-
calate multiplicatively in small steps, since at p = ½ each additional rollout halves the
degenerate fraction; the audited 4 → 6 → 8 schedule at multipliers of 1.5 and 1.33 cap-
tured most of the achievable reduction at a total cost of +55% rollouts, whereas G = 16
costs four times the rollouts of G = 4 for a ZVF drop from 0.76 to 0.63 and no held-out
gain on a near-saturated task. Consider spike-based rather than level-only triggers: the
reported panel has lag-1 autocorrelation of approximately 0.94 and upward drift, which
can make a level threshold fire persistently or rarely. That panel mixes measured and
projected rows (§6.3.2), so this remains a design hypothesis rather than a general mea-
sured law. Never compare controllers across stacks; ship the seven manifest fields with
item 5 carrying the escalation log. And treat ZVF = 0 as a purchase rather than a prize
— the toy dynamic-sampling arm reached zero observed ZVF at a 45% rollout premium
without exceeding the cheaper fixed recipe on held-out outcome. Neither the premium
nor zero ZVF is guaranteed on other tasks.
The chapter’s summary of P7 is the report’s own. No result establishes prospective
superiority over static G = 16, Dr. GRPO, or dynamic sampling; adaptive group size
is explicitly not promoted to a recommended policy; and all intervention evidence is
single-task, small-n, or retrospective. The report’s contribution is a retrospective con-
troller audit together with a pre-registered prospective test plan, and the bakeoff that
would close the question — a matched-budget, multi-seed held-out comparison against
the strong static baseline — is future work rather than a claim. In the same spirit, the
report flags its own August 2026 evidence boundary: unsigned ZVF alone cannot se-
lect between retiring, distilling, retrying, resampling or warm-starting a prompt, so any
operator policy remains a prospective evaluation contract rather than a demonstrated


                                            140
benefit.



7.4        Historical V2 Frozen-Policy Fresh-Rollout Diagnos-
           tic

Format-induced contrast is distinct from numeric contrast. A prospectively frozen
diagnostic completed on 2 October 2026 found that 33 of 461 initially homogeneous
eight-response groups became mixed after 24 additional responses: 7.16%, with a
prompt-level Wilson 95% interval of 5.14-9.88%. Twenty of those first reward changes
were parser failures whose visible final number still agreed with the gold answer. The
remaining 13/461 (2.82%) were assistant-audited numeric-correctness contrasts: two
wrong-to-correct recoveries and eleven correct-to-wrong changes. Thus the raw 7.16%
measures variation in this diagnostic’s binary reward, not a useful learning-signal rate
or an accuracy gain.
The main cohort contained 512 GSM8K train prompts, each with 32 ordered responses
from a frozen public Qwen/Qwen3-8B checkpoint in BF16 non-thinking mode. All
16,384 main responses completed. A disjoint 32-prompt smoke cohort was excluded
from every main estimate. Each draw was a fresh response to the same original prompt,
with a unique request seed, rather than a continuation of a previous answer. The primary
endpoint was predeclared: among prompts whose first eight binary rewards all agreed,
count those with both rewards in the combined 32. The strict parser used exact rational
matching; parser failures received reward zero, while cap-hit outputs retained their exact-
match reward and were separately flagged. Neither kind of output was removed from the
primary denominator. Appendix B §B.12 gives the pinned revisions, decoder, receipt
chain and audit method.
                   Table 7.1: Fresh reward mixedness under the frozen diagnostic contract. The all-correct
                   numerator includes all 20 format-induced first changes; its other 11 cases are numeric fail-
                   ures. Intervals use prompts as the unit and a binomial working model, not a guarantee of
                   population coverage under arbitrary dependence.

Initial eight-response
group                              Became mixed / eligible                                Rate               Wilson 95% interval

All homogeneous                                    33 / 461                             7.16%                        5.14-9.88%
(primary)
All-wrong (descriptive)                                2/9                             22.22%                       6.32-54.74%
All-correct (descriptive)                          31 / 452                             6.86%                        4.87-9.57%




The main corpus had 290/16,384 parser failures (1.77%) and 18/16,384 cap hits (0.11%),
each below the predeclared 5% quality threshold. These global quality checks do not
make a parser-induced reward change a numeric error. Separate assistant reviewers re-


                                                              141
viewed all 33 initial groups and first-changing responses in disjoint partitions, solving
the arithmetic and applying the frozen rubric; this was model-assisted qualitative re-
view, not human annotation. A coordinator separately checked both recoveries and
one bread-rise wording ambiguity. That question’s gold answer, 20 hours, assumes se-
rial rises; it is ambiguous rather than definitely wrong and was already excluded from
the numeric subset for a formatting failure. The two recoveries changed eight initial an-
swers of 1152 to the supported value 6(150 + 6 × 30 + 6 × 12) = 2412, and eight initial
answers of 12 to the supported coin total 2 + 5 + 3.25 + 2 + 0.4 + 0.35 = 13.
The predeclared clean-all-32 sensitivity was 12/439 (2.73%). Because eligibility re-
quires all 32 outputs to be parseable and uncapped, it selects on post-initial outcomes
and cannot replace the primary estimate. A post-hoc exploratory check found no later
parseable, uncapped numeric change in any of the 20 parser-first groups; it leaves the
frozen first-event audit unchanged and cannot predict further draws.
This parser effect is diagnostic-contract-specific. The repository’s existing GSM8K
test evaluator uses a more permissive extractor, including numeric conclusions embed-
ded in prose. These strict-parser events are therefore not evidence of reward failures in
the historical evaluator or training runs. The 13 conservative numeric witnesses show
that eight equal observed rewards need not imply a fixed numeric outcome on fresh draws
for this checkpoint, task and decoder. They do not reconstruct a historical checkpoint,
demonstrate training or controller benefit, identify a group-size optimum, or estimate
held-out capability. The earlier failed V1 engineering smoke remains separate and un-
pooled.



7.5     C1 Results with Reference and Unit Boundaries

Benchmark reference errors, ambiguous questions and equivalent units limit what this
diagnostic can establish. Its frozen parser-defined result is 11/64 questions (17.19%;
Wilson 95% interval 9.88-28.21%). Assistant-assisted reconciliation corroborates
three numeric recovery witnesses on the same 64-question denominator. This 3/64
count (4.69%) describes the support found under a conservative review rule; it does
not replace the frozen endpoint or estimate a population recovery rate. The other eight
parser events include three convention-dependent interpretations, four materially am-
biguous cases and one match to an incorrect reference answer. [S1-S4]
The study contains no training update. It does not measure useful reinforcement-
learning gradients, reasoning improvement, controller superiority, held-out accuracy or
general model capability. The prompts are public GSM8K training questions and are
fresh only to this campaign. Historical V2 evidence remains separate and unpooled.

The three corroborated witnesses are Santana presents (C00, source rank 676), Suzanne
bread (C07, rank 912), and Hank reading (C38, rank 2699). Santana’s supported answer
is (4 + 7) − 3 = 8 presents; Suzanne’s is 3 × 2 × 52/12 = 26 loaves; Hank’s is 5(30 +
60) + 2 × 2(30 + 60) = 810 minutes. Each has eight reviewed, genuinely wrong initial
final answers and at least one independently correct fresh final answer. [S3]
Santana also shows why final-answer correctness cannot validate reasoning. Its earliest
successful fresh response, sample index 12, returns eight through an incorrect argument
that no first-half birthday gifts were needed. Later successful indices 15 and 23 use
the valid 11 − 3 calculation. Hank’s reference final value is supported even though its
reference explanation mislabels subtotals. These distinctions remain visible in the event
ledger. All sample and selected indices in this addendum are zero-based. [S3]
The checkpoint case (C35, rank 2593) gives the opposite result. Checkpoints at miles
one and twenty-five enclose twenty-four miles across three gaps, so the separation is
eight miles. All eight initial answers of eight are numerically correct. Four fresh outputs
match the erroneous reference value six by dividing by four. The frozen analysis counts
the event exactly as specified; semantic review does not call it mathematical recovery.
[S3]

probability event, the pet-owner denominator and Reggie’s missing initial marble count.
A gold match does not resolve those questions. [S3]

A narrower cross-review analysis corroborates fifteen wrong-final-value judgments un-
der the stated or ordinary mathematical model. The towel case explicitly assumes equal
towel weights. Separately, two discrete-count questions have inconsistent premises and
erroneous reference relations, three correct-final references have corroborated text de-
fects, and two materially ambiguous questions contain explicit reference faults. These
are 22 disjoint cases with specified reference defects, not 22 wrong labels. The four
correct-final cases in the refined second review and the three corroborated correct-final
defects are different sets: a notation defect newly identified for Sonia/Joss is not corrob-
orated in both rounds. [S2, S4]
Thirteen first/second category disagreements are retained. The coin question illustrates
the reason: gross deposits total 225 cents and the balance after withdrawal is 200 cents.
The second review judges the recorded 200 wrong for the requested deposits, while the


                                            144
first retains a gross-versus-net target ambiguity. It is excluded from the narrower fifteen.
Ordinary mathematical conventions are not defects merely because every physical as-
sumption is unstated; genuinely different quantities or plausible readings are described
case by case. None of these audit counts estimates whole-GSM8K error prevalence
because the cohort was selected for eight parser-zero outputs and then truncated. [S2,
S4]
Equivalent representations also matter. For Jackson ingredients (C36), all eight ini-
tial values 0.24 are equivalent to the reference 24 percent under the same conventional
independent-selection probability interpretation; formal odds would be 6:19. The scalar
parser extracted 0.24 faithfully. For Gunner plugs (C62), 200 pairs equal 400 individ-
ual plugs. Four initial responses preserve pair units, three remain unit-ambiguous and
one incorrectly calls 200 the number of individual plugs. Neither case supports a claim
of eight genuine mathematical errors. The frozen scalar references and rewards remain
unchanged. [S5]


7.5.1    Separate Prospective Parser Validation

The undeployed parser v3 candidate was frozen at 02:43:30 UTC on 3 October 2026, be-
fore the earliest target batch at 02:59:39 UTC. The fixed target was the last 256 screening
questions, eight responses each, yielding 2,048 future outputs. This was a predefined
future subset of an ongoing study, not preregistration before the entire C1 campaign.
Historical candidate tuning is separate. C1’s primary score remains based on frozen
parser v2. [S8, S9]
All 2,025 previously valid full parse objects were preserved. Four previously rejected
outputs at four distinct prompts became accepted: one bold answer heading, two display-
box blocks and one hash-prefixed XML answer block. Two separately blinded, output-
only assistant annotations agreed on the same unambiguous scalar in all four changed
cases. No changed acceptance was ambiguous or incorrectly extracted under those re-
views. Nineteen outputs remained rejected. [S8, S9]
This supports only a narrow pass on the four observed extraction changes. It does not es-
tablish semantic precision for the 2,025 unchanged outputs, validate the nineteen rejects,
measure task correctness, or estimate population false-positive rates. The responses
are clustered within prompts. Four examples do not exhaust their wrapper classes or
malformed-input behavior. The candidate remains undeployed and is not used to re-
compute C1 recovery. [S9]
All source labels refer to Appendix G.




                                            145
Chapter 8

The E1–E14 Evaluation Campaign

This chapter reports the evaluation of a single Tinker-trained actor against fourteen
benchmark suites outside its recorded training-suite list, recorded lane by lane under
sealed reservations, immutable receipts and explicit terminal states. Item-level decon-
tamination is not established. Lanes that did not finish and lanes that could not start are
reported too, because the methodological aim is to make the boundary between what
was measured, what was only reported, and what can still be verified explicit. The cur-
rent repository does not preserve every cited artifact, so it cannot support an unqualified
claim that every figure has a surviving, hash-pinned evidence chain. Lane-by-lane detail
that does not change any figure is collected in Appendix D (Figure 8.1).
Two reporting rules govern everything below, and they are worth stating before any
number appears. First, original-contract results and replacement-scope results are never
pooled and never averaged across suites; a replacement scope is a different suite, a dif-
ferent interface and often a different grader, and combining such figures would produce
a number with no referent. Second, no figure in this chapter is quoted to a precision
beyond what its source receipt records, and no comparison against a baseline, a sibling
suite, or the untrained base model is asserted for the campaign lanes of Sections 8.2–8.6.
Those lanes have no matched base-model arm. The one paired baseline comparison in
this chapter is the small-scale trained-versus-base rerun of Section 8.7 (Table 8.B): 14
lanes, 5 to 151 identical items each, one vLLM engine for both arms. It finds no signifi-
cant difference in any lane, and this chapter therefore claims no improvement of any kind
over the base model. Where a lane’s record itself contains an unresolved discrepancy,
the discrepancy is stated.

_RESULTS_2026-09-19.md, which supersedes the working tables of 2026-09-05
and 2026-09-12; the per-lane terminal-state ledger, withheld historical campaign
chronology, was recovered from the original Mac checkout on 3 October 2026 with

without rerunning models or scorers, rejudging correctness, or reconstructing the miss-

only; the frozen-checkout absence record remains valid. E14’s recovered note contains
a denominator inconsistency, and the recovered finish ledger does not substantiate the
later E4 rerun; both are disclosed below.



8.1     The evaluated actor and the one-actor design

A single actor was evaluated across the whole campaign. The base model is
Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B at revision 995ad96eacd98c81ed38be0c5b274b0403
1597b0, and the trained parameter set is the LoRA adapter campaign-adapter
at commit withheld-adapter-revision, served in bfloat16 under the identifier
campaign-actor-bf16. The public package does not contain the original execution
route or operational repository access identities. It therefore cannot reproduce the orig-
inal sampler execution from these materials alone; no current provider-retention or
account-status claim is made here.
The one-actor design is deliberate. No lane received a separate checkpoint, per-suite
prompt engineering beyond its native harness, or tuning against its own evaluation
set: the recovered training-identity record lists only API-Bank-RLVR and SWE-Gym


                                           148
(§8.1.1), while the recovered E10 receipt explicitly reports TRAINING_INVENTORY_AB
SENT and makes no held-out claim. The E10 contract’s declaration of AgentDojo train-
ing is explicitly marked as not evidence of training consumption; neither record proves
content-level decontamination. Differences between suites therefore reflect the inter-
action of one fixed policy with fourteen different interfaces, observation spaces, tool
conventions and graders, and they are not evidence about what a specialised actor could
achieve. The campaign also cannot support any statement about seed variance. Infer-
ence conditions were frozen per lane and recorded in that lane’s own receipt (§8.1.2).


8.1.1     How the actor was trained

The adapter is attributed to a single Tinker training run (W&B run withheld-id).
The recovered training_ identity_ verified.json records that run ID, ag-
gregate dataset revision, start time, base Git commit and hashes of config.yaml,
wandb-metadata.json and output.log. Its status is TRAINING_CONFIG_VERIFI
ED_NOT_CONTENT_DECONTAMINATION, and it states that the dirty source diff was not
uploaded. The underlying W&B files and local run receipt have not been recovered in
this package, so their bytes cannot yet be matched afresh to those recorded hashes. Ap-
pendix D separates these surviving identity fields from the still-reported configuration
details in Table 8.1.

Four items remain unavailable or were never created — the exact training code, which 80
training prompts were drawn, the Tinker sampler weights, and any held-out training-time
evaluation — and the run departs from the planned Pavlov protocol; both are recorded
in Appendix D so that no reader fills them in.
What this means for the chapter is direct. The actor received 40 steps of sparse-reward,
critic-free policy-gradient training, with zero loss on 35 of them. The fourteen suite
results are properties of this checkpoint, not evidence about what GRPO-style training
contributes. The campaign is reported as an exercise in evaluation governance. The only
trained-versus-base evidence is the small-sample paired comparison of Table 8.B.


8.1.2    Serving conditions per lane

The E4 zero (§8.5) prompted identification of a serving bridge that sent no stop se-
quences. The defect can cause failed agent trajectories, but its contribution to the full
100-trial zero is not identified by the available comparison. The same bridge, zvf-p
rogram/flagship/modal_tinker_openai_bridge.py, served several other lanes,
so Table 8.2 records for every lane how the actor was served and whether that defect
could apply. Its /v1/responses route hard-codes stop=None and thinking on. Its
/v1/chat/completions route sends stop sequences only if the client supplies them,
and the agent clients used here (opencode, mini-swe-agent and the APEX client) do not.
The deployed bridge version (sha256 f59c08b9…) matches neither committed version
of the file.

The recovered E8 diagnostic now supports its aggregate failure counts, and the recov-
ered E10 receipt supports its declared inference limits. Other entries still depend on
unrecovered raw responses or finish-era sources, so recovery of these two receipts does
not verify every serving and failure count in the table. Two consequences follow. First,


                                                        154
the lanes served through the bridge (E2, E4, E5, E7 and the E9 streaming arm) may be
exposed to the E4 mechanism, but exposure does not demonstrate that it caused their
failures or quantify its contribution to E4. Second, the reported format and length fail-
ures mean that E8, E11 and E14 headline figures are end-to-end harness scores; §8.2
distinguishes their sensitivity figures and missing evidence. E11 has substantial extrac-
tion failures under a tight generation cap, E14 has a large capped/uncapped accuracy
difference, and E1 has widespread patch-application failures. These are end-to-end pro-
tocol outcomes; the evidence does not isolate the causal contribution of budget in E11 or
E14 (§8.2, §8.3). A note on the scope of the stop-sequence mechanism is in Appendix
D.



8.2     Suites with strictly complete scopes

The historical ledger and its check log report three strictly complete scopes: E8, E10 and
E11. E8 and E10 are replacement scopes. E11 is an original-contract full-suite result that
also meets the strict-completeness check, because the suite evaluated is VerilogEval it-
self; the ledger lists it both among its replacement scopes and among its original-contract
results, but it is one run and one result. A fourth, E14, is recorded terminal-complete
under its native protocol in the recovered terminal note, subject to its denominator in-
consistency and the raw-output limits below. The six replacement scopes completed
on 27 September postdate that check and are reported separately in §8.4 (Figures 8.2
and 8.3).
E8 — LAB-Bench, public split. The recovered outputs/public_portfolio_2026
-09-05/labbench_final_diagnostics.json records all 1,967 expected items eval-
uated across eight categories, with 450 correct and 1,517 incorrect responses. Those
counts give 450/1967 = 0.2288 (Wilson 95% CI [0.211, 0.248], unit = question, n =
1,967). It also records 1,259 unparsed responses (native_answer_parse_none) and
1,086 length-truncated finishes. The supplementary validation found the native receipt
at its recorded hash and checked 1,979 archive members against both declared and lo-
cal hashes. Its 1,967 unique exported grade records independently corroborate the cor-
rect/incorrect totals, category counts and 1,086 length finishes. The 1,259 parse-none
count remains a field of the byte-preserved diagnostic: the exported grade records con-
tain no parsed-answer field for a separate recount. The reported score is an end-to-end
harness score with substantial formatting and length limitations. The conditional de-
scriptive sensitivity remains 450/708 = 0.636 (Wilson 95% CI [0.600, 0.670]), not the
headline or a direct measure of domain knowledge; its response-level classification has
not been independently regraded. Per-category figures and evidence limits are in Ap-
pendix D.
