                                                         156
E10 — AgentDojo, benign utility scope. The recovered outputs/public_portfo
lio_2026-09-05/agentdojo_native_receipt.json records status COMPLETE_NA
TIVE_BENIGN_UTILITY, 97 completed episodes, 88 utility passes, AgentDojo v1.2.2
and decontamination status TRAINING_INVENTORY_ABSENT. Its score is 88/97 = 0.9072
(Wilson 95% CI [0.833, 0.950], unit = episode, n = 97). The receipt binds the bench-
mark version but contains no upstream commit field; the previously attributed com-
mit 089ed468cf3ed0322acc66b0211f26d9d90dbf60 is therefore not verified by this
receipt. Its scope is native default benign utility only: security_evaluation and
heldout_claim are false, while attack and defense are null. Completion and utility
are distinct: 97 completed episodes do not mean 97 correct episodes. The supplemen-
tary validation checks 525 archived member pins and maps all 97 selected episodes to
unique exact-hash traces (94 original and 3 explicit continuation), whose stored utility
fields independently tally to 88 passes and zero handled errors. It excludes extra his-
torical partial traces from that mapping. This is stored-trace corroboration, not a rerun
of AgentDojo or an independent judgement of task correctness. The earlier 94-episode
attempt and reported collector repair retain their separate missing-source limitations in
Appendix D.
E11 — VerilogEval, two native framings. All 312 pinned prompt instances were
evaluated under the upstream NVlabs/verilog-eval harness, but they represent 156 un-
derlying designs, each appearing in code-completion (code-complete-iccad2023)
and specification-to-RTL framing. The descriptive result remains 129 passes of 312 =
0.4135, decomposed into 67/156 = 0.4295 for code-completion and 62/156 = 0.3974 for
specification-to-RTL. Because paired framings share a design, a Wilson interval treat-
ing all 312 outcomes as independent is inappropriate. Resampling the 156 design pairs
together gives a 95% percentile cluster-bootstrap interval of [0.343, 0.487] (200,000
replicates, seed 20261002). This describes design-composition uncertainty under an
exchangeable-design assumption for this checkpoint and these fixed sampled genera-
tions; it is not training-seed, decoding-seed or protocol uncertainty. The observed full
pinned-benchmark fraction itself remains descriptive. The surviving receipt records 150
extraction failures included in the denominator, so 129/312 is an end-to-end harness
score: 150 of its 183 failures are extraction failures. A generation-budget mechanism is
plausible but not identified (corrected; see Table E.1). The run used a 1,024-token cap
with the chat template’s default thinking mode on, and the receipt’s 312,622 response
tokens give a mean of about 1,002 per sample. That mean shows near-cap lengths; with-
out per-response text or finish reasons it cannot establish how many outputs reached the
cap or where their reasoning blocks ended. On the 50 items of the 26 September small-
scale rerun (§8.7), the receipt’s verdicts give 23/50, while the same trained adapter with
thinking off and a 4,096-token cap scores 33/50 with no extraction failures (11 items pass


                                           157
only in the second run and 1 only in the first; exact McNemar p ≈ 0.006). That compar-
ison also changes engine and temperature (managed sampler at 0.2 against vLLM at 0)
and covers 50 of 312 prompts, so it shows an improvement under the combined protocol
changes, not an isolated budget effect or a corrected score. The extractor also accepted
a module found inside an unterminated reasoning block, and per-sample response text
was not kept, so some of the 162 extracted candidates, and possibly some passes, may
be drafts from the reasoning rather than final answers. The parseable-only descriptive
sensitivity is 129/162 = 0.796, hard to interpret for that reason; no interval is given be-
cause the receipt’s aggregate extraction count does not provide the per-design extraction
indicators needed for a clustered conditional interval. One prompt, verilog_eval/spe
c-to-rtl/Prob099_m2014_q6c, fails against its own test bench under both simulators;
excluding it yields 129/311 = 0.4148, but no immutable justification for the exclusion
was captured, so the raw 129/312 is canonical and 129/311 is a noncanonical sensitivity
only. Pair counts, the bootstrap method, harness revisions, verdict rule and amendment
are in Appendix D.
E14 — Omni-MATH replacement scope, receipt-recorded terminal-complete. The
surviving ledger and historical arithmetic check record 4,426 accepted rows of 4,428 and
official accuracy 0.5131043831902395, consistent with 2,271/4,426. Retaining 2,271
passes but counting both unjudged rows as failures gives 2,271/4,428 = 51.29 per cent
(Wilson 95% CI [0.498, 0.528]), 0.02 percentage points below the judged-row rate. The
recovered outputs/PES_Phase2_Review_2026-09-12/finish/e14_terminal_no
te_2026-09-19.json records truncated judge outputs before an Equivalence Judge-
ment section, two recorded skip reasons, no re-judge and terminal completion at 4,428
dispositions. The note nevertheless labels 2271/4428 as 0.5131043831902395, a de-
nominator inconsistency: that decimal equals 2,271/4,426, not 2,271/4,428 (corrected;
see Table E.1). The original note is preserved unchanged, and this thesis retains both
clearly labelled arithmetic conventions. The supplementary Mac-side traversal found
the stored disposition, judged-row and new-judge-response files with 4,428 valid JSON
rows apiece. It reports 4,426 accepted dispositions, 2,271 correct and two skipped, and
exports both exclusion records with missing Equivalence Judgement headings. It also
records the native score’s explicit 4,426 denominator and the separate all-disposition
diagnostic. The full E14 rows are not exported here, so the cloud check verifies the
exported arithmetic and exclusions rather than independently recounting all 4,428 rows
or rejudging any mathematics. The 2,048-token actor cap is an important protocol lim-
itation, with a strong observational association between cap hits and errors (corrected
reading; see Table E.1). Recounting the stored dispositions, the 2,982 responses that
hit the cap are correct in 1,029 cases (34.5%), against 1,242 of the 1,446 uncapped re-
sponses (85.9%). The split is confounded by difficulty, since harder problems both run


                                           158
longer and fail more, so it does not identify what a larger budget would score; it does
mean 51.29% should not be read as the actor’s Omni-MATH capability independent of
its generation budget.



8.3     Original-contract results

Seven lanes have an original-contract figure, reported separately from any replacement
scope with its coverage caveats; two are full-suite results. Lane detail is in Appendix D.

    • E1 — SWE-bench Pro. 2 of 731 tasks resolved, a pass@1 of 2/731 = 0.00274,
      or 0.274 per cent (Wilson 95% CI [0.08%, 0.99%], unit = task), over the complete
      731-row test split of ScaleAI/SWE-bench_Pro. The fourteen generation failures
      and four lost artifacts stay in the denominator. The 713 “native evaluations” are
      not 713 tests of a candidate fix (corrected reading; see Table E.1). Parsed with
      git apply, 593 of the 713 submitted patches are rejected as corrupt, because
      their @@ hunk headers state line counts that do not match the hunk bodies; the
      official entry script runs a plain git apply and then the tests, so for those 593
      the tests ran against the unpatched repository. With git apply --recount, 711
      of 713 parse. Both resolved instances are among the 120 patches that parse. The
      2/731 is therefore an end-to-end issue-resolution score with a major patch-format
      limitation. It does not isolate semantic problem-solving ability, and the stored
      run does not establish whether the rejected patches would solve their issues after
      header repair.
    • E2 — FrontierSWE. 1 of 17 tasks evaluated, replay-normalised score 0.8628;
      partial, because revision-bound authorisation for the remaining sixteen was never
      obtained.
    • E4 — BankerToolBench. 1 of 100 tasks evaluated, recovery metric 0.3115 — a
      verifier recovery metric on a single task, not the simple fraction 37/128 and not a
      suite score; the pass3/pass16 archive is missing.
    • E5 — APEX-Agents. 7 of 480 tasks natively scored. The prefix mean of
      0.050505 treats unscored attempts as zero and is not a native 480-task score; the
      seven scored tasks average 0.079365.
    • E7 — BinaryAudit. One of 46 attempts recorded, verifier reward 0.0 after an
      agent error, no grade.
    • E9 — MLE-bench. 40 of 75 competitions carry native grades, a coverage of
      53.33 per cent, and the suite score is null; a separate merged-vLLM arm holds
      one valid grade (H&M, 0.02132) and is not combined.
    • E11 — VerilogEval is reported in §8.2; it is the second original-contract full-suite


                                           159
       result, alongside E1, and not an additional one.

No lane here has a matched base-model run, so none licenses a claim that the actor
outperforms its untrained counterpart, a predecessor model, or a published number.



8.4      Replacement-scope lanes completed on 27 Septem-
         ber 2026

At the 19 September ledger of record, six replacement scopes were started but incom-
plete: E1 (SWE-bench Multilingual, 35 of 300 graded), E2 (CORE-Bench, 45 capsule
setups, no grades), E5 (Tau3, 20 of 97 cleaned) and E13 (BALROG, 13 of 255 episodes)
were launch-pending, and E6 (WebArena, 0 of 812) and E9 (MLDevBench, 0 of 34)
were PENDING_QUOTA because required execution capacity was unavailable. All six
were run to completion on 27 September (Figure 8.4).
The later launch used a lead-issued protocol override rather than independent approval.
That governance limitation matters: the candidate was both the experiment lead and the
author of the override. The public derivative omits operational authorization records
and account limits; the scientific departures from the native protocols remain explicit in
Table 8.D.

The six figures are on six different benchmarks with six different metrics, and they are

a base model. Five lanes (E2, E5, E6, E9, E13) now have a native score over their full

8.5     The E4 rerun as a diagnostic

A fresh full BankerToolBench rerun was reported to have run the base model with a
zero-initialised LoRA snapshot through the Modal bridge /v1/responses path after
the lane closed CLOSED_PARTIAL on 19 September. The surviving outputs/e1_e14
_small_scale_2026-09-26/E4/result.json (prior_base_run) repeats 100 tri-
als, one error and a mean reward of 0.0. The recovered terminal ledger, withheld
historical campaign chronology, includes updates through 21 September and ex-
plicitly says the rerun was NOT launched at that snapshot. It therefore cannot support the
later completed-run account. The raw job receipt, outputs/e4_banker_toolbench/
official_repo_ff6db552/jobs/btb-banking-tasks-tinker-bridge/result.
json, remains unrecovered in this package. The 100-trial result is supported here by the
surviving prior_base_run secondary record; the earlier detailed claims of 2–14 agent
steps, no deliverables and at least 27 role-token-degenerate trajectories are not freshly
verified. The surviving bridge finding documents a route that passes no stop sequences
and can run past end-of-turn tokens into invented turns. Under a corrected shim, the
same base model scored 0.4087 on task btb-19b3361c in the six-task small-scale re-
run (§8.7). That contrast demonstrates a plausible failure mechanism, but it compares
one successful corrected task with 100 bridge trials while changing tasks, serving path
and caps. It does not identify how much of the aggregate zero the bridge caused or ex-
clude model limitations. The 0.0 is retained as a confounded diagnostic, not a controlled
model-capability estimate. It is not set against the original single-task outcome, and no
valid full-suite BankerToolBench capability estimate exists for this actor family. The
fuller reported audit and evidence limitations are in Appendix D (Figure 8.5).

8.6     Externally blocked lanes and terminal states

Seven original benchmark contracts remain outside the completed evaluation scope:
SDAB, BinaryAudit, LifeSciBench, AgentHarm, AppBench, OpenReward and Fron-
tierMath. Their required benchmark payload or hosted evaluation scope is not available
in this public package. Six are recorded as externally closed; OpenReward proceeds
only through the separate BALROG replacement scope. These statuses are scope limits
and not graded model failures. Private access correspondence, operational requests and
account state are omitted. The following small-scale reruns keep original and substitute
benchmarks separate (Figure 8.6).



8.7     Small-scale reruns of all fourteen lanes

This section adds a separate, smaller set of runs, made on 26 September 2026, to give
every one of the fourteen lanes a measured number under one fixed protocol, including
the lanes that never produced a score. None of these figures is pooled with, averaged
against, or substituted for a result reported earlier in this chapter.
The protocol fixes one actor family, a fixed item-selection seed (20260926), temperature
0 and a non-thinking chat template, and scores each lane with its native grader where
one could be run. Lanes whose original benchmark is externally blocked were run on a
public substitute, and each substitute’s gap from the original suite is recorded in its lane
file. Items that errored or timed out are counted as failures in the denominator.
Two arms were run. The first is the base model, Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B with no
adapter, sampled on Tinker (Table 8.A). The second is a paired comparison of the trained
actor against the same base model, both served by one vLLM configuration on Modal,
on identical items (Table 8.B). The pairing exists because the adapter’s effect on in-
dividual token probabilities was measured to be about the same size as the numerical
difference between the two serving engines on the same base weights, so a trained-on-
vLLM against base-on-Tinker comparison would confound the adapter with the engine,
and none is made.

These are small samples. Most lanes score between five and eighty items, so a single
item moves a lane’s rate by several percentage points and the confidence intervals are
wide. In Table 8.B, b and c count items solved only by the trained actor and only by the
base model respectively, and the CI is a paired bootstrap interval on the difference. Four
lane-specific caveats matter. For E12 the 151 rubric items come from only six generated
applications, so its item-level McNemar p-value treats clustered observations as inde-
pendent; the corresponding item-level Wilson interval is also omitted from Table 8.A;
the exact sign-flip test over the six per-app differences gives p = 0.25, and the grader
is an LLM judge that is the base model itself, so the E12 rates are judge-dependent.
For E4, only btb-19b3361c scores above zero in either arm (trained 0.397, base 0.557;
0.409 in Table 8.A), so the table omits an interval: an empirical bootstrap is computable
but has little support for inference beyond these six tasks. For E13, the 1.875 difference
is the mean of per-environment differences computed from unrounded per-item values
(recomputed 1.8752); the source rounds the two arm means to two decimals, so sub-
tracting 25.51 − 23.63 loses precision. For E9, the one competition that earned a medal
in the Tinker run (nomad2018) has a 46.8k-token prompt that exceeds the 32,768-token
vLLM limit and fails identically in both vLLM arms, so the vLLM medal rates are not
comparable with Table 8.A. The paired differences are not combined across lanes, and
none of the seventeen paired tests excludes zero. At these sizes that is an inconclusive
result, not evidence of no effect: with 5 to 10 items, an exact McNemar test needs at
least six discordant items, all in one direction, to reach p < 0.05. No equivalence test
was run. Fuller E12 detail and the principal per-lane caveats from each result file are in
Appendix D.




                                                168
8.8     Summary of the campaign at the point of writing

By the 19 September ledger the campaign had established two full-suite results and one
complete replacement scope with a score: E1 at 2/731 = 0.274 per cent on SWE-bench
Pro, E11 at 129/312 = 41.35 per cent pass@1 (design-cluster bootstrap 95% interval
[34.3, 48.7], n = 156 designs) across its two framings, and E8 at 450/1967 = 0.2288
([0.211, 0.248]) on the LAB-Bench public split, alongside a complete benign-utility
evaluation of E10 at 88 of 97 episodes (0.907, [0.833, 0.950]), a terminal-complete E14
replacement scope at 2,271/4,428 = 51.29 per cent accuracy, and five original-contract
partials (E2, E4, E5, E7, E9), none of which is a suite score. Three scores have sub-
stantial protocol limitations: 593 of E1’s 713 submitted patches were corrupt for git
apply; E11 has 150 extraction failures under a 1,024-token cap with thinking on; and
E14’s capped responses are correct at 34.5% against 85.9% uncapped. The latter split is
confounded by problem difficulty, and the E11 comparison changes several conditions
together, so their causal budget effects remain unidentified (§8.2, §8.3). On 27 Septem-
ber six further replacement scopes were completed under a self-issued override whose
deviations are recorded per lane (Tables 8.C and 8.D). Seven original contracts remain
externally blocked, and no lane now waits on a launch or a cloud quota. The campaign
did not establish a multi-seed estimate, a held-out security result, a BankerToolBench
suite score, a graded result for the 108 WebArena tasks that need an LLM judge, or a
baseline comparison for the campaign lanes themselves; the only trained-versus-base
comparison, Table 8.B, is inconclusive in every lane. The E8 diagnostic, E10 native re-
ceipt, training-identity verification, E14 terminal note and both pending-state snapshots
are recovered in the private package while remaining absent from the frozen reposi-
tory. Their receipt contents are now inspectable, and bounded stored-payload checks
corroborate E8/E10 counts and the E14 denominator distinction; training-source, E4
raw-evidence and semantic-reproduction limits remain as in §8.2 and Appendix D. The
available evidence chains, reproduction paths and gaps are given in Appendix B; the
next chapter draws the cross-study synthesis and conclusions.




                                          169
Part IV

Synthesis




   170
Chapter 9

Cross-Study Synthesis, Threats to
Validity, Conclusions and Future Work

9.1     Cross-Study Synthesis

This report has presented two bodies of work built with different instruments. The first is
the P1–P8 corpus, which probes GRPO-style post-training across libraries, model scales,
group sizes and datasets and ends in a reporting standard, a machine-readable registry
and a controller audit. The second is the E1–E14 campaign, a governance-bound eval-
uation of one Tinker-trained actor against fourteen agentic and reasoning suites outside
its recorded training-suite list; the recovered identity record does not establish content-
level decontamination. They are the same methodological commitment applied twice
— once to what does GRPO do, once to what does a GRPO-trained actor score. That
commitment is attribution: every comparative claim holds the entire training stack fixed
except the one factor under test, and where that was unachievable the confound is re-
ported alongside the number rather than absorbed.
The corpus’s own synthesis, recorded at the close of Phase 1, is that on this compute scale
the measurement layer is useful while most algorithmic levers are noise-limited. The
dominant measured phenomenon is advantage-signal starvation, with the Zero-Variance
Fraction at approximately 0.72–0.77 on the Phase-1 main configuration, and it is easy-
driven: the collapsed groups are mostly prompts the model has already solved. That
rules out the most tempting remedy, because re-baselining the advantages merely con-
verts the signal into a difficulty proxy with no learning content, and the remedy that
remains — a curriculum that rejects equal-reward groups from accepted updates while
still paying for rejected sampling — returned a null. The narrow systems observation sur-
vived better than any algorithmic one: per-step telemetry separated the available method
traces more cleanly than reward-only features, though the classifier’s integrity generali-


                                           171
sation was never tested.
The Semester 4 internal reports sharpen those threads rather than reversing them. P2’s
diagnostics are recomputable from stored raw tensors rather than read back from logged
summaries: three stored GSM8K group tensors reproduce ZVF at 0.130, 0.190 and
0.155, pooling to 0.1583, while the historical outcome correlation, 𝜌 = 0.27 (95% CI
[−0.37, 0.88], source-labelled n = 23), mixes synthetic and measured rows and incon-
sistent outcome definitions and is withdrawn as evidence. The executable lineage audit
finds seventeen finite cells, nine synthetic. ZVF remains a recomputable diagnostic,
but this pooled table does not test measured held-out prediction. The historical reward-
adjusted result, r = +0.21 (95% CI [−0.31, +0.62], n = 17), uses the same mixed co-
hort and mixed source outcomes including late training reward. It is not independent
measured-only evidence (Chapter 6 §6.3.4). P4’s matched comparison of GRPO against
Dr. GRPO finds the two held-out gains indistinguishable, with pooled mean completion
length drifting from 194.4 to 183.5 tokens under a 200-token cap that bounds what the
comparison can say. P4’s companion error-bar audit grades the single P4-facing head-
line as DECISIVE: audit H7, the iter-136 arithmetic late-training-efficiency contrast,
paired n = 5 seeds, 𝑑 𝑧 = +2.68. Re-examined, it is suggestive only (Chapter 6 §6.5.4).
Its “one-sided parametric p = 0.031” is the floor of an exact one-sided Wilcoxon test at n
= 5. The two-sided exact sign-flip p is 0.0625, even though all five pairs point the same
way. The direction was one of eight hypotheses in its family, and Holm correction across
those eight raises the family’s p to 0.25. The audit’s other three DECISIVE grades do
not survive either. Two (H1, H2) rest on an interval that excludes its own point estimate
and on four reconstructed budgets (§6.4.2; Table E.1), and one (H6) is an AUROC com-
puted on a synthetic and imputed panel (§6.3.4). Of the seven audited headlines, then,
none is decisive, one is suggestive and the rest are null or hypothesis-generating. What
survives is a consistent-direction late-training efficiency contrast on a training metric,
not a capability claim.
P3 and P7 both treat group size as a lever and are best read as a pair, because they disagree
in emphasis. P3 finds that under saturation the retention ratio between G = 2 and G = 16
is approximately 1.00. That ratio is of last-10 training reward, and a seed-level TOST (n
= 3, margin ±0.02) does not establish G = 16 as equivalent to G = 2 (p = 0.056; Chapter
6 §6.4.1). P3 also finds that the effective signal-to-noise ratio reaches only about 52%
       √
of the G ideal, and that a 505-task conditional utility audit selects G = 4 over G =
5 by 0.00177 with a 95% bootstrap interval of [0.00082, 0.00270] — positive, small,
and explicitly cohort- and objective-conditional. P7 models usable signal per prompt
as 𝑆 = 𝑝(1 − 𝑝)(1 − ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝)) with ℎ𝐺 ( 𝑝) = 𝑝 𝐺 + (1 − 𝑝) 𝐺 , derives three falsifiable
consequences, and checks them: a 368-run Weights & Biases audit across nine model
labels (Appendix A, row P7-C1; earlier drafts said six) shows the U-shape in accuracy,


                                            172
which is largely the binomial identity plus prompt-difficulty heterogeneity rather than
independent confirmation (§6.3.3), with ZVF 0.95–0.97 at the low-reward extreme and
0.87–0.96 at the high-reward extreme and 0.25–0.29 at mid-range rewards of 0.35–0.50;
a model × G grid confirms the monotone effect at interior accuracy, Qwen3-32B moving
0.33 → 0.18 → 0.08 across G = 4/8/16; and a toy-scale open-backward-pass experiment
finds corr(∥∇∥, p(1−p)) = +0.71 on a 0.5B model, which the P7 report labels directional
only and which cannot be recomputed from the retained artefacts. Where P3’s sweep
could not find an optimum, P7 supplies a mechanism under which a shallow one should
exist. That is a reconciliation, not a resolution: the mechanism is checked in telemetry,
and its causal link to the gradient remains toy-scale.
The same report is clear about the control it does not have. A micro-jitter test collapses
the exact-tie fraction from 0.158 to 0.000 while barely changing PCD, but the declared
variance-threshold ZVF remains 0.158 (§6.3.1). It therefore does not establish supe-
riority of PCD over tolerance-based ZVF. In a four-arm open-trainer audit, adaptive
group size matches the best fixed-recipe held-out gain, +0.575, at 186 rollouts, while
DAPO-style dynamic sampling drives ZVF to 0.00 at +45% rollout cost — an accuracy-
versus-compute trade, not a controller win. In the larger 2,560-observation controller
trace, 1,723 of 1,867 escalation events, 92.3%, occur on all-correct groups and only
144 on all-wrong, so a symmetric ZVF trigger spends most of its budget on observed
all-correct groups. That share is what the reward level alone predicts (91.5% under per-
prompt binomial draws), and the trace is a counterfactual replay in which no controller
ran. Eight successes do not prove latent p = 1, and the plug-in simulation’s zero rescue
is imposed by its estimator. Deleting those fires remains counterfactual because perfor-
mance preservation was not measured. Adaptive group size is therefore not promoted
to a recommended policy.
A separate frozen-checkpoint diagnostic now observes fresh responses directly (§7.4;
Appendix B §B.12): 33/461 initially homogeneous groups became reward-mixed,
7.16% [5.14%, 9.88%]. Twenty first changes are strict-parser format failures with the
same visible gold number; only 13/461 (2.82%) are assistant-audited numeric con-
trasts, comprising two recoveries and eleven new failures. The format effect belongs to
this diagnostic contract, not the repository’s more permissive historical evaluator. The
numeric witnesses reject certainty from eight equal observations on this new check-
point/task/decoder, but supply no historical-checkpoint reproduction, training or con-
troller benefit, group-size optimum, or held-out capability estimate from the GSM8K
train split.
P5 and P6 supply the corpus’s methodological claim, and they contain its sharpest in-
ternal tension. P5’s headline exhibits are a nominally matched-configuration backend
audit whose final training rewards span 5.0% to 85.6% — a 17× same-label swing the


                                           173
P5 report declines to assign to the backend, because the managed stack also silently
pinned a different base checkpoint (Qwen3-8B-Base on Tinker against the instruction-
tuned Qwen3-8B on TRL); on the same Tinker harness the checkpoint change alone
accounts for 64% of the gap, and reward function, template, decoding, batch, data, loss
and optimiser also differ (§7.1) — and a pair of runs carrying the same “DAPO” label
in which an open trainer with true dynamic sampling produced mean ZVF 0.00 while a
closed stack running an asymmetric-clip surrogate produced mean ZVF 0.58, although
those two runs also differ in model, task, group size and steps, and the open trainer’s
0.00 holds by construction. Alongside them sits a third exhibit: a 12-cell head-to-head
of four labelled algorithms on one fixed stack, landing within a 0.034 band with mean
last-10 reward 0.710–0.744, a spread the P5 report calls comparable to seed-level vari-
ation; with three seeds per arm the band does not establish equivalence (TOST fails at
every margin up to ±0.15 unpaired; §7.1). P5’s framing is that a label-only compari-
son can hide stack-level variation, and that the converse also holds: when the stack is
genuinely fixed, the labels stop separating. P6 reads the same exhibit in the reverse di-
rection, using it to motivate a schema while explicitly declining to re-claim it as novel
empirical science; on the twenty seed entries the MIN-REPORT auditor badge spans
43–96, and stackdiff flags the open-versus-closed “DAPO” pair as an R5 label-flip risk
from manifests alone. The registry directory now holds 28 stack entries. The revised
stackdiff compares 48 fields and raises a level only on known differences; it flags the
17× exhibit at R5 on checkpoint, reward and template, but 342 of 378 pairs are under-
specified, so it is an implemented reporting check rather than a validated risk predictor
(§7.2).
Two gaps should be named. The first is that P7’s controller audit and P5’s stack-
attribution programme have not been joined: the controller is evaluated on open trainers
where the update rule is auditable, while the standard is motivated by a closed-stack di-
vergence that cannot be audited. The second is P8, the credit-card fraud side-probe, ab-
sorbed here as a measurement-discipline case study and nothing more. Its two AUCs are
not a like-for-like ranking: XGBoost’s 0.7955 and the Qwen3.5-4B SFT arm’s 0.48268
on a positive-enriched 500-row held-out evaluation sit on different held-out splits by
construction.
The E1–E14 campaign is a different instrument and should be read as one. Its figures
have unequal source accessibility. The six original files covering five missing basenames
listed in Appendix B §B.11 are now preserved in a withheld source archive, while re-
maining absent from the frozen checkout. Supplementary read-only payload checks
corroborate stored E8/E10 counts and E14 disposition/denominator reporting; they do
not rerun a model or scorer, independently judge correctness, establish content decon-
tamination or recover the missing training logs. A single Tinker-trained actor — base


                                          174
Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B at commit 995ad96e…, with adapter campaign-adapter at
commit withheld-adapter-revision…, in bfloat16 — was evaluated against fourteen
suites outside its recorded training-suite list, under per-lane native evaluation contracts.
The recovered AgentDojo receipt binds version v1.2.2 but no upstream commit; the
broader revision claims retain their source-specific limits (§8.2). Three scopes closed
strictly complete: two replacement scopes, LAB-Bench public at 1,967/1,967 across
eight categories and AgentDojo benign utility with 97/97 episodes completed and utility
88/97 = 0.907 (Wilson 95% CI [0.833, 0.950]), and one original-contract suite, Verilo-
gEval, at 312/312 with 129/312 = 41.35% pass@1 canonical (design-cluster bootstrap
95% CI [34.3%, 48.7%], resampling 156 paired designs (§8.2)). Omni-MATH closed
terminal-complete under its native protocol, with all 4,428 dispositions recorded, 4,426
accepted, and an accuracy of 2,271/4,428 = 51.29% with the two unjudged rows counted
as failures (the stored native-score fields give 51.31% over the 4,426 judged rows). The
recovered terminal note incorrectly associates that decimal with denominator 4,428; the
thesis retains the corrected arithmetic and preserves the source unchanged (Table E.1;
sources: recovered E14 terminal note and stored-payload source audit, Appendix
B §B.11). Two suites carry full-coverage scores under their original contracts: SWE-
bench Pro at 2/731 = 0.274% pass@1 (Wilson 95% CI [0.08%, 0.99%]) and VerilogEval,
the same E11 result counted once. Both, and Omni-MATH, are dominated by the har-
ness configuration: 593 of 713 SWE-bench Pro patches were corrupt for git apply,
and the VerilogEval and Omni-MATH scores are bound by their generation budgets
(§8.2, §8.3). Six further replacement scopes were run in full on 27 September 2026 (Ta-
ble 8.C): CORE-Bench hard at 27/45 = 0.600, Tau3 at 8/97 = 0.082, WebArena at 90/812
= 0.111 (a lower bound, with 108 judge-dependent tasks ungraded), ML-Dev-Bench at
10/34 = 0.294, BALROG at 26.1% progression over 255 episodes, and SWE-bench Mul-
tilingual at 1/190 on the tasks not attempted before, kept separate from the earlier 4/110.
Lane scope and status are tabulated once, in Table C.3. The rest are partial or externally
blocked, each with a terminal state and a named gate.
What the campaign establishes is descriptive, and its scope matters. For this actor, at
this revision, under each suite’s own evaluator and denominator, these are the scores. It
is not a post-training delta: no per-suite base-model comparator appears in the campaign
ledger at the campaign’s own scopes, so no campaign number, high or low, is evidence
about what the GRPO step contributed. The one paired comparison of trained actor and
base model is the 26 September small-scale rerun (Table 8.B): 14 lanes, 5 to 151 identi-
cal items each, one serving engine. It finds no significant difference in any lane, which
at those sizes is inconclusive rather than evidence of no effect. The surviving E4 prior
_base_run secondary record reports a BankerToolBench base-model rerun with mean
reward 0.0 across 100 trials. The recovered finish ledger explicitly says the rerun was


                                            175
not yet launched at its 21 September snapshot, so it is not evidence for the later comple-
tion. The bridge finding documents omitted stop sequences and reports invented turns;
the detailed historical claims of termination after 2–14 steps without deliverables/
output remain unverified against the still-unrecovered raw job receipt and trajectories.
One corrected-path task achieved positive reward, supporting a concrete harness defect,
but task, serving path and caps changed together; the fraction of the 100-trial zero at-
tributable to that defect is unidentified. The result describes that model-plus-harness
configuration (Section 8.5; sources: outputs/e1_e14_small_scale_2026-09-26/E
4/result.json, prior_base_run; outputs/e1_e14_small_scale_2026-09-26/
E4/BRIDGE_STOP_TOKEN_FINDING.md).
What governance bought is visible in the ledger itself: eleven of eleven named deter-
ministic checks passing on 2026-09-19 (scope in Appendix B), and the recovered finish
ledger preserving a historical snapshot recording E7 as “1/28 errored” visibly beside the
superseding table’s “1/46 attempted”, without inventing a reconciliation.



9.2     Rigour and Threats to Validity

What rigour was exercised. Diagnostics are recomputable from stored raw tensors, and
headline structural claims were re-verified by eleven named deterministic code checks
on 2026-09-19, all passing, covering exact divisions, receipt presence, sealed-artifact
hash equality and lost-source absence probes. Held-out n is reported alongside every
gain, and failed runs are counted rather than hidden: failed attempts remain part of the
stated limitations.
Table 9.1 lists the threats discussed below, grouped as internal, construct and external,
with the mitigation applied to each. Most mitigations narrow what is claimed rather than
repair the evidence.
Threats to validity and the mitigation applied to each.

Statistical power: single-seed Tinker experiments. Most Tinker configurations ran
once, because cost precluded repetition, and those single-seed runs carry no variance
estimates or significance tests. The exceptions are stated where they occur. They are
the three-seed-per-arm managed head-to-head (Chapter 7 §7.1), the five-seed Qwen3-
8B held-out control (p = 0.26, Chapter 6 §6.5.4) and the Qwen3-8B scale extension.
Henderson et al. (2018) motivate reporting variability across random seeds; no uni-
versal seed-count threshold guarantees power. A follow-up needs a prespecified effect
size and power rationale. The seed counts are: one seed for most Tinker runs and all
P1 anchors; three for the P3 sweep, the P4 GSM8K-CoT and scale-extension arms and
the managed head-to-head; five for the same-stack PPO/GRPO contrast, the P4 arith-
metic arms and the Qwen3-8B pre/post control; and eight for the variant audit (P11).
The limitations document says the ten-seed bar was met for the TRL baseline and the
GSM8K evaluation and then that the evaluation was cut to five seeds; the run records
in Appendix A show five seeds for both the TRL Modal campaign (Table A.3) and the
GSM8K pre/post evaluation. The 30B MoE scaling analysis uses three seeds; the boot-
strap confidence intervals assume independent observations, so any positive correlation
from shared initialisation or data ordering would make the reported intervals too nar-
row, and the true intervals wider; and secondary analyses carry uncorrected p-values.
Quantitative conclusions therefore come from the open-source side, where every hyper-

                                         178
parameter is auditable, while Tinker figures are descriptive.
Horizons of 30–50 steps. The Tinker runs are early-training snapshots; long-horizon
effects such as reward hacking, catastrophic forgetting and late-stage policy collapse
are unlikely to manifest, and the source conclusion is direct: “Asymptotic claims are
unsupported”. This bounds results rather than vitiating them: P1’s failure to recover a
scaling law across roughly 2.4 orders of magnitude is a bounded negative, and P4’s drift
from 194.4 to 183.5 mean completion tokens is bounded by the 200-token cap.
Closed Tinker internals. The Tinker SDK exposes a custom loss path, a limited opti-
miser surface and standard LoRA hyperparameters, but not the exact server-side GRPO
loss formulation, the reward normalisation or baseline-subtraction scheme, minibatch
or gradient-accumulation strategy, hardware configuration, or system-level telemetry.
This applies unevenly: the inspected custom-loss Tinker reference loops define their
loss in client code (REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline, Table 4.2), while the
actor’s missing original execution source limits verification of its historical loss path
(Appendix D), whereas the runners that use Tinker’s built-in importance_sampling
loss leave its server-side details unrecorded. The consequence is best quoted: Tinker
results measure “the platform’s implementation of GRPO, not an abstract algorithmic
specification”. The illustrative case in the limitations document compares a pooled last-
10 training reward of 99.9% for Tinker GRPO with 73.4% for TRL GRPO on GSM8K.
Those figures are training rewards, not held-out accuracy, and they pool Tinker runs
on 4B–235B models against TRL on Qwen2.5-0.5B, so model size and implementation
are confounded; the source itself attributes part of the gap to the model difference. The
only same-model pair, Qwen3-8B, used the Base checkpoint on Tinker and the Instruct
checkpoint on TRL (Table A.2), so it does not remove the confound either. The threat
propagates into the campaign, whose actor was trained on Tinker. The SDK is also
a moving target: Phase 1 pinned tinker==0.16.1, while the September 2026 bridge
required tinker==0.30.0 after the older SDK produced a no-longer-supported error.
Per-suite native denominators are not comparable. Every E1–E14 result is expressed
on its own suite’s denominator, and those denominators differ in size, in what they count
and in what they exclude. SWE-bench Pro is 2/731, whose denominator retains 14 gen-
eration failures and 4 artifact losses alongside 713 native evaluations, 593 of which ran
on the unpatched base because the submitted patch was corrupt for git apply (§8.3);
APEX-Agents is 7/480; E9 MLE-bench is 40/75 competitions natively graded, stated as
53.33% coverage with a null suite score, because coverage and score are different quan-
tities; E1’s replacement SWE-bench Multilingual is 1/190 on one contract and 4/110 on
another, never summed to a 300-task figure; E6 WebArena is 90/812 with 108 tasks un-
graded, so its denominator counts items that were never graded; E13 BALROG is a mean
of six per-environment means rather than a rate over episodes; and E14’s Omni-MATH


                                          179
records 4,428 dispositions of which 4,426 were accepted, with the headline diagnostic
computed over all 4,428 (the stored native score’s 51.31% uses the 4,426 judged rows;
the recovered terminal note’s contrary denominator label is corrected in Table E.1). A
41.35% on one denominator and a 0.274% on another do not license a comparison, an
average or a ranking, and none is offered; the rule holds within lanes too, and the ledger
states it flatly: “N/A means unverified; no pooled results”.
No cross-suite aggregate. No mean, median or composite index over suites appears
anywhere in this report or in the campaign materials it draws on: an aggregate over
native denominators would be a number with no referent, mixing suites that measure
different capabilities at different difficulties under different scoring rules. The cost is that
the campaign has no single headline; the three strictly complete scopes, the terminal-
complete Omni-MATH scope, the six replacement scopes completed on 27 September
and the two original-contract scores are the most the record supports.
The E10 benign-utility scope says nothing about adversarial robustness. The E10
lane’s completed evidence is a benign-utility evaluation of 97/97 completed tasks with
utility 88/97 = 0.907 under AgentDojo’s benign scope; its original contract, private
AgentHarm tasks, is externally blocked and carries a closure record with a named reopen
condition. The completeness result therefore carries no adversarial-robustness measure-
ment, and this report makes no such claim for the trained actor. The lane has a dual-use
dimension, though the limitations analysis finds the marginal uplift of the released arte-
facts small, because the base model’s capability is overwhelmingly pre-existing: the
pre-RL Qwen3-8B-Instruct checkpoint already scores 82.0% on held-out GSM8K, and
full GRPO adds only +1.3 points to 83.3%, not significant at p = 0.26. The 82.0% is the
same Instruct checkpoint before RL, not a separate base model.
The retained E11 result came through the original sampler. The 129/312 full-run
receipt attributes its samples to the managed Tinker sampler rather than a locally served
copy. Private execution and repository access identities are omitted, so this public pack-
age cannot replay the original route. A different serving stack would be a protocol change
with unmeasured same-weights, different-sampler divergence. The separate four-task
step-40 pilot has different source and decontamination limitations and is not substituted
for the full run.
Known evidence loss in the finish-era recovery source. The record is explicit:
the historical source-loss incident recorded in withheld historical source-loss
chronology removed the execution sources of the finish-era recovery and build code —
the E1 wave-10 controller, the E9 restored dependencies and the E6 validator — while
sealed requests, validation receipts and test logs survive with matching hashes, and the
replacement lanes’ native runners survive under zvf-program/flagship/. The lost-
source policy was applied rather than waived: a faithful re-implementation was written

                                             180
into a tracked path under zvf-program/e1_wave10/, where it verifies 10/10 targeted
regressions offline (test_process_group.py 4/4, test_cleanup_regressions.py
6/6). Two consequences follow: a sealed request without its execution source is not a re-
run, so the E9 MLDevBench runtime recipe was never reconstructed (the 27 September
run built a fresh environment on Google Cloud instead); and the policy response — ex-
ecution sources henceforth live under tracked paths — cannot restore what was already
lost. A related item: the Jev (TypeSafe) judgment harness lost reliability from roughly
13:10Z on 2026-09-19, both blocker-classification batteries returning degenerate distri-
butions and a controlled yes-probe falling from about 1.0 to 0.3; the receipts are retained
as incident evidence and the batteries must be re-run once the health check passes, so
the ledger’s first-uncleared-blocker column stands on document evidence rather than
judgment output.
Scope and confound inventory. Several Phase-1 threats remain live. Many early runs
logged train-set rewards only, so time-series comparisons spanning old and new runs
must account for the instrumentation change. Initial phases used LoRA at ranks 8–64
exclusively; historical baseline comparisons use default hyperparameters from the Tin-
ker cookbook and the TRL documentation; and no full learning-rate × rank × batch
× group-size grid was swept. Tinker, Modal, Colab and the PES A100 node use dif-
ferent accelerators, memory hierarchies and training stacks, so observed differences in
reward dynamics are not purely algorithmic, and the work was not pre-registered. The
model-scale range is 0.6B–1T, with the anchors above 235B single-seed and descriptive
(Chapter 6 §6.1); the limitations record’s “reliable” range of 0.6B–235B is the multi-run
part of that roster, so dense-70B and trillion-parameter MoE extrapolations are unreli-
able; benchmark coverage is narrow and biased toward verifiable rewards such as exact
match, unit tests and schema validity, with no human preference data, so findings should
not be extended to reward-model-based RLHF; and the work was carried out at one in-
stitution in Bengaluru, so benchmark choices and evaluation design reflect that context.
The resource accounting is itself a threat. The report estimates roughly 60 A100 GPU-
hours of in-kind PES University compute and projects environmental use of about 776
kWh and about 296 kg CO2 -eq; the dominant uncertainty is the Tinker GPU-hour count,
inferred because the platform exposes no hardware telemetry, giving a pessimistic Tinker
contribution of about 540 kg against an optimistic one of about 130 kg. The mitigation
offered is reproducibility by artefact.




                                           181
9.3     Conclusions

This report set out to build a multi-framework training harness for GRPO-style post-
training of large language models and to study that post-training under a single method-
ological rule: hold the stack fixed, change one factor, report what happens. The harness
dispatches one configuration to several back-ends, but only Tinker and TRL produced
completed runs, on different base checkpoints, so it is not a completed multi-framework
benchmark. The four research questions of §1.4 have the following answers.

    • RQ1 (ZVF). Yes, it is recomputable: the three stored Qwen3-8B tensors repro-
      duce 0.130, 0.190 and 0.155 exactly. Its predictive validity remains unestab-
      lished: the historical outcome and collapse correlations mix synthetic and mea-
      sured records, and the outcome is not a common held-out measure (§6.3; Table
      1.1). Its magnitude is regime-dependent, from 0.16 to 0.84 across selected mea-
      sured cohort means.
    • RQ2 (algorithm labels on a fixed stack). Answered only in part, and nega-
      tively. PPO versus GRPO, GRPO versus Dr. GRPO and G ∈ {2, 4, 8, 16} all sit at
      evaluation accuracy 0.98–0.99 on 0.5B two-digit arithmetic, and nulls at ceiling
      are not evidence of equivalence: they cannot separate no effect from no head-
      room. The unsaturated GRPO/Dr. GRPO comparisons (Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-
      CoT; the eight-seed variant audit of §1.2) are inconclusive and establish no equiv-
      alence either (§6.2.3, §6.4, §6.5; Table 1.1). The same-stack rerun on the unsat-
      urated Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT configuration answers part of it, negatively.
      Its first version (v1: 200-token cap, cold critic, 200 test items, five seeds) showed
      GRPO at G = 8 exceeding PPO by +0.050 [+0.015, +0.085], but its PPO arm fin-
      ished below its starting accuracy (0.195 against 0.200) and the sign-flip p after
      Holm adjustment was 0.19. The corrected v2 rerun (512-token cap, warm-started
      critic, full 1,319-item test split, independent re-grade agreeing on every item) su-
      persedes it: GRPO G8 − PPO is +0.0076 [−0.0034, +0.0185] over seven paired
      seeds, equivalent within ±0.02 (TOST p = 0.016); G8 − G2 is +0.0079 [+0.0002,
      +0.0156] over ten seeds, not significant after Holm adjustment (p = 0.14); and
      G2 − PPO is +0.0004, from a base accuracy of 0.654. Once the token cap and
      critic are fixed there is no detectable GRPO advantage over PPO on this config-
      uration. The figures are provisional while three PPO seeds and the pre-specified
      exposure-matched PPO arm complete (§6.5.5, Table 6.2).
    • RQ3 (manifest-level flip risk). Yes, as a reporting check. The revised stackdiff
      flags both exhibits from their records alone — the 17× pair at R5 on checkpoint,
      reward and template (coverage 0.58) and the “DAPO” pair at R5 on checkpoint,
      reward, template, dataset and evaluation metric (coverage 0.87) — and reports

                                           182
      missing information separately; 342 of the 378 pairs are underspecified because
      16 entries are hand-entered (§7.2). Whether its flags predict outcome differences
      was not tested.
    • RQ4 (governance-first evaluation). It yields receipt-bound per-suite scores and
      an explicit terminal state for every lane (Table C.3). It does not yield a post-
      training delta: the actor had 40 training steps, and the paired Table 8.B comparison
      is inconclusive in every lane.

On the analytical side the contribution is a measurement layer and a set of bounded
negatives. The Zero-Variance Fraction is a reproducible diagnostic of advantage-signal
starvation, recomputable from stored tensors, regime-dependent in magnitude (0.72–
0.77 on the Phase-1 main configuration, 0.16 to 0.84 across selected measured cohort
means; Table 1.1), with predictive validity unestablished. The historical pooled asso-
ciation (𝜌 = 0.27, 95% CI [−0.37, 0.88], reported n = 23) includes simulation and in-
consistent outcome definitions, and actually uses seventeen finite cells; it is withdrawn
as evidence and cannot establish a measured held-out relationship. Its theory has been
made falsifiable, and two of its three consequences are observed — the U-shape across
reward levels and the monotone group-size effect — but both follow largely from the
binomial identity once prompt difficulty varies (a beta-binomial with intraclass correla-
tion 0.16 predicts 0.170 against 0.158 observed; §6.3.3), so they do not independently
validate it; the gradient link remains toy-scale, directional and unrecomputable. The
same quantity is studied in contemporaneous work as the Advantage Collapse Rate [31]
and as gradient starvation [32], so ZVF is reported as instrumentation, not as a new di-
agnostic. Group size therefore has a candidate mechanism, but the measured three-seed
sweep found its arms statistically indistinguishable and establishes no optimum (Chap-
ter 6 §6.4). Length bias is not established: the roster-level flag that marks four of eleven
GRPO runs — peak before 65% of training, terminal reward below 90% of peak — has
post hoc thresholds, is not specific to length, and fires on most runs in the wider trace
pool, so it is not evidence of length bias; in the controlled comparison the same rule
fires on both GRPO and Dr. GRPO (five of six GSM8K-CoT runs) while no run shows
length growing, completion length drifts shorter, and the two are indistinguishable on
held-out gain (Chapter 6 §6.5). No claim is made that Dr. GRPO is uniquely free of
the pattern. The levers we could test — a naive curriculum, a matched-token-budget
intervention, an integer group-size sweep — did not produce reliable held-out gains.
The earlier same-stack de-confounds (PPO versus GRPO, GRPO versus Dr. GRPO, and
group size) were all measured on a saturated 0.5B arithmetic task with evaluation ac-
curacy near 0.99, so their nulls say that no difference was detectable there, not that
the methods are equivalent; the unsaturated Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT rerun supplies
PPO/GRPO and G = 2 versus G = 8 contrasts (§6.5.5). Its first version’s +0.050 GRPO

                                            183
G8-minus-PPO estimate came from a cold, untuned critic under a binding 200-token cap
and did not survive the corrected v2 rerun, which finds no detectable GRPO advantage
(equivalent within ±0.02, provisional), while the group-size contrast is not significant
after multiplicity adjustment. The strongest original contribution remains P5, the report-
ing standard, whose status the record states precisely: a targeted search of the GRPO,
model-reporting and provenance literature through July 2026 did not identify an equiv-
alent GRPO-specific machine-readable run datasheet with rollout provenance, and this
is “an evidence-bounded novelty claim, not proof of non-existence”. The earlier sec-
ond bet — predictive layer freezing from step-1 behaviour — is weakened rather than
withdrawn: it held at 1.5B and collapsed to chance at 3B across four seeds, at a mean of
0.083 ± 0.048.
The E1–E14 campaign is an evaluation-governance contribution, not evidence about
GRPO: the actor received 40 steps of critic-free policy-gradient training (§8.1.1), so its
scores describe a checkpoint rather than a training method. It delivered receipt-bound
results on three strictly complete scopes (two replacements and one original contract),
with Omni-MATH terminal-complete; full-coverage original-contract scores on two (E1
and E11); and explicit lane states and remaining gates (Table C.3). Original-contract and
replacement-scope numbers are never pooled, no cross-suite aggregate is computed, and
no improvement over the base model is claimed for this campaign: the paired Table 8.B
comparison is inconclusive in every lane. The controlled Chapter 6 gains are separate,
stack-specific findings. VerilogEval’s 129/312 = 41.35% pass@1 at full coverage is
an end-to-end harness score. Of its 183 failures, 150 are extraction failures, plausibly
related to the 1,024-token cap with thinking enabled; missing per-sample finish reasons
prevent a definitive causal attribution. On a 50-item subset the same adapter scores 33/50
with thinking off at 4,096 tokens against 23/50 in this run, but engine and temperature
also changed. That comparison does not isolate the cap’s effect or replace the full-run
score. Operational source bindings remain outside this public derivative.
The report’s overall conclusion is about method rather than algorithms. On this compute
scale, and with these instruments, the measurements are informative while the algorith-
mic levers are noise-limited; the stack is a material part of the result, so a label without
its stack is not a reproducible experimental specification; and the most durable artefacts
produced here are the ones that make other people’s results checkable — the ZVF/PCD
diagnostic layer, the eight-item minimum reportable stack (seven manifest fields and one
evaluation item), the registry and its CLI, and a campaign ledger with named receipts
and gates, with recovered primary receipts preserved separately from the frozen check-
out and remaining raw-evidence and provenance limits stated explicitly (Appendix B
§B.11).




                                            184
9.3.1    C1 adds numerical witnesses and exposes reference limits

The C1 extension makes the evidence boundary more precise. Its frozen score is 11/64
on a deterministic first-64 subset, with a question-level Wilson 95% working-model
interval of 9.88-28.21%. Assistant-assisted reconciliation corroborates three numer-
ical recovery witnesses on that unchanged denominator. Three further events retain
convention-dependent interpretations, four are materially ambiguous and one matches
an incorrect checkpoint reference after eight correct initial answers. The frozen score is
preserved, not retrospectively repaired. Selection on parser zeros can enrich the refer-
ence faults and representation mismatches that prevent a direct mathematical-recovery
interpretation (Appendix G, sources S1-S5).
These three witnesses show that eight observed wrong numerical answers do not force
future numerical answers to remain wrong for this frozen checkpoint, task and decoder.
They do not validate the reasoning in a successful response: Santana’s earliest successful
number was reached by an incorrect argument. They do not measure a gradient, train-
ing update, held-out capability or controller benefit. Reference auditing and unit-aware
evaluation therefore belong in the measurement design before a future intervention is
interpreted. The full 64-case audit retains thirteen category disagreements and provides
no whole-benchmark error-prevalence estimate. Historical V2 remains separate and un-
pooled.
The separately frozen parser-v3 candidate passes only its narrow four-change extraction
check in 2,048 prospectively selected future outputs. Preservation of 2,025 old-valid
objects and agreement on four new scalar extractions do not establish task correctness
or broad parser precision. The candidate remains undeployed; the C1 endpoint stays on
parser v2 (Appendix G, sources S8-S9).



9.4     Future Work

The work leaves three kinds of open question: evaluation lanes whose measurement is
incomplete, analytical claims that the present evidence could not settle, and method-
ological tools that have been built but not yet tested at scale.
Closing the remaining evaluation gaps. The six replacement lanes that were launch-
pending or quota-blocked on 21 September were run to completion on 27 September
2026 with the reported lane outcomes (Chapter 8 §8.4). Two measurement gaps re-
main in them. The 108 WebArena tasks that need an LLM judge are ungraded because
the judge was unavailable, and the native judge model has since been retired, so a re-
grade would record a judge substitution. SWE-bench Multilingual has been attempted
on all 300 tasks, but under two serving contracts, so a single 300-task figure would

                                           185
need the first 110 tasks rerun under the 27 September contract (32,768-token context
and the re-implemented source collector). Six original-contract scopes are recorded
CLOSED_EXTERNAL with written closure records: E3 (SDAB private bundle), E7 (Bi-
naryAudit private payload), E8-original (LifeSciBench package), E10-original (Agen-
tHarm private tasks), E12 (AppBench deployment) and E14-original (FrontierMath
hosted evaluation). A seventh original contract, E13’s OpenReward held-out games,
is also blocked on external access but has no closure record, because the lane contin-
ues through its BALROG replacement scope (Table C.3). A new evaluation would re-
quire the unavailable benchmark scope. A full BankerToolBench score for E4 would be
a new-runtime experiment; the recovered pending-state ledger recorded a grader-only
lower bound of approximately an undisclosed operational estimate before launch. The
later 100-trial zero-mean rerun is reported separately by outputs/e1_e14_small_s
cale_2026-09-26/E4/result.json (prior_base_run) and remains a confounded
diagnostic with its raw job receipt unrecovered, not a suite capability estimate or a result
substantiated by that pending-state ledger (§8.5).
Turning the campaign into a post-training measurement. The campaign reports
scores for one actor, not the contribution of the training step. The paired same-engine
comparison of §8.7 (Table 8.B) is the first base-model arm; scaling it from 5–151 items
per lane to the full native scopes, with more than one training seed and a longer training
run than the actor’s 40 steps, would turn each lane from a descriptive score into a post-
training delta. The original execution route is not reproducible from this public package.
A future controlled rerun on an explicitly documented serving stack would measure the
currently unmeasured sampler divergence.
Settling the analytical questions. Five Phase-1 directions remain open, each answering
a threat identified above:

    • adequately powered evaluation, moving from the Phase-1 probes’ held-out n of
      8–20 (the multi-seed controlled comparisons already use 200–500 items, Chapter
      1 §1.5) to the full GSM8K test split or a large stratified subset at five or more
      seeds;
    • a token-budget-optimal curriculum with staleness bounds, tested multi-seed;
    • testing whether the mid-to-late band in the per-layer gradient profile supports a
      FLOP-saving freeze schedule, even though step-1 predictability did not survive
      scaling;
    • hardening the Phase-1 method-trace classifier on grouped run-level and temporal
      splits rather than row-wise cross-validation;
    • consolidating the diagnostic findings and the reporting standard for publication.

Three more follow from the Chapter 6 results. The first is a length-confounded regime,

                                            186
with the completion cap lifted and longer horizons, in which a Dr. GRPO length advan-
tage would be expected to appear if it exists. The second is the head-to-head protocol
between ZVF and the pairwise-contrast density, to test whether PCD meets its target as-
sociation. The third extends the unsaturated same-stack rerun of §6.5.5: a PPO arm with
a warmed-up, tuned critic and generalised advantage estimation, so that the GRPO–PPO
gap can be separated from critic quality, and enough seeds or evaluation items to resolve
the G = 2 versus G = 8 contrast at a ±0.02 margin.
Testing the methodological tools. Three extensions come from the Semester-4 work:

    • a pre-registered controller bakeoff against static G = 16 and naive boundary heuris-
      tics, the test P7 declines to claim in advance of;
    • a cross-framework divergence study that runs an identical task, reward, decoding
      and seed configuration across TRL, veRL, OpenRLHF and Tinker, turning P5’s
      attribution argument from an audit of existing runs into a designed experiment;
    • dogfooding the registry schema on the campaign’s own runs, so that every future
      benchmark result ships with a verifiable provenance record.

Two commitments carry forward, because the threats above are structural rather than
incidental. The first is the never-pool rule: original-contract and replacement-scope
numbers stay separate, and per-suite native denominators are never averaged. The sec-
ond is that failures are reported with their mechanism, as the E11 sampler path, the E4
zero, the lost execution sources and the seed-count discrepancy are here.

    198
Appendix A

Historical Run Registry and Coverage
Reconciliation

This appendix records the historical run inventories used by the report and the later rec-
onciliation of those inventories. It is not an exhaustive current account history. Provider
logging identities, independent scientific executions, checkpoint revisions, and claim-to-
run links are different units and must not be added as though they were interchangeable.
Run identifier, library and platform, base model, algorithm, group size, dataset, seed,
steps, and terminal status are retained where a primary source records them; unknown
fields remain unknown. A claim without supporting primary evidence must be qualified
or withdrawn rather than inferred from an inventory count.
Historical inventories differ in scope and counting units. Provider-ledger entries,
opening-study records, claim links and independent scientific trials are not interchange-
able. This public derivative omits all account-wide totals, project breakdowns and exact
provider identities. Only scientific study configurations, denominators, outcomes and
limitations are retained; it makes no complete-coverage claim.



A.1      Historical boundary and source scope

The Semester-3 boundary is the public tag capstone-final-2026-04-25, commit
21a99ef7 dated 23 April 2026. Attribution of inherited and individual work rests on
commit history, not provider-account ownership or activity counts. The study tables
below retain scientific configurations, seeds, steps, outcomes and evidence tiers. Oper-
ational identifiers are withheld; locally named scientific study units remain for interpre-
tation.

mon harness, a fixed evaluation configuration, and integrations for several independent
RL post-training libraries. These runs are the shared baseline measurements, and they
are the ones for which the registry is most complete, because each was produced under
a declared configuration.

Three facts here are load-bearing. First, no empirical headline row in the P1–P8 ledger
reaches Tier A or B under the statistical-rigor thresholds; the rectified P1–P8 tally is
C = 12, R = 2, X = 4. P11 adds an eight-seed matched block but does not meet the
stated Tier A step threshold, as clarified below. Second, the four Tier X rows are X for
specific, checkable reasons: P3-C2 rests on G = 32 sampling cells with zero optimizer
steps, so no matched G = 4/G = 32 training replication exists; P4-C1’s abstract names

                                                           205
Qwen3-8B while every mapped run is Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct; P5-C1 com-
pares Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base against Qwen/Qwen3-8B, so the base checkpoint changes
together with the backend and the pair must not be cited as a backend-only causal effect;
and P8-C3 is a proposed architecture with no execution, hence zero linked runs. Third,
some cross-provider model-identity conflicts remain unresolved. Their private identity
reconciliation is outside this public package; the affected scientific model-attribution
claims remain contested.
Two column caveats travel with the table. The dataset cell for P7-C1 is — because
the ledger’s only dataset-adjacent field for those 368 runs is a prompt/tokenizer family
(llama3, qwen3_instruct, gpt_oss_low_reasoning, nemotron3_disable_think
ing), not a dataset name. And the P3 sweep here is not the Semester-3 sweep of Table
A.4: it is a different project (study logging group), a different model (Qwen3.5-4B),
and it reports deltas of 0.0000 rather than held-out accuracies near 0.99. The two share a
group-size grid and nothing else. Chapters 4 and 6 cite the Table A.4 sweep (Qwen2.5-
0.5B) whenever they give the 0.838 → 0.631 ZVF decline or the 0.98–0.99 held-out
figures. They do not cite P3-C1.
The extension ledger added in August 2026 carries five rows besides P11: P10-C1 is
Tier R (an algebraic identity, not an empirical result), P10-C2 and P12-C2 are Tier X
(placeholder figures and unlinked prospective claims, zero runs), and P12-C1 is Tier R
as a lemma and Tier X as an empirical headline.
P11 is an eight-seed, matched-stack block of 40 arm–seed units at local:zvf-progra
m/audit/results/full/{grpo,dapo,gspo,drgrpo,aero}-seed-{11,23,37,53
,71,89,107,131}.json, five arms × eight seeds on one stack (Qwen3-8B, GSM8K,
30 steps, Colab A100, held-out n = 500). Its headline contrast, DAPO versus GRPO, is
Δ = +0.001, percentile-bootstrap CI [−0.0045, +0.00675] (paired-t 95% CI [−0.0063,
+0.0083], n = 8 seeds; the bootstrap undercovers at this n), MDE80 = 0.01012, all raw
p > 0.21, with Benjamini–Hochberg rejecting nothing. The mechanism is visible in the
diagnostic: DAPO’s mean ZVF is 0.000 against GRPO’s 0.693, bought with 3.61× the
rollouts (1,734 versus 480) and 1.44× the wall clock. This eight-paired-seed contrast
supports a null. Every retained P11 final manifest specifies max_steps=30; the 40 units
are 30-step runs. Accordingly this block does not satisfy the stated Tier A requirement
of at least 100 steps per run and remains Tier C under the vocabulary above. Pooling
steps across arms or seeds does not satisfy that per-run requirement. This corrects the
evidence tier, not the recorded rewards, seed counts, interval, or null result.




                                           206
A.3.1        Opening and null-result runs

Chapter 6 reports four null families — a mixed-variance curriculum, a matched token
budget, hard-prompt filtering, and step-1 layer selection (§6.4.3) — and each rests on
source-specific scientific result records rather than an account-wide inventory. The study
The rows are counted from the per-run name (or mode) fields of each artefact’s JSON
— results.json for the curriculum, token-budget, hard-prompt and loss-formulation
blocks, result.json and its siblings for P1 — not from an inventory API, which is
why this cohort is reported as its own total. Three neighbouring blocks add nothing to
it: the P3 group-size artefacts are already indexed — groupsize_zvf/results.json
reproduces the per-group deltas of Table A.5’s P3-C1 row exactly, and the second sweep
file is discussed below — and the P2, P7 and P8 “openings” (p2_openings/collaps
e_baseline_analysis.json, p7_controller/sim.json, p8_openings/*.json)
are zero-compute re-analyses that launch no runs.
Semester-4 opening and null-result runs (46 runs). Model is Qwen/Qwen3.5-4B and
dataset GSM8K unless a row says otherwise. All rows are Tinker-managed except the
P1 layer-freeze block, which ran on Colab for per-layer gradient access. “Curriculum”
means training only on non-collapsed (mixed-variance) groups. Group sizes marked
default are the launch defaults of the scripts in platform_hybrid/experiments/op
enings/; only the campaign artefact records a group size per run. Δ is the change in
held-out accuracy.
                  Table A.6: Semester-4 opening and null-result runs: the curriculum, token-budget, hard-
                  prompt, loss-formulation and layer-freeze blocks.

Three provenance notes belong with this table, because two of its blocks carry super-
seded siblings and one carries a refuted claim. First, campaign/FINDINGS.md describes
a nine-run, three-seed version of the campaign on a twelve-prompt held-out set with
arm means of +0.028 and −0.028; campaign/results.json holds fifteen runs over
six seeds on a thirty-prompt held-out set (0.8667 = 26/30), and the table follows the
per-run file. The FINDINGS warning that zero_loss_frac was invalid (an empty-
metrics-dict bug, “since fixed”) belongs to the nine-run batch, not to that file. Second,
the P1 layer-freeze block has the same shape: the two-seed 3B pass (overlap 0.1111 ±
0.0000) is superseded by the four-seed pass (0.0833 ± 0.0481), and both sit near chance,
so the toy-scale overlap of 1.0 must not be read as a transferable layer-selection signal.
Third, the two single-seed training blocks flagged SUSPECT by independent verifica-
tion (kimi, 2026-07-06) are superseded by the multi-seed blocks above: the curriculum
opening’s +0.05 (campaign curriculum and baseline arms separate by 0.006 over six
seeds, at 3.3–7.2× oversample cost), and a single-seed G = 4 gain of +0.125 in p3_gr
oupsize/sweep_results.json — the campaign’s G4 seed-0 baseline gains 0.0000,
not 0.125, and that sweep file retains only two of its twelve entries, so the W&B group
is its record.

A.4          E1–E14 evaluation campaign lanes

The fourteen evaluation lanes are not training runs; they are evaluations of a single
frozen actor, and they carry the registry’s strictest receipt discipline. The actor is
base Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B (commit 995ad96e…) with adapter campaign-adapter
(commit withheld-adapter-revision…), bfloat16, trained on Tinker, run seed 809
taken from the adapter name. The adapter was trained for 40 steps at G = 4 and learning
rate 1 × 10−5 by REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline (no KL, no clipping), as
recorded in the local W&B run directory for run [identifier withheld] and the run receipt
withheld actor-training receipt (Chapter 8 §8.1.1). Those are properties of the
single actor rather than of any lane, and every lane carries the same run seed (809), so
algorithm, group size, seed and step columns are omitted from Table A.8. Terminal
states are from the ledger of record, withheld historical campaign chronology;
all scores are from outputs/E1_E14_FINAL_RESULTS_2026-09-19.md, except the
six rows marked “finish 2026-09-27”, which are from outputs/finish_pending_20

A.5      Completeness limitations

The historical registry is an index of its retained snapshot and is incomplete as a scientific
dataset. Many records lack model, dataset, algorithm, group size, steps or a usable
outcome. Missing fields remain unknown; names and provider state are not used to
infer them. The public version does not include the account-wide field-coverage table
or private per-identity export.
Scientific metadata limitations.

              Table A.10: Metadata limitations in the historical retained
              registry; private account-level field counts are omitted.


 Required scientific field                      Interpretation of missing data

 Model and dataset                              Do not infer identity from a display
                                                name or tokenizer family
 Algorithm and group size                       An unrecorded configuration remains
                                                unknown
 Training steps                                 Provider state does not establish
                                                completed optimization
 Outcome                                        A logged record without a metric is not a
                                                scored scientific result


Four further limits apply.

   1. Aggregate presentation. This appendix names 105 runs individually: the 4
      cross-framework runs of Table A.2, the 20 Modal campaign runs of A.3, the 12
      sweep runs (in 4 rows) of A.4, the 46 opening runs (in 16 rows) of A.6, and the
      23 E-lane rows of A.8 — plus the 18 claim rows of Table A.5, which index claims
      rather than runs. Other historical inventory entries are not published in this deriva-
      tive.



                                            213
   2. Non-runs inside the count. Three entries are not training results: verl-dryrun
      and openrlhf-dryrun are seeded deterministic fallbacks, and the E4 base-model
      rerun performs zero training steps; to these add §A.3.1’s P1 gradient probe, which
      takes no optimizer steps at all. They are counted because they are runs in the
      registry’s sense, and flagged so no reader treats them as measurements.

   3. Identity conflicts. Historical source-identity disagreements remain a limitation;
      private provider reconciliation and its counts are withheld.

   4. Snapshot scope. Historical source inventories differ in scope. They are neither
      current live accounts nor counts of independent experiments, and this public pack-
      age does not provide a complete inventory replay.

None of this changes a reported result, because every result in Chapters 6–8 is bound to
named artefacts rather than to the registry as a whole. It does bound what the registry
can be used for: it establishes provenance and identity, shows that the run population is
far larger than the reported claims, and shows that those claims rest on a small, explicitly
tiered subset with the P1–P8 empirical rows at Tier C or X and P11 at Tier C under the
stated per-run step threshold. The four Tier X P1–P8 rows must not be cited as supporting
evidence.
The run totals that appear in different chapters count different corpora. Table A.10 lists
the scientific study corpora retained publicly.
