E.1           Evidence-scope repairs after the 2 October review

The following corrections were made without new model training or regenerated histor-
ical receipts.

     • ZVF definition (§§4.3, 6.3.1, 7.1, 7.3). Exact ties and sample variance ≤ 10−6
       are now separate metrics. On the 600 stored groups, seeded micro-jitter changes
       exact ties from 95 to zero, but leaves 95 below the declared threshold. The epsilon
       sweep and source hashes are recorded in pcd_vs_zvf_tolerance_analysis.
       json.
     • Controller interpretation (§7.3). Zero rescue after plugging 𝑝ˆ = 0 or 1 into
       a Bernoulli model, or resampling eight identical rewards, is estimator-imposed.
       It does not show latent certainty or impossibility of recovery with independent
       continuations.
     • Loss provenance (§§1.1, 4.2–4.3, 6.3.1). Zero-KL claims are row-specific. TRL
       nonzero-KL configuration evidence, adaptive-KL defaults, managed unknowns
       and actor source-binding gaps remain visible; zero reward-relative advantage does
       not imply a zero full optimiser update.
     • PPO and group-size scope (§6.5.5; Chapter 9). The v1 unsaturated rerun’s
       +0.050 GRPO G8 − PPO gap (paired-t p = 0.016; exact p = 0.0625; Holm sign-
       flip p = 0.19) came from a binding 200-token cap and a cold critic whose PPO
       arm finished below its start (0.195 against 0.200). It is superseded by the v2 rerun
       (512-token cap, warm-started critic, 1,319 items): G8 − PPO +0.0076 [−0.0034,
       +0.0185], equivalent within ±0.02 (TOST p = 0.016); G8 − G2 +0.0079, Holm p
       = 0.14. Provisional pending three PPO seeds and the exposure-matched PPO arm
       (Table 6.2).
     • ZVF prior work and binomial identity (§6.3.3, §7.1, §7.3). The U-shape across
       reward levels is largely the binomial identity plus prompt-difficulty heterogene-
       ity (beta-binomial, intraclass correlation 0.16, predicts 0.170 against 0.158 ob-
       served); the 368-run audit spans nine model labels, not six; the 92.3% all-correct
       escalation share is predicted by the reward level (91.5%) and comes from a coun-

                                                         259
  terfactual replay in which no controller ran; the +0.71 gradient correlation cannot
  be recomputed. Contemporaneous prior work is now cited [31, 32].
• Stack exhibits (§7.1, §7.2). The 17× exhibit is decomposed: the Base → Instruct
  checkpoint change alone is 64% of the gap on one harness, with further listed
  confounds. The two “DAPO” runs are not a comparable pair. The 0.034 band
  is not an equivalence result (TOST fails). The registry holds 28 stack entries,
  not 20, and the revised stackdiff (48-field catalogue, levels only from known
  differences, 27 unit tests) replaces the committed version’s description.
• Citations (Chapter 2). The “A Sober Look” sentence now describes evaluation
  sensitivity rather than reward hacking; the GSM8K release figures distinguish the
  fine-tuned baseline from verification; an uncited MATH-500 accuracy claim is
  removed; the Nimmaturi and Tan claims carry page or appendix locations; the
  Stiennon entry carries its arXiv identifier.
• E11 uncertainty (§8.2; Appendix D). The point score stays 129/312. Resam-
  pling the 156 paired underlying designs gives a 95% percentile interval [34.3%,
  48.7%], replacing the iid-312 Wilson interval. The conditional extracted-only
  score remains descriptive.
• E4 mechanism (§8.5; Chapter 9). Missing stops are a verified defect, but the
  one corrected-path task changes several conditions together and cannot attribute
  the full 100-trial zero to stops alone.
• Source access (Appendix B §B.11; recovery update, 3 October). Six exact
  originals covering all five previously missing basenames were recovered from
  the Mac, including both pending-state snapshots, and preserved with hashes in
  a separate private package. All remain absent from the frozen checkout. Sup-
  plementary read-only checks corroborate stored E8/E10 grades and E14 dispo-
  sition/denominator reporting; they do not rerun scorers or establish content de-
  contamination. Training raw logs and E4 raw rerun evidence remain unresolved.
  The E14 denominator and E10/E4 attribution repairs above change no scientific
  counts or existing thesis denominator arithmetic.
• Fraud side-study intervals (§F.4). Sensor-minus-full AUC and accuracy inter-
  vals are [−0.0320, −0.0174] and [−0.0160, −0.0109], reversing and reordering
  the source’s opposite-direction intervals.




                                      260
Appendix F

Applied Side Study: Credit-Card
Fraud Detection (P8)

Scope statement. This appendix reports a side study. It uses synthetic data and sits
outside the RL contribution of this thesis. The dataset is generated with scikit-learn’s
make_classification; the language-model arm is trained by supervised fine-tuning,
not by reinforcement learning; and no result in this appendix is used to support any
GRPO, PPO, ZVF, group-size or campaign claim elsewhere in the report. The study
is retained, in condensed form, as the P8 slot of the internal report series and as one
worked example of measuring a claimed capability rather than accepting its label.
The study compares a gradient-boosted tree with a fine-tuned language model on credit-
card fraud detection. Its conclusion is that the tree keeps the real-time scoring seat, and
that the language model’s plausible roles lie in tasks the tree cannot perform at all.



F.1 Dataset, splits, and the two arms

The dataset is synthetic: make_classification (seed 42), 50,000 transactions, 20
anonymised numeric features V1–V20 (10 informative, 2 redundant), two clusters per
class, class separation 0.8 and flip_y=0.01, with a realised fraud rate of 1.44%. Four
per-row aggregates (mean, standard deviation, maximum, minimum) are appended, giv-
ing 24 features. The data reproduce class imbalance and anonymised feature shape, and
none of the temporal drift, verification latency or adversarial adaptation of real card-
fraud data.

                            Table F.1: The two arms of P8.

                               0.05,                         10,000 test rows, 144
                               subsample/colsample 0.8,      positives
                               scale_pos_weight=7;
                               no search
 Qwen3.5-4B SFT                TabLLM-style row              500-row positive-enriched
                               serialisation;                held-out subset at 20%
                               answer-token                  fraud, disjoint from
                               cross-entropy, no             training

                               63 steps at batch 32

F.2 The head-to-head result

             Table F.2: Head-to-head result on the synthetic card-fraud
             set, by arm and evaluation split.


     Arm                  Eval split               n         Accuracy ROC-AUC

     XGBoost (scorer) full test            10,000 n/a                    0.7955
     Qwen3.5-4B SFT positive-enriched test 500    0.7920                 0.4827


Source: platform_hybrid/experiments/results/quick_20260704/qp8_fraud.tsv.
The two AUCs sit on different held-out splits, so this is not a like-for-like ranking. The
language model’s AUC of 0.4827 is chance-level ranking: its Hanley–McNeil 95% CI,
with 100 positives and 400 negatives, is [0.42, 0.55] and includes 0.5. Its accuracy of
0.792 is slightly below the 0.800 that a constant “legitimate” answer would score at 20%
prevalence, so it is not evidence of discrimination. The tree’s AUC interval is [0.75,
0.84], with 144 positives among 10,000 test rows. The tree’s precision 0.723 (34/47,
Wilson 95% CI [0.58, 0.83]), recall 0.236 (34/144, [0.17, 0.31]) and F1 0.356 at the


                                           262
default threshold come from the released script’s own run (AUC 0.7942, 0.49 s training,
about 6 ms inference per 10,000 rows), which uses a slightly different configuration from
the 0.7955 artefact. An older record of 21 June 2026 (XGBoost AUC 0.975, language
model 0.948) could not be reconstructed and is not used.



F.3 Why the scoring seat stays with the tree

Four operational arguments hold even at AUC parity:

    • Latency. Card authorisation needs a score within tens of milliseconds; the tree
      takes about 6 ms per 10,000 rows, against roughly 300 ms for a language-model
      pass.
    • Cost. The released cost table puts the synchronous language-model path at about
      35 times the per-transaction cost of the tree path.
    • Calibration. Downstream threshold and expected-loss rules consume the score
      as a probability; verbalised language-model confidences are poorly calibrated.
    • Prompt injection. A model that reads attacker-controlled text fields in a syn-
      chronous decision loop opens an injection channel that a tree scoring numbers
      does not have.

F.5 Proposed roles for the language model

The study argues, without measuring, that the language model’s useful roles are tasks the
tree cannot enter: document and image fraud through a vision-language model, draft-
ing compliance narratives (Suspicious Activity Reports under 31 C.F.R. § 1020.320;

                                              264
adverse-action reasons under 12 C.F.R. § 1002.9), cold-start triage of new fraud typolo-
gies before labels exist, and post-score investigation of the alert queue. The proposed
architecture keeps XGBoost as the scorer and uses the language model as a sensor (typed
features, no authority) and a scribe (narratives conditioned on the tree’s own attributions,
with a human signature). The “85 times cheaper than an analyst” triage figure is an in-
ternal estimate from June-2026 token prices and assumed investigation times, and has
not been validated.



F.6 Limitations

The dataset is synthetic and single-generator, so no number here is a production expec-
tation, and there is no cross-institution or public-benchmark replication. The challenger
is one model family, one serialisation and one fine-tuning budget. Of the proposed roles,
only the scorer comparison carries a measurement of the study’s own; the others rest on
external literature and regulatory text, and nothing in the compliance discussion is legal
advice.




                                            265
Appendix G

C1 Evidence and Case Ledger

Benchmark reference errors, ambiguous questions and equivalent units limit what this
diagnostic can establish. Its frozen parser-defined result is 11/64 questions (17.19%;
Wilson 95% interval 9.88-28.21%). Assistant-assisted reconciliation corroborates
three numeric recovery witnesses on the same 64-question denominator. This 3/64
count (4.69%) describes the support found under a conservative review rule; it does
not replace the frozen endpoint or estimate a population recovery rate. The other eight
parser events include three convention-dependent interpretations, four materially am-
biguous cases and one match to an incorrect reference answer. [S1-S4]
The study contains no training update. It does not measure useful reinforcement-
learning gradients, reasoning improvement, controller superiority, held-out accuracy or
general model capability. The prompts are public GSM8K training questions and are
fresh only to this campaign. Historical V2 evidence remains separate and unpooled.

based selected index. Fresh indices identify all parser-success outputs among the 24
fresh draws. Complete question IDs, reference hashes, response hashes and review notes
accompany the editable case ledger. [S3]

C00 Santana presents. Gold 8 is (4 second-half birthdays+7 Christmas gifts)-3 March
gifts. Both reviews support the quantity. Fresh 12 matches 8 through a false 8-0 argu-
ment; fresh 15 and 23 give the correct 11-3 calculation.
C07 Suzanne bread. 3 slices per meal × 2 weekend meals × 52 weeks/12 slices per
loaf = 26. Sharing one slice does not require knowing the number of daughters. All 8
initial 90/91 claims use seven days per week; every fresh 26 uses the correct weekend
schedule.
C08 Citations. Other citations most naturally means the combined non-parking count:
L+L+2(L+L) = 24 gives 4. All initial 6 claims substitute twice one category. First treats
that as material ambiguity; second supports 4. Ordinary-reading support is stronger
than the per-category alternative, but disagreement remains and this is not added to the
corroborated recovery count.
C19 Pet owners. Half of pet owners plus disjoint exhaustive dog/cat ownership gives
100; half of all citizens gives 300 under the same partition. Overlap allows other values.
The denominator ambiguity is material even if ordinary classroom partition assumptions
are granted.
C20 Raft capacity. The ordinary exact additive-space model makes 14 jackets occupy
7 person-spaces; 8 jackets reduce 21 by 4, yielding 17. Arbitrary real packing/safety
possibilities do not by themselves constitute a defect. First conservatively withheld sup-
port; second accepts the conventional model. Retain that disagreement and conditional
event support only in the ledger.
C29 Fundraiser. Next 4 days naturally names a single comparison period, making
950 the four-day total and shortfall 475 per student. Initial 0 answers insert per day
before multiplying by 4; the text does not say each day. Retain the first alternative-scope
objection and second conventional support, not a revised recovery estimate.
C35 Checkpoints. Interior route checkpoints at mile 1 and mile 25 enclose 24 miles
and 3 intervening gaps, hence 8 miles. The closing 2-mile arc is outside those gaps.
All 8 initials give 8; all 4 gold matches 6 divide by 4 and are wrong. Screen 1 has an
internally contradictory parenthetical despite its correct final 8.
C37 Walking distance. Summed person-distance is 5 × 3 × 6+12+6 = 108; shared
outings counted once give 36. The aggregate quantity is genuinely different. Initial 0-6


                                            267
are correct under person-mile reading; initial 7 incorrectly repeats weekly extras and
gets 198. Neither gold-matching response proves the 108 answers wrong.
C38 Hank reading. 5 × (30+60)+2 × 2 × (30+60) = 810 minutes. Reference final
sentence mislabels subtotals but final 810 is correct. Initial 630/690/1350 claims are
wrong; fresh 2 visibly supplies the correct 450+360 calculation.
C47 Cake and ham. Cake-day overlap with any ham day during the week has prob-
ability 3/5 = 60%. A specified/randomly sampled test day has joint probability 3/25 =
12%. Both reviewer rounds retain sampling-unit ambiguity; all 5 fresh 12 matches use
the latter event.
C50 Reggie marbles. With starting Reggie count R and losses L,90 = R+10 × (9-2 L),
so R = 20 L. His friend’s 100 does not set R. Multiple feasible starts/losses exist. Gold’s
net-loss shortcut is invalid; all 3 fresh 1 matches have defective justifications. There is
no uniquely correct loss count.

Appendix I

Public Coverage and Source
Availability

This appendix is the publication-safe derivative of a bounded source audit completed
on 3 October 2026. It reports scientific source availability and limitations. It does not
publish authenticated provider inventories, private project names, account-wide activ-
ity totals, collaborator identities or operational access correspondence. The underlying
private audit records are withheld; these paragraphs do not turn their contents into inde-
pendently reproducible public evidence.



I.1    Counting units and publication boundary

A scientific execution, a logging record, a training identity, a checkpoint revision and
a claim-to-source link are different units. One execution can generate multiple records,
retries and checkpoint versions. These units must not be added as independent trials. Ap-
pendix A retains scientific study scope and qualitative registry limitations; no account-
wide inventory is included.
Provider inventory work used bounded pagination, immutable revisions where avail-
able, explicit source joins and missing-field handling. An inventory match establishes
recorded identity within the inspected scope, not successful training, benchmark cor-
rectness or availability of all historical weights. Unavailable or inaccessible records,
unresolved joins, unproven consumer-list exhaustion and missing history remain limita-
tions. Account-wide counts and exact operational identities are omitted.
The editable sources and selected scientific case ledgers in this package can rebuild the
public document. They cannot reproduce every experiment. The source-reference map
is a map of reported scientific sources, including references whose original payloads are


                                           292
retained outside this public package. The public availability manifest is the authority on
which files are actually included.



I.2     Bounded checkpoint and provider evidence

Training metadata, checkpoint bytes and an executable serving route are distinct ev-
idence. This public derivative does not publish private provider state, checkpoint-
retention findings or exact execution identities. The public package cannot reproduce
the original managed execution route, and no current account or private repository avail-
ability is asserted.
Public upstream model and benchmark revision references remain where scientifically
relevant. Published Git or LFS identifiers are metadata, not a fresh byte-integrity test of a
large tensor payload. Small public card, configuration and manifest checks do not estab-
lish numerical equivalence across serving stacks. No training, sampling or benchmark
evaluation was rerun by this source audit. Per-step metric histories were not reanalysed.



I.3     Public P11 confirmatory bindings

The forty public manifest bindings below belong to P11, the five-algorithm by eight-seed
confirmatory study. The repository naming convention includes e1, but these are not the
E1 SWE-bench lane of the later fourteen-suite campaign. At the recorded audit date, all
forty public pinned commits matched the public heads, and all forty downloaded run
manifests matched both their expected SHA-256 and retained local bytes. The compan-
ion public binding file retains scientific units, immutable commit and checksum fields.
Execution-specific repository access paths are omitted to avoid publishing embedded
provider identifiers; this limits independent public replay from this package alone.
Each accepted unit records checkpoints at steps 5, 10, 15, 20, 25 and 30 plus a final
adapter. Optional evaluation-resume sidecars were recorded for 36 of 40 units; that dif-
ference does not remove the matching final manifests. These are forty accepted scientific
units, not forty additional trials inferred from provider logging.

The manifests specify thirty optimizer steps. P11 therefore does not satisfy the report’s
Tier A threshold of at least one hundred steps, despite its eight-seed design. The reported
contrast, confidence interval, power estimate and null result remain unchanged. Failed
and superseded attempts are not pooled with the accepted forty units.



I.4     Recovered campaign evidence

Six unchanged original files resolved five named primary-file gaps in the retained-source
audit. Their private filesystem and export metadata are not included here. The scientific
availability result is narrower than validation of every claim: LAB-Bench records 450
correct of 1,967; AgentDojo records 88 utility passes across 97 benign episodes, with
security evaluation false and no held-out claim. The recovered training-identity note
explicitly disclaims content decontamination and does not provide the dirty source diff.
Bounded stored-payload checks cover 29 explicit file-reference records across 27 tar-
gets, 1,979 E8 archive/local bindings and 525 E10 archive-member bindings. The se-
lected 1,967 E8 grades and 97 E10 traces reproduce the stored success counts of 450
and 88. E8’s 1,259 unparsed-answer total remains diagnostic-supported rather than in-
dependently recalculated from the reduced grade rows. These are structural, hash and
tally checks, not model execution, scorer reruns or independent semantic grading.
For E14, the source-side record contains 4,428 dispositions, 4,426 accepted judged rows
and 2,271 successes. The official judged-row score is 2,271/4,426 = 51.31%; the all-
disposition diagnostic is 2,271/4,428 = 51.29%, counting two missing-verdict rows as
failures. The recovered terminal note incorrectly associates the official decimal with the
full denominator. The erratum remains explicit, the original source is unchanged, and
strict-contract score is not inferred from terminal closure.
The recovered finish-era chronology precedes the later E4 rerun and cannot verify that
later execution. The later one-hundred-trial zero-reward account remains a separate re-


                                           295
ported result with raw-trajectory limits. Original training-log payloads, the dirty source
diff and content-level decontamination remain unresolved.



I.5    Preserved C1 result and residual limits

The C1 parser-defined endpoint remains 11/64. The conservative review supports
three numerical recovery witnesses on the same denominator; these counts are separate
and neither establishes training improvement. The sixty-four reference classifications,
eleven event records, scientific hashes and disagreements remain in the included case
ledger. Historical V2 findings remain separate and unpooled.
This package excludes private provider exports, authenticated inventory and reconcilia-
tion ledgers, raw access/budget chronologies, private notebook links, original recovery-
export wrappers, personal signature data and account identifiers. Omission is a publica-
tion boundary, not evidence that a scientific result failed. Conversely, a retained source
reference or checksum is not proof that the cited bytes are publicly available or that all
claims have been independently verified. The public PDF has a distinct title and file-
name and does not replace the preserved historical manuscript or certify institutional
approval.




                                           296
Appendix J

Number-to-Artifact Provenance

This registry covers selected numeric token occurrences, not every number or scientific
claim in the thesis. tools/audit_thesis_numbers.py regenerates this table and
provenance_audit.json, which lists each registered token, its check and the cited
files’ full paths and SHA-256 hashes. A PASS means the token appears verbatim in its
chapter and satisfies its named check; it does not establish independent scientific ver-
ification. Methods: R recomputed from rows (counts, means, SDs, Wilson, Fisher, t,
TOST, ANOVA, McNemar, sign-flip, cluster bootstrap, git apply --stat); S stored
summary field; C hyperparameter matched in the runner code; T numeric transcription in
the cited source report; P selected cited-prose tokens matched to a source or another reg-
istered check; A arithmetic from printed counts, without independently checking those
inputs against an artifact; W withheld source, not checked. CONTEXT includes defini-
tions and unverified printed inputs; these tokens are not independently checked and are
excluded from passing-check counts. Σ marks the SHA-256 of a group’s sorted sha256
path lines. Hashes bind the author’s checkout; Appendix I states which files the public
package includes.
Number-to-artifact registry for Chapters 1, 4 and 6–9 and Appendices A and F. Of 967
registered token occurrences, 945 checks pass and 22 are context only, not indepen-
dently checked. The passing checks include 34 calculations from printed counts only. 0
occurrences rest on withheld sources and are not checked; 0 checks fail, 0 are unbound
and 0 lack required artifacts. Passing this registry does not verify every numerical thesis
claim or authenticate its sources.
